
## C01 Falls and fear of falling (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 by score are 6 practitioner (18, 27, 24, 02, 05, 08), 4 public (28, 03, 25, 31) and 4 both (14, 23, 19, 13), so public and carer coverage is adequate but thinner than practitioner; Reading the evidence is over-represented (02, 08, 18, 19, 24) and Care that works is absent, so consider swapping 08 or 02 for 06 (carers deciding on the floor) or 20. Avoid pairing 03 with 21, 13 with 09, or 06 with 07, and keep 23, 26, 30 and 11 in one category only.
Judge allocation reason: 29 of 31 candidates reach 36 or more with no disqualifying flag, and even after collapsing internal clusters (floor and long lie, fear of falling, head injury, handover) and setting aside the four that belong equally to C02, C06, C12 or C14, more than 14 distinct strong posts remain.
- [SEL] C01-18 total 50.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | What the cameras showed :: When falls in care homes were filmed, the commonest cause was incorrect shifting of body weight during ordinary movements such as walking, turning and sitting down, and trips were less common. || accuracy: Verify the figures (about 227 falls in 130 residents; weight shifting about 41%, trip or stumble about 21%) and note that cameras were in common areas, not bedrooms or bathrooms; the position on transfers is an inference, not a tested intervention. || sharpen: Mark the recommendation on transfers and sitting down as judgement drawn from the finding, and state the camera limitation on the chart itself.
- [SEL] C01-27 total 50.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Watch the walk :: Some patterns of walking and falling point to a specific diagnosis: early backward falls in progressive supranuclear palsy, broad shuffling steps with urinary urgency and slowed thinking in normal pressure hydrocephalus, or unsteadiness with eyes closed in sensory neuropathy. || accuracy: MDS PSP criteria specify repeated unprovoked falls within 3 years of onset; 'backward' is characteristic but is not in the criterion wording; the NPH gait description and shunt response should be worded from the Relkin or Nakajima guideline. || sharpen: Match each carousel label word for word to the cited criteria and cut to three vignettes if four cannot be read at phone size.
- [SEL] C01-24 total 49.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Stops walking when talking :: An older person who stops walking to answer a question may need their full attention to walk; a small 1997 study linked this sign with falls, and later work found it specific but insensitive. || accuracy: Verify Lundin-Olsson 1997 numbers (58 residents in Umea; 12 stopped walking; 10 of 12 fell within six months, compared with about 11 of 46) and the Beauchet 2009 sensitivity and specificity conclusions. || sharpen: End with the plain rule on weight: a positive sign means look harder, a negative sign does not reassure.
- [SEL] C01-14 total 48.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Hip protectors: a small benefit that depends on wearing them :: In care homes, hip protectors probably prevent a small number of hip fractures, mostly among people who wear them consistently; for people living in their own homes, trials have not shown a clear benefit. || accuracy: Cochrane 2014 figures (nursing care RR about 0.82, about 11 fewer hip fractures per 1000, moderate certainty; community little or no effect) must be copied exactly; the pelvic fracture signal is uncertain and should be omitted or stated as uncertain. || sharpen: Use a 100-person or 1000-person icon array that stays legible at phone size, and open with the absolute number rather than the description of the garment.
- [SEL] C01-28 total 47.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | Weeks after the fall :: A slow collection of blood between the brain and the skull (chronic subdural haematoma) can follow even a minor knock to the head and show itself weeks later as unsteadiness, headache, drowsiness or slower thinking, and it is treatable. || accuracy: Middle meningeal artery embolisation is now used as an adjunct after 2024 trials; avoid implying surgical drainage is the only treatment, and say 'often treatable' rather than 'treatable'. || sharpen: Keep the timeline card and add the one sentence families most need: the knock may have seemed too minor to mention, so mention it anyway.
- [SEL] C01-23 total 47.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Varifocals and kerbs :: Varifocal and bifocal glasses blur the ground near your feet, and a trial found that single-vision distance glasses for walking outside reduced falls in people who went out often, while outdoor falls rose in people who rarely went out. || accuracy: VISIBLE overall result did not show a clear difference; the benefit in the regular-outdoor-activity subgroup and the increase in outdoor falls in the low-outdoor-activity group must be stated as a planned subgroup result with direction and size verified. || sharpen: Lead the second slide with the overall null result and then the two subgroups, and allocate to C01 or C14, not both.
- [SEL] C01-02 total 46.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Fewer falls, or fewer people falling? :: Falls trials report two different counts, the number of falls and the number of people who fell, and an intervention can reduce the first by more than the second. || accuracy: Absolute figures must come from the Cochrane summary of findings table (rate ratio about 0.77, risk ratio about 0.85; per-1000 figures to verify); the two outcomes use different denominators (person-years and people), and 'what one person can expect' needs careful wording because neither figure predicts for an individual. || sharpen: Open with the two numbers from the same review side by side, so the reader sees the gap before the explanation.
- [SEL] C01-03 total 46.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Can you get up off the floor? :: Being unable to get up after a fall is common and harmful, and getting up from the floor is a skill that a physiotherapist can assess and help a person practise before it is needed. || accuracy: Tinetti 1993 is a 1993 New Haven cohort (about half of non-injured fallers could not get up unaided; verify); evidence that practising reduces long lies comes from small studies, so the post should say practice builds confidence and skill, not that it prevents long lies; the carousel must tell people with pain or suspected injury not to try. || sharpen: Pair the getting-up carousel with one slide from C01-21 on what to do if you can't, so the set covers both outcomes on the floor.
- [SEL] C01-25 total 46.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | Is it the right kind of class? :: Exercise that reduces falls challenges standing balance, gets harder over time and continues for months; a class done sitting down has other benefits but is not the kind shown to prevent falls. || accuracy: 'Seated exercise has not been shown to reduce falls' must match Sherrington's wording; the 3 hours a week and high balance challenge finding comes from meta-regression across trials, not a trial of dose. || sharpen: Keep the four questions but add one line on what to do if no such class exists locally (ask about a home-based programme such as Otago).
- [SEL] C01-05 total 46.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | A falls rate that improves for the wrong reason :: A ward or care home falls rate can go down because people are kept in bed or in a chair, so falls figures need to be read alongside a measure of how much people are moving. || accuracy: There is no direct evidence here that wards lower falls rates by immobilising people; present it as a limitation of the metric (judgement), and give Brown 2009's time-in-bed figure as one US hospital cohort. || sharpen: Name one specific mobility measure a ward can report next to falls per 1000 bed days, and cite a service or audit that already does so.
- [RES] C01-19 total 46.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | How afraid, and how at risk? :: How afraid someone is of falling and how likely they are to fall often don't match, and each mismatch calls for a different kind of help. || accuracy: Group names and proportions from Delbaere 2010 (vigorous, anxious, stoic, aware) and which group had which associations must be verified; one Sydney cohort of people aged 70 to 90. || sharpen: Put the grid first and give one plain sentence per cell on the help that fits, and keep the percentages only if they come straight from the paper.
- [RES] C01-08 total 45.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Checking the six-month figure :: The often-quoted figure that half of people who lie on the floor for over an hour die within six months comes from a small 1981 study and describes an association; newer cohorts still link long lies with serious harm. || accuracy: Verify the Wild, Nayak and Isaacs 1981 sample size and recruitment and that the 'half died within six months' figure refers only to those who lay more than an hour; do not imply the figure is wrong, only that it is small and associational; check which outcomes Fleming 2008 linked with long lies. || sharpen: State in the first two lines what the check found (small, old and associational, but newer cohorts still point the same way) so the post does not read as a concealed answer.
- [RES] C01-13 total 45.0 | both | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | The question about what has stopped :: Asking an older person what they have stopped doing because they worry about falling reveals fear and activity restriction that a count of falls misses. || sharpen: Add what to do with the answer (tell the GP or physiotherapist, ask about a strength and balance programme and help with the activity itself), so the question leads somewhere beyond E076.
- [   ] C01-31 total 44.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Write the fall down :: People often forget falls within months, so a short note of each fall (date, time, what they were doing, how they felt just before, whether they could get up) gives the clinician a pattern that memory loses. || accuracy: Cummings 1988 reported the share of people with a confirmed fall who did not recall falling at 12 months (about 13 to 32% depending on how they were asked), not the share of falls forgotten; the opening line must be corrected. || sharpen: Fix the opening statistic to people rather than falls, and make the diary template printable with the column for 'could you get up' kept.
- [   ] C01-06 total 43.5 | both | Care that works | teaching | risk medium | The first minute after finding someone on the floor :: Before helping someone up after a fall, a few checks decide whether to call an ambulance or coach them to get up themselves, and lifting a person bodily risks injuring both people. || accuracy: The ISTUMBLE items must be quoted exactly from the current ambulance service version; the first-aid and HSE sources are web pages that could not be opened from this environment, and safety-critical wording needs an opened document source. || sharpen: Take the checklist verbatim from one opened, current source and keep the head-knock-on-a-blood-thinner item here, so that C01-07 can be dropped or merged.
- [   ] C01-15 total 42.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | The date the falls started :: Asking when the falls began and what changed in the weeks before (a new or increased medicine, an illness, a hospital stay, new glasses) is often the quickest route to a reversible cause. || accuracy: Butt 2012 measured hip fracture, not falls, and reported a relative increase (about 40% in the first 45 days, verify) from observational data; give the absolute risk and use association wording. || sharpen: Make the onset date the single instruction and show one worked, labelled illustrative timeline, so it reads as a history-taking method rather than a repeat of E066.
- [   ] C01-17 total 42.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | What 'mechanical fall' leaves out :: Calling a fall 'mechanical' records how it happened, but even a trip has causes in the person (foot clearance, speed of the recovery step, vision, attention, strength) that an assessment should look for. || accuracy: 'No agreed clinical definition' needs a citable source; trip biomechanics come mainly from laboratory studies of healthy older adults (Pavol 2001), so avoid overstating how they apply to a frail 85-year-old. || sharpen: Give the replacement note in full (for example: tripped on threshold, did not lose consciousness, lying and standing BP done, gait observed) so the reader leaves with wording to copy.
- [   ] C01-22 total 42.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | After a long lie, write down the hours :: Time on the floor changes the checks needed after a fall: skin, temperature, fluids, kidney function and creatine kinase for muscle breakdown. || accuracy: NICE CG179 does not give a time frame for pressure damage on a hard surface; use 'can include' for the complications list and avoid a precise hour threshold unless sourced. || sharpen: Add the practical point that 'time on the floor' is often an estimate, and record the last time seen well alongside it, as the post already hints.
- [   ] C01-20 total 42.0 | practitioners | Care that works | teaching | risk low | Fear of falling has its own treatment :: Concern about falling can be measured with a short validated questionnaire and reduced by exercise and by cognitive behavioural programmes that include graded practice of avoided activities. || accuracy: Kendrick 2014 found a small to moderate effect immediately after exercise with low-certainty evidence for longer-term effects; say so, and note that CBT-based programmes are not widely available in the UK. || sharpen: Open with the treatment rather than the questionnaire, and say where a practitioner can refer or what they can start themselves.
- [   ] C01-29 total 42.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Bed rails: who they help :: Bed rails can stop someone rolling out of bed but can't stop someone who wants to get up and may climb over them, so the decision should be made for each person. || accuracy: Healey 2008 conclusions must be quoted precisely; MHRA guidance date and the restraint framing differ between UK nations (Care Inspectorate in Scotland, CQC in England). || sharpen: Add the two questions families can ask the ward or home (might they roll out, will they try to climb over), which is what the post's argument turns on.
- [   ] C01-12 total 41.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | Where is the alarm at 3 a.m.? :: A pendant alarm only helps if it is worn when falls happen, including at night and in the bathroom, and if the wearer is willing to press it. || accuracy: Fleming 2008 studied people aged 90 and over in Cambridge; the non-use figure must be stated with that population and denominator (falls when alone and unable to get up among people who had an alarm). || sharpen: Add the specific agreement to make in advance ('any time on the floor, press it') as the closing instruction and check whether automatic fall detectors need a sentence.
- [   ] C01-11 total 41.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | The walking frame comes home :: A walking aid issued in hospital needs checking in the home where it will be used, because height, doorways and the need to carry things all change how safely it works. || accuracy: The wrist-crease height rule and the share of aids set at the wrong height rest on patient guidance and small studies; avoid implying frames cause falls rather than demanding attention and both hands. || sharpen: Allocate to C02 or here, not both, and give the three home checks in the order a family would do them on the first day.
- [   ] C01-30 total 41.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Stopping the tablet, in trials :: Trials of withdrawing medicines that increase falls risk have given mixed results, partly because withdrawal often doesn't happen or doesn't last, so a medication review needs follow-up to check that the change held. || accuracy: The explanation that mixed results are 'partly because withdrawal doesn't happen or last' is inference; Campbell 1999 had 93 participants and restarting was measured after the trial; IMPROveFALL withdrawal rates and the Lee 2021 pooled result need verifying. || sharpen: Present the follow-through problem as a plausible explanation marked as such, and give the one practical step (check at a set interval that the stopped medicine has stayed stopped); allocate to C01 or C06.
- [   ] C01-09 total 40.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Stop it, or change it? :: Giving up activities to avoid falls can reduce strength and balance, so after a fall it helps to ask how an activity could be made safer before deciding to stop it. || accuracy: Deshpande 2008 is observational: say activity restriction is linked with later decline in function rather than that giving up activities reduces strength and balance. || sharpen: Merge with C01-13 or give it a distinct test, such as one named activity worked through from 'what makes it risky' to 'what can change'.
- [   ] C01-26 total 40.5 | both | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | The rush to the toilet :: Urgency incontinence is linked with falls and fractures in older people, so a falls assessment should ask about the bladder, and urgency can often be treated. || accuracy: Brown 2000 studied women only, and the link is an association; there is no trial evidence here that treating urgency reduces falls, and antimuscarinic bladder drugs can themselves raise falls risk. || sharpen: Say plainly that treating urgency is worth doing for its own sake and that trials have not shown it prevents falls; allocate to C01 or C12.
- [   ] C01-07 total 39.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | The blood thinner and the knock to the head :: An older person who takes an anticoagulant and hits their head in a fall should get medical advice the same day, because UK head injury guidance recommends a scan for people on anticoagulants even when they seem well. || accuracy: NICE NG232 wording for anticoagulated people with no other CT indication must be checked ('consider' or 'perform' a CT within 8 hours, and whether antiplatelet monotherapy is excluded); 'recommends a scan even when they seem well' may overstate the guidance. || sharpen: Merge into C01-06 or C01-28, since the single fact it carries appears in both of those posts.
- [   ] C01-01 total 38.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk medium | 'No bony injury' is half a letter :: An emergency department letter after a fall without injury is a prompt for the GP to arrange a falls assessment, because the attendance itself marks a person at higher risk of falling again. || accuracy: The World Falls Guidelines 2022 put a single non-injurious fall in an intermediate group (gait and balance check first), not straight into multifactorial assessment; 'whether or not there was an injury' must match the exact NICE NG249 criteria, and later ED-based trials after PROFET were mixed. || sharpen: Lead with the evidence on further falls after an ED attendance and give the GP two or three things to do this week, rather than a general list that echoes E067 and E079.
- [   ] C01-21 total 37.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | If you can't get up :: If you fall and can't get up, keeping warm, calling or signalling for help, and changing position every so often reduce the harm of a long wait on the floor. || accuracy: The NHS advice wording (for example, changing position about every 30 minutes) comes from web pages that could not be opened here; verify from an opened document before quoting. || sharpen: Merge with C01-03 as the 'if you can't get up' slide, or give it a stronger opening that names the preparation to do today (phone in a pocket, a throw at floor level).
- [   ] C01-10 total 37.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | When the ambulance leaves you at home :: Many older people who call an ambulance after a fall are checked and left at home, and they should ask for a referral to a falls service so that the reason for the fall is looked into. || accuracy: SAFER 2 did not show a clear difference in its primary outcomes and referral uptake was low, and non-conveyance rates vary widely between services; the post must not imply that ambulance referral pathways are proven to reduce falls. || sharpen: Keep one handover post (this or C01-01), and give the reader the exact words to ask the crew and a named route (GP or local falls service) if they forget.
- [   ] C01-04 total 33.0 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | The first month home :: The weeks after discharge from hospital are a period of high falls risk, and a few steps in that month (a home hazard check, restarting strength and balance work, reviewing medicines changed in hospital) have evidence behind them. || accuracy: Naseri 2018 found benefit mainly for home hazard modification in people with a falls history after discharge, and did not show clear benefit for post-discharge exercise or medication review; the claim that all three steps 'have evidence behind them' in that window overclaims. || sharpen: Narrow it to the one post-discharge intervention with trial support (occupational therapy home hazard work for people who have fallen, or the Safe Recovery education trial) and say plainly what is less certain.
- [   ] C01-16 total 31.5 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Signposted, but out of reach :: Community strength and balance classes reach fewer of the people who are poorest, most isolated or housebound, and home-based programmes exist that reduce falls. || EXCLUDED: duplicates earlier post E083 || accuracy: Evidence that community classes reach fewer of the poorest people may be thin: Nyman and Victor 2012 addresses participation factors rather than socioeconomic reach; Liu-Ambrose 2019 effect size to verify. || sharpen: Find a source that measures who takes up classes by deprivation or housebound status; without it the post repeats E083's call for home-based programmes.

## C02 Mobility, deconditioning and rehabilitation (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 by score are practitioner-heavy: 7 practitioners (C02-21, 18, 24, 16, 08, 15, 27), 4 both (C02-14, 05, 23, 26) and 3 public (C02-01, 10, 22). To give older people and carers substantial coverage, swap C02-15 for C02-19 (public; shares the Fiatarone trial, so take only one of 15, 19 and 29) and consider C02-03 or C02-17 (public) in place of C02-27. Thin the clusters: hospital mobility (C02-16, 17, 11, 28, 04: take at most two), post-discharge function (C02-10, 12, 13, 07: at most two, and C02-10 and C02-13 share the Loyd figure), walking speed (C02-05 and 06 make different points and can both stand). The top set mixes good care and teaching (C02-14, 16, 18, 21, 22) with evidence-based criticism (C02-05, 23, 24), which fits the category's 'practical and hopeful as well as critical' brief.
Judge allocation reason: Twenty-eight of 29 candidates reach 36 with no disqualifying flag and 18 score 42 or more across distinct angles (sarcopenia diagnosis, rehabilitation in dementia, walking aids, sit-to-stand, crossing times, in-hospital exercise, prehabilitation, life-space, Parkinson's medicine timing), so the category supports the maximum even after clustered near-duplicates are thinned.
- [SEL] C02-18 total 48.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Sarcopenia is found by weakness first :: Under the 2019 European definition, low muscle strength measured by grip or chair-rise time identifies probable sarcopenia and is enough in clinical practice to look for causes and start treatment, with muscle quantity measured to confirm. || accuracy: EWGSOP2 cut-offs are European and differ from the Asian (AWGS 2019) criteria; the 2024 GLIS definition is conceptual and gives no operational cut-offs, so mixing it into the pathway may confuse; the 'no licensed medicine' claim needs a BNF or MHRA check. || sharpen: Add one practical starting point for resistance exercise and one line on what to tell the patient, so the post goes beyond the diagnostic pathway to action.
- [SEL] C02-21 total 48.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | No memory of the session, but the movement improves :: People with Alzheimer's disease often keep the ability to learn movements through practice even when they do not recall being taught, so rehabilitation works best through repeated practice and demonstration rather than verbal instruction. || accuracy: Most implicit motor-learning evidence comes from laboratory tasks in mild to moderate Alzheimer's disease; transfer to real rehabilitation tasks and to other dementias is less certain; the exclusion-from-rehabilitation claim needs a source; Hauer 2012 details to verify. || sharpen: Show one concrete demonstration-based technique (the same sit-to-stand sequence with one cue each time) and state that the evidence is mainly in Alzheimer's disease.
- [SEL] C02-01 total 47.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | A borrowed stick is set to someone else's height :: A walking stick helps only when it is the right height, held in the right hand and kept in good repair, and studies of older stick users find these basics are often wrong. || accuracy: Liu et al. is a small US senior-living sample (93 stick users; Arch Gerontol Geriatr 2011;52:299-303), so 'often wrong' must be presented as that study's finding; the opposite-hand rule applies to one-sided leg problems, and some people need a frame rather than a stick; the ferrule claim still needs an opened source. || sharpen: Lead with the wrist-crease check as a ten-second test the reader can do now, and add one line on when a stick is the wrong aid and a physiotherapist should assess.
- [SEL] C02-14 total 47.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Help that removes the practice :: When help arrives for dependence and independent effort goes unnoticed, people do less for themselves; observational research in long-term care described this pattern, and a staff training programme changed it. || accuracy: The Baltes studies date from the 1980s and early 1990s in German institutions with small samples and short follow-up; present them as a described pattern and a tested staff programme, not as current UK care home practice; Resnick function-focused care citations are unverified. || sharpen: Date the research in the caption and state the time cost on the rota explicitly, so the post reads as a classic, still-useful finding with a practical implication.
- [SEL] C02-24 total 46.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | What prehabilitation trials in frail older people found :: Exercise and nutrition before surgery (prehabilitation) is plausible and widely promoted, but a randomised trial in frail older people having bowel cancer surgery found no fewer complications, and overall evidence certainty is low. || accuracy: Carli 2020 compared prehabilitation with the same programme started after surgery, not with no programme; 110 patients may be underpowered for complications; check the umbrella review's certainty ratings exactly, especially for functional recovery; confirm the PREHAB (McIsaac 2022) result before citing it. Category fit is partly C07. || sharpen: State plainly that the comparison was prehabilitation versus the same programme after surgery, and end with the counselling sentence practitioners can use.
- [SEL] C02-05 total 46.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Crossings timed for a speed most older people do not walk :: UK pedestrian crossing timings have assumed a walking speed of 1.2 metres per second, and a national survey found that most people over 65 in England walked more slowly than that. || accuracy: The 1.2 m/s figure is background from the paper's introduction and needs a current Department for Transport source; HSE walking speed was timed over a short course (about 8 feet) from a standing start, which may understate speed on a crossing; data are from 2005; many UK crossings with detectors (Puffin) extend clearance time, so 'timed for' needs qualification. || sharpen: Put the test method and survey year on the chart and add one line on crossings with detectors, so the striking comparison stays accurate.
- [SEL] C02-10 total 46.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | The first hour home :: Before discharge, ask whether the person can do the specific tasks of their first hour at home, because many older people leave hospital less able to manage daily activities than before they were ill. || accuracy: Loyd 2020's pooled 30% combines studies using pre-admission and admission baselines; the first-hour task list is a judgement, not a tested checklist; the NICE NG27 wording on home assessment is unverified. || sharpen: Cut the list to five tasks that read at phone size and add who to ask (the ward therapist) and what to request if a task has not been tried.
- [SEL] C02-22 total 46.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Help someone stand without pulling :: When helping someone up from a chair, pulling on their arms or lifting under the armpits risks injury to them and to you; setting up the movement (shuffle forward, feet back, lean forward, push on the armrests) lets them do more of the work. || accuracy: The sequence assumes the person can bear weight and follow instructions; people who cannot need an assessment and equipment; the 'drag lift is unsafe' claim needs an openable moving and handling source; some guidance allows a palm-to-palm hand hold, so avoid an absolute ban on any hand contact. || sharpen: Add a final panel on when to stop and ask for an assessment or equipment (cannot take weight, dizzy, knees buckle), sourced to moving and handling guidance.
- [SEL] C02-23 total 46.0 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | How far beyond the front door :: Life-space assessment measures how far and how often a person moves beyond their home, capturing outcomes many older people value, such as the shop, the bus or a friend's house, that daily-living scores miss. || accuracy: Brown 2016 was 100 US veterans (97% male) at one centre; check that life-space was a pre-specified main outcome, otherwise 'the choice of measure decided' reads as outcome selection; the life-space associations with death and care home admission need primary sources. Shares the Brown 2016 trial with C02-17. || sharpen: Lead with the concentric-zone diagram and give readers a plain question they can use ('How far from your front door did you get last month?').
- [SEL] C02-26 total 46.0 | both | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | Parkinson's medicines on time keep people moving :: In hospital, people with Parkinson's who receive their medicines late, missed or wrong are more likely to leave with worse movement, and letting people take their own medicines where safe is one protection. || accuracy: Gerlach 2012 is self-reported survey data and Gerlach 2013 is a small cohort (46 admissions), so 'none who self-managed deteriorated' rests on small numbers; confirm NICE NG71 wording and recommendation number; overlaps medicine-timing territory in C06. || sharpen: Lead with the concrete admission request (self-administration or doses at the person's usual times) and describe the survey findings as self-reported.
- [RES] C02-16 total 45.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Two short exercise sessions a day, in hospital, in the late 80s :: In a randomised trial of acute medical patients aged 75 and over, individualised twice-daily resistance, balance and walking exercise improved function by discharge, while function fell with usual care. || accuracy: Keep between-group differences (SPPB 2.2, Barthel 6.9) separate from within-group changes (usual care minus 5.0, exercise plus 1.9); single-centre, single-blind, outcomes at discharge only; participants had to be able to walk and cooperate; confirm session length and staffing in full text. || sharpen: Lead with the diverging bars (usual care down, exercise up) and describe what the two daily sessions involved in one plain sentence.
- [RES] C02-08 total 44.0 | practitioners | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | 'Is she safe for discharge?' :: Asking a therapist whether someone is 'safe for discharge' asks for a certainty that no assessment gives; a better record states what the person can do, what could go wrong, what reduces the risk and what the person wants. || accuracy: Section 1(4) of the Mental Capacity Act applies only when the person has capacity for that decision, so the post must say what happens when capacity is lacking (best interests, least restrictive option); the risk-aversion qualitative claim still needs a source; the 2007 DH guidance may be superseded. Category fit is partly C09 or C15. || sharpen: Lead with the four-line note structure as the visual and add one sentence on best-interests decisions when the person lacks capacity for this decision.
- [RES] C02-15 total 44.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Why small strength changes affect the weakest most :: The link between leg strength and walking speed is non-linear: in weaker people small differences in strength go with larger differences in walking, so both short periods of disuse and modest strength training affect the frailest most. || accuracy: Buchner 1996 is cross-sectional, so the claim that small strength changes produce larger walking changes in weak people is inferred from a curve, not observed within people; Kortebein studied healthy older adults; some trials in frail people after hospital showed limited functional gains, so 'large gains' needs qualifying; the 113% is a relative change from a very low baseline. || sharpen: Keep the curve as a labelled illustrative shape and add one line on trials where frail people gained less, so the teaching point does not overpromise.
- [   ] C02-27 total 44.0 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Time the pain relief to the physiotherapy :: Oral pain relief takes time to work, so giving it well before a planned therapy session, rather than when the therapist arrives, lets the person move more with less pain. || accuracy: Peak plasma concentration is not the same as peak analgesic effect; regular paracetamol makes timing less relevant, so the point applies mainly to as-needed doses; opioid sedation and dizziness before mobilisation need mention; NICE CG124 wording to verify. || sharpen: Focus on as-needed opioid doses before therapy, add the sedation and dizziness caution, and use sourced onset times on the timeline.
- [   ] C02-19 total 43.5 | public | Who gets left out | good_care | risk low | Strength training works in care homes, in the 80s and 90s :: In a randomised trial in frail care home residents aged 72 to 98, ten weeks of high-intensity progressive resistance training more than doubled muscle strength and improved walking speed and stair climbing. || accuracy: The 113% strength gain is a relative change from a low baseline, achieved with supervised high-intensity machine training in residents able to take part; data are from 1994; the Cochrane review found smaller and less certain effects; the UK physiotherapy-access claim has no source yet. Shares the Fiatarone trial with C02-15 and C02-29. || sharpen: State that the training was supervised and high intensity on resistance machines, and put the more modest Cochrane picture in the caption rather than only a footnote.
- [   ] C02-06 total 43.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Gait speed: a population predictor used on one person :: Usual walking speed predicts survival across large groups of older people, but for an individual it is most useful as a prompt to look for treatable causes of slowness and as a measure to repeat, not as a personal prognosis. || accuracy: The hazard ratio of 0.88 per 0.1 m/s is a population association; the 19% to 87% survival range spans the extremes of gait speed and must not be read as an individual prediction; the list of causes of slow gait still needs a source; cohorts were 1986 to 2000 and about 80% white. || sharpen: Open with the Studenski survival range at age 75 as the visual hook, then turn at once to what to do for one slow patient: measure, repeat, look for causes.
- [   ] C02-11 total 42.5 | both | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Every tube is a reason to stay in bed :: Cannulas, drips, urinary catheters, oxygen tubing and monitoring leads make getting out of bed harder, so asking each day whether each one is still needed helps a person keep moving. || accuracy: Direct evidence that each attachment reduces mobility in older inpatients is limited (the catheter 'one-point restraint' source is an editorial); the idle-cannula prevalence needs a checked source; oxygen and monitoring decisions are clinical, so families can ask but staff decide. || sharpen: Build it around one daily question ('Which of these still needs to be here today?') and source the idle-cannula figure before using it.
- [   ] C02-12 total 42.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Watch the first month after discharge :: Some older people who leave hospital less able recover fully, but whether recovery has started by one month is a strong signal of the year ahead, so the first month deserves a planned review. || accuracy: Boyd's cohort is US data from 1993 to 1998; the one-month finding is prognostic and does not show that a review at one month improves outcomes; check that Hardy and Gill's 81% refers to recovery from an episode of disability and that recovery was often not sustained. || sharpen: Lead with the recovery range, mark the one-month review as judgement, and give families the specific request (a review of walking and daily activities around four weeks after discharge).
- [   ] C02-03 total 41.5 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Strength and balance training without a class :: Strength and balance exercises built into daily routines at home, taught by a therapist, reduced falls and improved strength and function in a randomised trial, which makes them an option for people unable to get to a class. || accuracy: LiFE was a three-arm trial (LiFE, structured home exercise, gentle-exercise control); the structured arm was also home-based and its falls reduction was not statistically significant, so the trial did not compare home with class-based training; results apply to people with recent falls and without moderate to severe cognitive impairment. Falls outcome also overlaps category C01. || sharpen: Name the three arms honestly and reframe the angle around training built into daily routines rather than 'without a class', with one concrete LiFE move pictured.
- [   ] C02-29 total 40.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Protein drinks work best alongside strength exercise :: Nutritional supplements taken without exercise did not improve strength in a care home trial, and guidelines recommend resistance exercise as the main treatment for age-related muscle loss, with protein as a support. || accuracy: In Fiatarone 1994 the supplemented residents reduced their usual food intake, so total intake rose little; the trial does not show supplements are ineffective in general, and its population (frail nursing home residents) differs from community families; protein target figures and the Liao meta-analysis need checking. || sharpen: Mention the substitution effect in the Fiatarone trial and base the advice on the guideline ranking (exercise first, protein as support) rather than on that single trial.
- [   ] C02-02 total 39.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | 'Take it easy' needs an end date :: Episodes of staying in bed or cutting down usual activities because of illness at home, not only hospital stays, are linked with later loss of independence, so rest after an illness should have a planned end. || accuracy: Restricted activity may mark more serious illness rather than cause decline (confounding by severity), so the opening instruction goes beyond an associational finding and must be marked as judgement; Gill 2004 and 2010 details are unverified. || sharpen: Recast the opening as clearly marked judgement and put the null result in the high-risk group on the visual, so the post reads as careful evidence reading rather than an instruction drawn from an association.
- [   ] C02-07 total 39.5 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | Still at home at 91 days :: One of England's official measures of reablement and rehabilitation after hospital records whether the older person is still at home 91 days later, which says nothing about whether they can walk to the shop, wash, or do what they wanted to regain. || accuracy: ASCOF measure numbering and wording changed in the 2023 to 2024 refresh and must be checked; ASCOF also reports an outcome-of-short-term-services measure, which is not a function measure but should be acknowledged; the measure covers England only and the handbook is an official web document that may not be openable here. || sharpen: Name one concrete alternative (a function score or goal attainment at the end of reablement) so the criticism ends with something services could adopt.
- [   ] C02-09 total 39.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | When nobody else knows how far she walked :: For a person who lives alone, their mobility before this illness may be known only to them, so ask them directly and precisely early in the admission, and record it in terms rehabilitation can use. || accuracy: Sinoff and Ore 1997 compared self-reported with observed Barthel performance in the old-old and reported discrepancies (self-report tending to overstate ability, particularly with cognitive impairment), which cuts against the claim that self-report is reasonably consistent; the documentation-gap claim has no source yet. || sharpen: Drop the self-report validity claim and keep it as a clerking teaching card built around the six questions and the reason 'independently mobile' gives rehabilitation no target.
- [   ] C02-17 total 39.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | Ask when she last walked :: Older people in hospital spend most of their time lying in bed, even when they can walk, so a daily question about walking helps families and staff notice it and plan for change. || accuracy: Brown 2009 was 45 older male veterans in one US hospital; generalising the 83% figure to UK wards needs a second source (for example Pedersen 2013, Danish medical inpatients); the '78% willing and able' figure should be checked against the abstract. || sharpen: Add a second, non-veteran source for time in bed and phrase the question as 'What is the walking plan for today?' so it prompts a plan rather than a demand.
- [   ] C02-13 total 39.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | The harm without an incident form :: About 3 in 10 older people leave acute hospital less able to do basic daily activities than before they were ill, yet this loss is seldom reported as a harm in the way falls and pressure ulcers are. || accuracy: The claim that functional loss is not a routine UK harm indicator is an absence claim that needs a checkable source or softening; Loyd's pooled figure mixes baselines; the 'little change over time' finding needs checking in full text. Shares the Loyd statistic with C02-10. || sharpen: Pair the 3 in 10 figure with one named, simple measure a ward could start recording, and soften the national-indicator claim to what can be verified.
- [   ] C02-20 total 38.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | Walking, and strength on two days a week :: UK physical activity guidelines for older adults ask for activities that build strength and balance on at least two days a week, alongside aerobic activity such as walking, and strength needs effort that increases over time. || accuracy: The proportion meeting the strength guideline is unverified; the post must not imply walking has little value; the CMO documents are gov.uk pages that may not open here, so a published summary of the guideline is needed. || sharpen: Open with one concrete strength activity from the CMO infographic and a sourced figure for how few older adults meet the strength guideline.
- [   ] C02-28 total 37.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Up, dressed and moving: reading the evidence :: End PJ paralysis rests on good evidence that low mobility in hospital is linked with decline, but evaluations of the campaign itself are observational, and one project reached far fewer patients dressed than it aimed for. || accuracy: One small Canadian quality improvement project's 14% cannot stand for the campaign; the Alberta time-series study reported falls in length of stay and inpatient falls, which must be stated as plainly; the 'no randomised trial' claim needs a documented search; 70-day challenge dates unverified. || sharpen: Report the favourable Alberta findings as plainly as the shortfall, and give practitioners one simple measure (the proportion of patients up and dressed by midday).
- [   ] C02-25 total 36.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Ask what you can practise together :: More therapy time is linked with better mobility and shorter hospital stays, and families who wish to can sometimes add safe practice between sessions if the physiotherapist shows them what is safe. || accuracy: Extra therapy delivered by therapists does not show that family-led practice gives the same benefit; the family-exercise trial (FAME) is in stroke; there is no evidence cited on the safety of family-assisted walking in older inpatients. || sharpen: Centre the post on the safety question ('What should I not do, and when do I call a nurse?') or merge it with C02-17 as a single family-facing hospital mobility post.
- [   ] C02-04 total 32.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | When the ambulance comes, ask about the frame :: A person who walks with an aid at home needs an aid that fits them in hospital, so families can ask whether the frame or stick can travel with them, or bring it on the first visit, with shoes and glasses. || accuracy: No evidence that bringing the person's own frame changes outcomes; ward teams often assess and supply a different aid, and personal aids can go missing; ambulance policy on carrying frames is unverified and varies between services. || sharpen: Fold the walking aid into a broader 'what to bring to hospital' item or merge with C02-17, and drop the ambulance claim unless a service policy can be sourced.

## C03 Hip fracture (judge recommends 13; mechanical allocation 9)
Judge balance note: The top 13 split 5 public (C03-12, 22, 02, 09, 18), 3 both (06, 07, 14) and 5 practitioner (17, 21, 19, 04, 03), which is balanced. Most are teaching or explanation rather than complaint. Two series are missing from the top 13: Care that works (best candidates C03-24, 26) and Who gets left out (C03-10, 01). Swapping C03-03 or C03-18 for C03-24 or C03-10 would cover them at little cost in score. Also watch format: 17, 18, 20, 25 and 28 all use trial-result icon arrays, so no more than two of them should run. Note: the weights as given sum to a maximum of 55, not 50.
Judge allocation reason: About 15 distinct candidates score 44 or more on checkable trial or guideline evidence with no disqualifying flag; 13 takes the strongest without two posts on one trial or cohort (Trondheim, Magaziner) or a run of near-identical trial-result icon arrays, and the earlier set already had 20 hip fracture posts.
- [SEL] C03-12 total 49.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Deciding about a care home in week two :: Recovery after hip fracture continues for many months, with leg function taking close to a year to reach its best in a large study, so a decision about a permanent move to a care home made in the first weeks may rest on the person at their weakest. || accuracy: Magaziner's cohort was community-dwelling people in Baltimore in 1990 to 1991; times to maximum recovery are group averages and many people never regain earlier function; the UK discharge-guidance claim is unverified and official pages could not be opened. || sharpen: Give the family the words to use ('Can we decide after rehabilitation, or after a supported trial at home?') and verify the discharge-guidance line or drop it.
- [SEL] C03-06 total 49.0 | both | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | Block the pain before the moving starts :: A nerve block (an injection of local anaesthetic near the nerves that supply the hip) reduces pain on movement within 30 minutes; because each transfer on the way to the ward moves the fracture, giving it early in the emergency department spares the person pain at every later move. || accuracy: Check NICE CG124 wording (the original text says 'consider adding nerve blocks' rather than 'offer'); a single-shot block lasts hours, so 'spares pain at every later move' overstates; in UK practice the block often follows X-ray confirmation, so the drawn sequence must match that. || sharpen: Say how long a single block usually lasts so the timing argument stays accurate, and give families the exact question: has a nerve block been offered?
- [SEL] C03-17 total 48.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Fast enough, and faster :: Observational studies link delays beyond about a day or two with more deaths after hip fracture, but the HIP ATTACK trial found that surgery within about 6 hours did not reduce deaths or major complications compared with standard care at a median of about 24 hours. || accuracy: HIP ATTACK secondary outcomes (less delirium and pain, earlier mobilisation) favoured accelerated surgery, so the post must say it did not reduce deaths or major complications, not that speed has no benefit; the observational delay figures are associations. || sharpen: Add one line on the secondary outcomes so the post can't be read as 'speed doesn't matter', and close on the concrete target: surgery on the day of or the day after admission.
- [SEL] C03-22 total 48.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | When an operation may not be the kindest choice :: For most people who break a hip, including many with dementia, surgery gives the best pain relief; for a small group of very frail care home residents near the end of life, a Dutch study found that choosing not to operate, after shared decision-making and with good palliative care, gave similar quality of life in the following weeks, although most of those people died within 30 days. || accuracy: FRAIL-HIP was not randomised (people chose); the 83% 30-day mortality without surgery reflects who chose it near the end of life and must not be read as the effect of not operating; quality of life was proxy-rated; the 'quality of dying' figure needs checking. || sharpen: Show what care without an operation involves day by day, and say near the top that this was a small non-randomised group who chose.
- [SEL] C03-19 total 47.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Partial weight-bearing is an instruction few older people can follow :: In a gait study, none of 16 patients aged 75 or over could keep to a partial weight-bearing limit after hip fracture surgery, and in a large US dataset restricted weight-bearing was linked with more complications, so surgery for older people should aim to allow full weight-bearing from the start. || accuracy: The gait study had only 16 older patients; the NSQIP association is likely confounded by fracture pattern and fixation (restriction is prescribed for harder cases), so 'linked with' only; the BOAST or NICE full weight-bearing wording is unverified. || sharpen: Add what restriction means for the patient (not walking, or walking in breach) and one question a physiotherapist can put to the surgeon.
- [SEL] C03-21 total 47.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | What should and shouldn't hold up hip fracture surgery :: Guidance lists a short set of problems worth correcting before hip fracture surgery (such as marked anaemia, anticoagulant effect, dehydration, electrolyte problems, uncontrolled diabetes or heart failure, a correctable arrhythmia or a chest infection), while antiplatelet drugs and waiting for an echocardiogram are not usually reasons to delay. || accuracy: NICE CG124 frames the list as comorbidities to identify and treat immediately so that surgery is not delayed, not as reasons that justify delay; the antiplatelet and echocardiography advice must be checked in the Griffiths 2021 full text; DOAC timing varies with drug and kidney function. || sharpen: Keep NICE's framing (treat these immediately so they don't delay surgery) and add who to ask about DOAC timing.
- [SEL] C03-02 total 47.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | A normal X-ray, and still can't stand :: When an older person has hip pain and can't bear weight after a fall, a normal X-ray does not rule out a hip fracture; NICE recommends MRI, or CT if MRI is not available within 24 hours. || accuracy: Check whether the Haj-Mirzaian 39% is the prevalence of occult fracture on advanced imaging or of fractures needing surgery, and state that it applies only to people selected for MRI or CT; NICE's trigger is fracture still suspected after normal X-rays, not inability to bear weight as such. || sharpen: Name the trigger as NICE does (fracture still suspected after normal X-rays) and add the words a care worker or relative can use when the person is sent back still unable to stand.
- [SEL] C03-09 total 47.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Who signs the form? :: In the UK, being next of kin does not give you legal power to consent to an operation for an adult; if the person can't decide, the doctors decide in their best interests (or, in Scotland, under the Adults with Incapacity Act) unless you hold the relevant power of attorney or guardianship, and they must still ask you about the person's wishes. || accuracy: The Mental Capacity Act (Northern Ireland) 2016 is only partly in force, so avoid stating the NI process; in Scotland a welfare attorney or guardian with medical treatment powers can consent, not only be consulted; statutes, codes and GMC guidance need opened copies, not web pages. || sharpen: Keep the nation-by-nation detail in the source reply and build the caption around one question: what would she want, and who is making this decision?
- [SEL] C03-07 total 46.0 | both | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Watch the first drink after surgery :: In one prospective study about a third of older people had swallowing difficulty after hip fracture surgery, so the first drinks and meals are worth watching, sitting upright, for coughing, a wet voice or very slow eating, with a swallow assessment requested if they appear. || accuracy: The 34% comes from one Australian orthogeriatric unit with formal swallowing assessment; the link with pneumonia and death is observational and must be 'linked with'; the list of warning signs still needs an RCSLT or NICE source. || sharpen: Say who can do a bedside swallow screen on an orthopaedic ward, and make the family action concrete: sit the person upright and watch the first cup.
- [RES] C03-14 total 46.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Fewer per person, more in total :: In most of 19 countries and regions studied, the age- and sex-standardised rate of hip fracture fell between 2005 and 2018, but because more people are living into their 80s and 90s the yearly number of hip fractures is projected to nearly double by 2050. || accuracy: Check that 'decreased in most' is the authors' wording and whether a UK series is included; the near doubling by 2050 is a projection that assumes current rates and population forecasts. || sharpen: Use the UK line from Sing 2023 if it exists and end with the reading habit: ask whether a figure is a rate or a count.
- [RES] C03-04 total 45.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Out of bed once, upright for how long? :: Audits record whether a person got out of bed by the day after surgery, but in a Norwegian trial people spent under an hour a day upright on the fourth day after hip fracture surgery, and geriatric ward care increased that time. || accuracy: Upright minutes (57.6 versus 45.1) are day-4 means from one Norwegian trial of a geriatric ward model; confirm the exact wording of the mobilisation indicator before contrasting with it. || sharpen: Pair the day-4 upright minutes with a practical ward measure a team could record (times out of bed per day) and keep C03-23 from repeating the same trial.
- [RES] C03-18 total 45.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Spinal or general: what the big trial found :: In a trial of 1,600 older adults having hip fracture surgery, spinal and general anaesthesia gave similar results for walking and survival at 60 days, so the choice can follow the person's preferences and the anaesthetist's assessment of their particular risks. || accuracy: The primary outcome is a composite (death or inability to walk about 3 metres at 60 days); about 16% crossed from spinal to general; the population was previously ambulatory patients in the US and Canada; families inform the choice rather than make it. || sharpen: Say what the trial did not settle (people with advanced dementia, longer-term outcomes) and give families one question to ask the anaesthetist.
- [   ] C03-03 total 44.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | teaching | risk medium | A score for 100 people, used on one :: The Nottingham Hip Fracture Score estimates what share of people with a given score die within 30 days; it helps plan care and compare hospitals, but it can't say which person will die, so it should inform conversations and the intensity of care rather than decide whether someone is offered surgery. || accuracy: Predicted mortality by band must come from the published equation or validation tables, and the constants were recalibrated as mortality fell (Moppett 2012 and later); the premise that the score is used to decide against surgery needs a source or softer wording. || sharpen: Show two icon arrays (a middle and a high band) so readers see that most people in high bands survive 30 days, and cite how the score is actually used in decisions.
- [   ] C03-10 total 44.0 | public | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Who is at home waiting? :: Many older people are unpaid carers themselves, so a sudden admission with a hip fracture can leave a partner with dementia or a disabled adult child at home without help; asking 'does anyone depend on you?' at admission lets staff arrange cover the same day. || accuracy: There are no data on how many hip fracture patients are carers, as the candidate notes; census figures need an opened ONS or NRS document; the Carers (Scotland) Act emergency planning provision needs checking; the problem applies to any emergency admission, so hip fracture is the example, not the claim. || sharpen: Name a same-day route that exists (ward social work, a local carer centre's emergency line) so the request leads somewhere.
- [   ] C03-28 total 44.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Sixteen weeks of home physiotherapy, and the walk to the shops :: In the US CAP trial, people still not walking in daily life months after a hip fracture received 16 weeks of home physiotherapy; only about 1 in 5 in either group could then walk 300 metres in 6 minutes, and the full training programme did not clearly beat an active control. || accuracy: The difference (22.9% versus 17.8%) was not statistically significant, so the wording must be 'did not show a clear difference'; participants were a select group still not walking in daily life at enrolment. || sharpen: Explain an active control in one sentence and close on the realistic point that many people need more than 16 weeks.
- [   ] C03-20 total 43.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Transfusion after hip fracture surgery: symptoms first :: In the FOCUS trial of 2,016 people after hip fracture surgery who had heart disease or its risk factors, transfusing to keep haemoglobin above 100 g/L did not improve survival or walking at 60 days compared with transfusing for symptoms or a haemoglobin below 80 g/L. || accuracy: UK guidance (NICE NG24) uses 70 g/L, or 80 g/L with acute coronary syndrome, not the FOCUS 80 g/L threshold; the trial is from 2011 and most UK practice has already moved; the result must not be extended to people with acute myocardial infarction. || sharpen: Align the threshold with UK guidance and say what a symptom trigger means at the bedside, so the post adds something to what most readers already do.
- [   ] C03-24 total 42.0 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | A person whose job is to help people eat :: In a Cardiff trial of women with hip fracture, adding dietetic assistants (staff whose job was to help patients choose, get and eat food) increased daily energy intake, and fewer women died on the trauma ward and by four months. || accuracy: Single-centre, open trial in women only from the early 2000s; a mortality difference this large has not been a consistent finding in later nutrition trials and the Cochrane review rates certainty low, so the limitation must sit beside the mortality result. || sharpen: Put the limitation in the same sentence as the mortality result and show the energy difference as an everyday food equivalent.
- [   ] C03-25 total 41.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Cemented half hip replacement: the trial that measured quality of life :: In the UK WHiTE 5 trial of 1,225 people aged 60 and over, cemented hemiarthroplasty gave modestly better quality of life at 4 months and fewer fractures around the implant than uncemented hemiarthroplasty, with no clear difference in deaths at 12 months. || accuracy: EQ-5D data at 4 months were available for only 71.6%, largely because of deaths and missing responses; the post should say how the trial addressed the intraoperative cement reaction rather than leaving it out. || sharpen: Frame it for families and GPs as why most half hip replacements now use cement, or leave it to orthopaedic accounts.
- [   ] C03-26 total 41.0 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Rehabilitation after hip fracture can work with dementia :: In a Finnish trial, people with mild or moderate dementia who received intensive geriatric rehabilitation after hip fracture were more often living independently three months later than those who did not, so dementia alone should not exclude someone from rehabilitation, even though the overall evidence is limited. || accuracy: A subgroup of a 1990s Finnish trial with very small moderate-dementia groups; 'living independently' is place of residence at 3 months; the Cochrane review rates the evidence low or very low. || sharpen: Lead with the mild-dementia result, print group sizes, and add two adaptations (repetition, familiar staff) a family can ask for.
- [   ] C03-27 total 41.0 | both | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | After a fall, knee pain can come from the hip :: Pain from the hip joint can be felt in the thigh or knee, so an older person with new knee pain after a fall, especially one who can't bear weight, needs the hip examined and, if in doubt, imaged. || accuracy: Evidence on how often hip pain is felt in the knee is thin (one descriptive injection study and case reports); the anatomy needs a textbook source; no frequency should be given. || sharpen: Combine with C03-02 as a two-part 'missed hip fracture' carousel, or keep to a sourced anatomical diagram and one examination habit.
- [   ] C03-05 total 40.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | criticism | risk medium | Two clocks start at admission :: Fasting guidance allows solid food until 6 hours and clear fluids until 2 hours before anaesthesia, yet people waiting for hip fracture surgery are often kept without food or drink much longer, and again each time the operation is moved. || accuracy: The practice finding rests on one small retrospective Sydney cohort (92 people); 'no one was offered food or drink' must not be generalised beyond that hospital; the ESA limits are for elective anaesthesia and the Sip til Send consensus source is still unlocated. || sharpen: Find a UK or multi-centre fasting audit to sit beside the Sydney cohort and lead with the one practical change: restart fluids when an operation is postponed.
- [   ] C03-11 total 40.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | An opioid at discharge needs an end date :: Opioids started for hip fracture pain should leave hospital with a short supply, a written plan to reduce and stop, a laxative and a named person to review them, because most people's pain lessens as they recover while the harms of continued use (constipation, drowsiness, falls) remain. || accuracy: Levy 2021 has not been read; persistent opioid use after hip fracture (Danish cohort) is unverified; the link between opioids and falls is observational. || sharpen: Read Levy 2021 and quote its discharge recommendation, and add the one line the discharge letter should tell the GP.
- [   ] C03-13 total 39.5 | practitioners | Who gets left out | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | The patients hip fracture trials leave out :: People with dementia make up a large share of people with hip fracture, yet many hip fracture trials exclude them, so results may apply poorly to many of the patients clinicians treat; UK law allows research inclusion with consultee advice. || accuracy: The 22% and 36% are shares of all exclusions, not the share of patients excluded, and readers will misread them; the 'over 40% have dementia' figure is Cochrane background and needs a primary source; consultee rules differ between England and Wales and Scotland. || sharpen: Replace 'share of exclusions' with the proportion of trials that excluded people with dementia, which readers grasp at once, and consider moving it to C20.
- [   ] C03-01 total 39.0 | both | Who gets left out | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | The same fracture, a poorer area :: In England, people from the most deprived areas were more likely to die within 30 days and within a year of a hip fracture than people from the least deprived areas, so an equal pathway can still give unequal results. || accuracy: Deprivation is measured by area (deprivation fifth), not personal income; adjusted odds ratios of about 1.15 to 1.19 are modest and the absolute gap may be only a few percentage points; the data are 2004 to 2011 and cover England and Wales; the link does not show that the pathway itself produces the gap. || sharpen: Get the absolute 30-day and 1-year figures by deprivation fifth from the full text and lead with them as 'N in 100', dropping the invented scenario.
- [   ] C03-08 total 38.0 | both | Who gets left out | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Back to the care home, and then? :: People living in care homes are often left out of hip fracture care trials; the one trial of outreach rehabilitation in care homes found better mobility at 4 weeks that did not last after the programme ended, which points to rehabilitation that care home staff can continue day to day. || accuracy: 'The one trial' of outreach rehabilitation in care homes is probably an overclaim; that staff-continued rehabilitation would keep the gains is an inference, not a tested finding. || sharpen: Drop 'the one trial', open on the 4-week gain that faded when visits stopped, and turn the handover diagram into three things a care home can ask the ward for.
- [   ] C03-15 total 38.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Start low, go slow, and keep going :: Older people with hip fracture need lower starting doses of opioids and frequent review, but doses then need increasing until pain on movement is controlled; in a US study people with advanced dementia received about a third of the opioid given to cognitively intact patients, while an orthogeriatric unit using a standard protocol found no such gap. || accuracy: Morrison and Siu data are from 1990s New York and may not describe current practice; Ruel 2021 is small and retrospective; the '83% of cognitively intact patients had no standing order' figure needs checking against the abstract. || sharpen: Lead with the protocol finding rather than 25-year-old US data, state plainly that the data are old, and keep clear of the earlier pain-in-dementia posts.
- [   ] C03-23 total 38.0 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | A geriatric ward improved walking at four months :: In the Trondheim trial, people aged 70 and over with hip fracture admitted directly to comprehensive geriatric care on a dedicated ward had better mobility four months later than those given usual orthopaedic care. || accuracy: Trondheim tested admission to a geriatric ward with comprehensive geriatric assessment, which differs from UK orthogeriatric co-management on an orthopaedic ward; the SPPB difference of 0.74 on a 0 to 12 scale needs a plain reading. || sharpen: Merge with C03-04, or open on the four-month walking result in plain words rather than on the trial design.
- [   ] C03-30 total 37.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Mood and confidence belong in hip fracture follow-up :: Low mood and fear of falling are common in the months after a hip fracture and are linked with slower recovery, so follow-up should ask about mood and confidence as well as the wound and walking. || accuracy: The links between low mood or fear of falling and slower recovery are observational; the Lenze and Visschedijk sources are unverified; the Bower 2020 trajectories come from one US cohort. || sharpen: Make it specific to hip fracture with the fear-of-falling trajectory figures, give the one question to ask at the 6-week review, and check it against the C01 fear-of-falling candidates.
- [   ] C03-29 total 33.0 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | What gets reported after hip fracture, and what patients want back :: A UK consensus says hip fracture trials should measure deaths, pain, daily activities, mobility (walking indoors and outdoors) and quality of life, and patients interviewed in the months after a fracture put steady walking, without falls or fear of falling, for the activities they value at the top of what they wanted back. || accuracy: The NHFD already collects 120-day follow-up on mobility and residence, so 'rarely followed after discharge' may be wrong for UK audit; Griffiths 2015 is a qualitative study of 31 people and supports a priority, not a measure. || sharpen: Check what the NHFD reports at 120 days before contrasting, and centre the post on the patients' priority rather than on audit design.
- [   ] C03-16 total 40.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Which abilities come back first :: After a hip fracture, different abilities recover at different speeds: in a large study, mood, arm function and thinking reached their best in about 4 months while leg function took close to a year, and many people did not regain their earlier level for tasks such as stairs. || EXCLUDED: duplicate of C03-12 (kept the higher score) || accuracy: The opening says abilities come back 'at very different speeds', but the trousers (about 20%) and stairs (about 90%) figures are new dependence at a time point, so they describe whether an ability returns, not how fast; the cohort dates from 1990 to 1991. || sharpen: Rewrite the opening to match the data (how many people regain stairs compared with dressing) and run it only if C03-12 does not use the same Magaziner timeline.

## C04 Osteoporosis and bone health (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: Of the top 14 by score, 6 are for practitioners (24, 29, 16, 09, 30, 14), 5 for both (17, 04, 20, 26, 03) and only 3 for the public (07, 06, 13), and 06 and 13 overlap on the tablet routine. To strengthen the public and carer share, swap 13 or 30 for 12 (Ask this: what would change a no-treatment decision) or 22 (cancer hormone treatment, which also reaches men). Men are otherwise barely represented because most of the trial evidence is in postmenopausal women. Reading the evidence dominates the top group (17, 20, 29, 30, 24, 03), so series spread needs attention; the only Care that works candidate (27) is weak.
Judge allocation reason: All 31 candidates clear the 36-point bar and at least 18 are strong, distinct and supported by trials, Cochrane reviews or product information, while avoiding the earlier treatment-gap angles, so the category merits the top of the range even after merging the overlapping clusters (tablet routine: 06/09/13; vitamin D: 05/20/21; trial numbers: 27/29/30; exercise: 03/18).
- [SEL] C04-07 total 51.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | The bone injection that must not be forgotten :: Denosumab must be given every six months, and stopping or delaying it without a plan can cause rapid bone loss and several spinal fractures at once, so the next due date needs a named owner through every move between services. || accuracy: Cummings 2018 is a post hoc analysis of FREEDOM and Lyu 2020 is observational; 'several spinal fractures at once' needs the absolute rates (about 1 versus about 7 per 100 person-years) to avoid alarm; the illustrative timeline must not read as a real case. || sharpen: Give the absolute fracture rates after stopping instead of 'several spinal fractures at once' and add what to do if the due date has already passed (contact the GP now and ask about a switch plan).
- [SEL] C04-17 total 50.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Cement for spinal fractures: what sham-controlled trials found :: When vertebroplasty (injecting cement into a fractured vertebra) was compared with a sham procedure, pain and function improved about as much in both groups, and open trials against usual care probably overestimated the benefit. || accuracy: NICE TA279 still recommends vertebroplasty or balloon kyphoplasty in a narrow group with severe ongoing pain after a recent unhealed fracture, and the sham-trial evidence is for vertebroplasty, not kyphoplasty; the positive VAPOUR result must be stated plainly. || sharpen: Separate vertebroplasty from balloon kyphoplasty and state NICE's narrow indication so the position reads as consistent with UK guidance.
- [SEL] C04-04 total 49.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | The weekly bone tablet in the last months of life :: In trials, bisphosphonates took about a year to prevent one non-spinal fracture per 100 women treated, so for someone likely to live only weeks or a few months, the tablet's routine may bring burden with little time to help, and stopping can be the right decision. || accuracy: Deardorff 2022 time to benefit comes from postmenopausal women with osteoporosis in trials, not frail people near the end of life (whose higher baseline risk could shorten it); STOPPFrail criterion wording must be checked; the denosumab caveat should state that rebound fractures appear from several months after a missed dose. || sharpen: Put the verified 12-month time to benefit in the first line and add one sentence that stopping the tablet is a review of the drug's likely benefit while pain relief, falls care and comfort continue.
- [SEL] C04-20 total 48.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | The vitamin D trials that found no fracture benefit studied people who had enough :: Large recent trials found vitamin D supplements did not reduce fractures in generally healthy older adults who were mostly not deficient, which says little about people who are deficient, housebound or in care homes, or about vitamin D given alongside bone medicines. || accuracy: VITAL analysed participants with lower baseline vitamin D and found no clear benefit there either (few were severely deficient), so 'says little about deficient people' must be framed as limited evidence, not untested; Chapuy combined calcium with vitamin D; the D-Health hazard ratio needs checking. || sharpen: Acknowledge VITAL's lower-vitamin D subgroup so the argument is about limited evidence in deficiency, and say Chapuy tested calcium and vitamin D together.
- [SEL] C04-29 total 48.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk medium | Bone density is a stand-in for fractures :: A treatment can raise bone density without preventing fractures, as sodium fluoride did in a 1990 trial, so fractures and the person's function, not the density number on a repeat scan, are the outcomes to count. || accuracy: The proposed caption 'The number went up. The fractures did not go down.' is a mirrored aphorism (banned shape); vertebral fractures were not significantly different (163 versus 136), and the SABRE meta-regression citations are unverified. || sharpen: Replace the mirrored caption with a literal one (spine density rose by about a third; fractures away from the spine were about three times as common) and keep the line that approved drugs' density gains do track fracture reduction across trials.
- [SEL] C04-24 total 48.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Denosumab in advanced kidney disease: plan for calcium :: Denosumab is often the option when kidney function rules out a bisphosphonate, but in people on dialysis severe low calcium in the first 12 weeks was common, so calcium, vitamin D and bone and mineral status need checking before, with calcium measured in the weeks after. || accuracy: The 41.1% figure is from dialysis-dependent women aged 65 and over in an observational study; the FDA boxed warning covers advanced CKD more broadly and UK product wording differs, so the two should not be merged. || sharpen: Keep the 41% figure tied to dialysis-dependent women and give the calcium monitoring timing from the product information instead of a placeholder.
