- [SEL] C04-16 total 47.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | New thigh ache on a long-term bisphosphonate: X-ray both femurs :: Atypical femoral fractures are rare, but they are stress fractures that can give weeks of aching thigh or groin pain beforehand and can occur on both sides, so new thigh pain in someone on long-term bisphosphonate or denosumab needs full-length X-rays of both femurs. || accuracy: Proportions with prodromal pain and bilateral fractures must come from the Shane 2014 full text; Black 2020 benefit-harm figures need their denominator and time period; thigh pain has common other causes. || sharpen: Add the next step when plain X-rays are normal but pain continues (MRI or isotope bone scan) and keep the Black 2020 benefit-harm comparison with its denominator.
- [SEL] C04-09 total 47.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | Check the swallow before the weekly bone tablet :: Oral bisphosphonates must be swallowed whole with a full glass of water and followed by 30 minutes upright, so for people who are unable to swallow tablets safely, have oesophageal problems or are unable to sit upright, a yearly infusion or six-monthly injection is usually the better choice. || accuracy: The claim that swallowing assessments and medicine lists are 'rarely read side by side' is unsupported; the SmPC contraindications are oesophageal abnormalities that delay emptying and inability to stay upright for 30 minutes, not dysphagia as such; alendronate oral solution and effervescent forms exist. || sharpen: Drop the unsupported 'rarely' and add one line that liquid or effervescent alendronate exists but still needs the full glass and 30 minutes upright.
- [SEL] C04-30 total 47.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Which spinal fractures did the trial count? :: Headline reductions in spinal fractures in osteoporosis trials often count fractures found on scheduled X-rays, many never noticed by the person; the absolute numbers for painful spinal fractures and hip fractures are smaller and are what many people want to know. || accuracy: 'Never felt by the person' overstates: morphometric fractures can cause pain and height loss without being diagnosed; FREEDOM figures and any proportion of vertebral fractures that come to clinical attention need a source. || sharpen: Replace 'never felt' with 'not diagnosed at the time' and show all three bar pairs per 100 women over 3 years.
- [SEL] C04-26 total 47.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Hip protectors in care homes: a small benefit when they are worn :: In care homes, hip protectors probably reduce hip fractures a little (about 11 fewer per 1,000 residents), make little or no difference for people living at home, and help only if they are comfortable enough to be worn. || accuracy: Evidence is from a 2014 Cochrane review with variable trial durations, so '11 fewer per 1,000' needs its timeframe; any current NICE NG249 statement on hip protectors is unverified. || sharpen: Add a question a family can ask the care home (has a hip protector been offered that fits and can be removed quickly at the toilet?) to make it usable.
- [RES] C04-14 total 46.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Bisphosphonate drug holidays: who can pause, who should continue :: After about five years of an oral bisphosphonate or three years of yearly zoledronate, a review can offer a pause to people at lower risk, while people with a previous hip or spinal fracture, a fracture during treatment or high-dose steroids usually continue; denosumab has no holiday. || accuracy: NOGG continuation criteria include age 70 or over and a hip T-score of -2.5 or lower as well as the listed fractures and glucocorticoid dose; FLEX showed fewer clinical vertebral fractures only, and the HORIZON extension morphometric vertebral fractures only; the reassessment interval needs verification. || sharpen: Use NOGG's exact continuation criteria and reassessment interval in the flow diagram and replace the placeholders before drafting.
- [RES] C04-03 total 45.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Walking and bone: testing a familiar piece of advice :: Walking helps heart, mood and balance, but trials of walking on its own found little effect on spine bone density; UK consensus advice for bone is resistance (strength) exercise and, where suitable, impact exercise, alongside walking. || accuracy: Martyn-St James 2008 found some femoral neck benefit in sensitivity analyses and studied postmenopausal women, not men; cohort studies link walking with fewer hip fractures, so the post must stay with bone density and not imply walking does nothing for fracture risk. || sharpen: Keep the claim to bone density, say walking is still linked with fewer hip fractures in cohort studies, and show two named strength or impact exercises with how often to do them.
- [RES] C04-06 total 45.0 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Tablet, injection or drip: where the effort falls :: The main bone medicines put the effort in different places: the weekly tablet needs a strict morning routine from the person; the six-monthly injection needs a schedule that must not lapse; the yearly drip needs a hospital or clinic visit and often brings a short flu-like reaction after the first dose. || accuracy: The 42.4% acute-phase figure includes background symptoms (11.7% with placebo); UK guidance makes an oral bisphosphonate first line, so the form is not a free choice; zoledronic acid needs a kidney function check; denosumab rebound should be stated with absolute rates. || sharpen: Add that UK guidance usually starts with the tablet, so the useful question is: if the tablet routine will not work for me, can we use the injection or the drip instead?
- [   ] C04-13 total 45.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | The first half hour after the weekly bone tablet :: Alendronic acid is absorbed in tiny amounts, and coffee, juice, food or other tablets taken too soon cut that further, so take it first thing with a full glass of plain water, stay upright, and wait at least 30 minutes before breakfast, drinks or other medicines. || accuracy: Absorption figures come from a pharmacokinetic study in volunteers and do not show a loss of fracture protection; the 'more than 85% with breakfast' figure needs checking against Gertz 1995. || sharpen: Lead with the plain instruction and give the absorption figure second, saying it came from a volunteer absorption study and not from fracture outcomes.
- [   ] C04-25 total 44.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | For very high fracture risk, the order of treatment changes the result :: For people at very high fracture risk, such as those with recent or multiple spinal fractures, starting with a bone-building drug (teriparatide or romosozumab) and then an antiresorptive prevented more new spinal fractures than an antiresorptive alone in head-to-head trials. || accuracy: VERO compared two drugs over 24 months rather than a sequence; both trials were in postmenopausal women only; romosozumab is contraindicated after heart attack or stroke; UK access criteria (NICE TA791, teriparatide) need checking. || sharpen: Define very high risk in one plain line and add the romosozumab contraindication after heart attack or stroke, which applies to many older people.
- [   ] C04-08 total 44.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | FRAX asks yes or no. Count the fractures and the falls. :: FRAX records a previous fracture as yes or no and does not include falls, so a recent fracture, several fractures or frequent falls mean the calculated risk may be too low, and UK guidance lists these as reasons to adjust the decision. || accuracy: FRAXplus (from 2023) offers optional adjustments for fracture recency and number of falls, so 'FRAX does not ask' must specify the standard calculator; the NOGG list of situations where FRAX underestimates risk must be quoted exactly. || sharpen: Name FRAXplus and the NOGG adjustments so the post teaches how to correct for recency, number of fractures and falls rather than implying the tool has no answer.
- [   ] C04-28 total 44.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Acid-reducing tablets and fractures: link versus trial :: Observational studies link long-term proton pump inhibitors (PPIs) with fractures, but in a large randomised trial of pantoprazole for about three years there was no significant difference in fractures, so bone health alone is a weak reason to stop a PPI that is needed. || accuracy: The COMPASS fracture hazard ratio and interval must come from the full text; follow-up was 3 years in a cardiovascular trial population, so 'did not show a clear difference' must not become 'no effect'. || sharpen: Report the COMPASS fracture result with its range from the full text and name the 3-year follow-up and cardiovascular population as limits.
- [   ] C04-18 total 44.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Replace 'be careful' with specific movement advice :: UK consensus advice for people with osteoporosis, including after spinal fractures, is to stay active with strength, balance and suitable impact exercise and to avoid postures with a lot of forward bending, which gives the person instructions instead of a worry. || accuracy: 'Vague caution tends to mean less movement, weaker muscles and more falls' is a causal chain not shown in Brooke-Wavell 2022 and should be labelled as judgement; lifting advice must match the consensus wording on spinal flexion. || sharpen: Mark the point about vague caution as judgement, keep the four instructions verbatim from the consensus, and schedule it well apart from C04-03, which uses the same source.
- [   ] C04-12 total 43.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | 'No bone treatment for now': ask what would change that :: A decision not to start bone treatment is a decision for now; a new broken bone, starting steroid tablets for months, losing height, new back pain after a minor strain, or falling are reasons to have fracture risk looked at again. || accuracy: Trigger thresholds (height loss of 4 cm, glucocorticoids for 3 months or more) must match NOGG 2022 wording; falls as a reassessment trigger is less explicit in NOGG and needs a source. || sharpen: Make each trigger match NOGG wording exactly and add that a review can be requested at any GP appointment rather than waiting for a recall.
- [   ] C04-01 total 43.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | Counting the bone medicine at 12 months :: Many people who start an oral bone medicine after a fracture have stopped within a year, so a bone plan needs named checks at about 16 weeks and 52 weeks, not only a first prescription. || accuracy: Persistence range (about 18% to 75% at 12 months) must be checked against Fatoye 2019; the opening claim that 'far fewer' services can report 12-month persistence needs FLS-DB figures or should go; persistence and adherence are different measures; Imaz 2010 is observational. || sharpen: Open with the verified persistence range as a plain natural frequency and drop the unsupported comparison of what services count unless FLS-DB completeness figures are opened.
- [   ] C04-22 total 43.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Hormone treatment for prostate or breast cancer: ask about your bones :: Hormone treatment for prostate cancer (androgen deprivation therapy) and aromatase inhibitors for breast cancer speed up bone loss and are linked with more fractures, so a fracture risk check at the start of treatment is worth asking for. || accuracy: Shahinian 2005 is observational, so fracture excess is 'linked with' ADT; NICE NG131 and NG101 wording (consider versus offer assessment) must be checked, and those NICE pages may not open. || sharpen: Offer a men-first opening for the prostate version and verify the NICE recommendations through a published summary or NOGG 2022, which can be opened.
- [   ] C04-23 total 42.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | In older people, read the hip result before the spine result :: In older people, arthritic bony spurs in the spine (and collapsed vertebrae or calcium in the aorta) can raise the spine bone density reading, so a reassuring spine result with a lower hip result should be read with care and the hip usually relied on. || accuracy: The position 'base the decision on the hip when spine and hip disagree' is too broad: a genuinely lower spine T-score (for example with glucocorticoids) is valid and the WHO diagnosis uses the lowest valid site; Liu 1997 is an old cohort. || sharpen: Narrow the position to 'when the spine looks better than the hip' and state that a genuinely lower spine result still counts.
- [   ] C04-11 total 42.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Osteopenia: a label, a trial and a choice :: In a trial of 2,000 women aged 65 and over with osteopenia, four zoledronate infusions 18 months apart reduced fragility fractures over six years, with 15 women treated to prevent one woman having a fracture; that trade-off deserves an open conversation. || accuracy: A New Zealand trial selected by T-score; UK treatment decisions use FRAX thresholds and zoledronate is not licensed by T-score category, so the post could be read as advising treatment outside guidance; secondary findings (deaths, cancer) must not be presented as benefits. || sharpen: State that UK decisions use FRAX thresholds rather than the osteopenia label and give the absolute figures per 100 women over 6 years alongside the number needed to treat.
- [   ] C04-19 total 42.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Count the calcium you eat before adding a tablet :: Many people get enough calcium from food; UK guidance prefers calcium from diet and suggests supplements mainly when dietary intake is low, so it is worth estimating your daily intake before taking or refusing a calcium tablet. || accuracy: 'Some people stop their bone medicine because of calcium tablets' needs a source or should go; the WHI kidney stone finding was for calcium plus vitamin D; NOGG target (700 to 1,200 mg) and food calcium values must be verified. || sharpen: Drop the unsourced claim about stopping bone medicine and give the NOGG daily target with three common foods' verified calcium content.
- [   ] C04-15 total 42.0 | public | Weighing it up | public_explanation | risk low | The flu-like days after the first bone infusion :: After a first zoledronic acid infusion, about 4 in 10 people have a short flu-like reaction such as fever, aches or tiredness, usually mild and lasting about three days, and it is uncommon with later infusions. || accuracy: About 4 in 10 had symptoms after a first infusion but about 1 in 10 did with placebo, so the drug-attributable figure is about 3 in 10; second and third infusion rates need verification. || sharpen: Give the placebo comparison in the first line and add the practical prompt about drinking well beforehand and paracetamol afterwards from the product information.
- [   ] C04-02 total 41.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | After the scan, ask for the fracture risk :: A bone density T-score is one input: whether treatment is advised depends on your overall chance of breaking a bone, which also counts age, previous fractures, steroid tablets, family history and other factors. || accuracy: The 82% figure (Siris 2004) comes from peripheral (heel, wrist, finger) densitometry in US women over one year and should not be presented as a hip DXA finding; 'most fractures occur in osteopenia' partly reflects how many people fall in that band. || sharpen: Add what to do with the answer (ask whether your FRAX result is above or below the treatment threshold for your age) and name the scan method behind the 82% figure.
- [   ] C04-10 total 41.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Older age can raise the benefit of bone treatment :: Fracture risk rises steeply with age, so the same relative effect of a bone medicine prevents more fractures per 100 people treated in an older person with osteoporosis, provided they are likely to live longer than the time the medicine takes to help. || accuracy: Constant relative effect across ages is an assumption the HIP trial's over-80 result partly contradicts; competing mortality reduces absolute benefit; illustrative icon arrays risk being read as trial data; the Boonen analyses are unverified. || sharpen: Put the HIP trial over-80 null result in the main text, not a footnote, and replace invented icon arrays with published FRAX age-band probabilities.
- [   ] C04-21 total 40.5 | both | Small detail, real effect | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Very large, infrequent vitamin D doses: more was not better :: In a randomised trial of older women, a single very large dose of vitamin D each year led to more falls and more fractures than placebo, so daily or weekly doses are the safer way to give vitamin D. || accuracy: 'Daily or weekly doses are the safer way' is an inference: Sanders 2010 shows harm from 500,000 IU yearly, not proof that daily dosing is safer; Bischoff-Ferrari 2016 compared doses (no placebo) with falls as a secondary outcome. || sharpen: Aim it at prescribers and replace 'safer' with 'avoid the yearly single-dose schedule that increased falls and fractures in a trial'.
- [   ] C04-27 total 39.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Show fracture risk as people out of 100 :: People understand bone treatment choices better when their risk with and without treatment is shown as people out of 100 over a stated time, with a picture; an osteoporosis decision aid built this way improved understanding in trials. || accuracy: Montori 2011 was a small trial (100 women) and figures need checking; better understanding is not the same as better decisions; the NICE bisphosphonate decision aid's title and status are unverified. || sharpen: Use real published figures in the icon array rather than an illustrative one, and state in the caption that the decision aid did not change how many started treatment.
- [   ] C04-05 total 38.5 | public | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk medium | Vitamin D all year for people who rarely go outside :: UK advice is that people who rarely get sunlight on their skin, including people who are housebound or live in care homes, should take 10 micrograms of vitamin D a day all year, not only in autumn and winter. || accuracy: SACN, PHE and NICE PH56 are official web documents that may not open from this environment, leaving only search extracts; UK wording is 'take' for people rarely outdoors or in care homes but 'consider' for people with dark skin, and this should be reproduced exactly. || sharpen: Anchor the advice in a source that can be opened (NICE PH56 PDF or a published SACN summary) and end with one concrete check: is vitamin D on the medicine chart, and is it being given?
- [   ] C04-31 total 38.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk high | Dementia and bone treatment :: People with dementia have more fractures and, in population studies, are less likely to receive osteoporosis treatment; the decision should rest on the same grounds as for anyone else: likely benefit within the person's expected time, their goals, and a form of treatment they can manage. || accuracy: Haasum 2012 figures (25% versus 7%, OR 0.34) need full-text verification and come from cross-sectional Swedish data of the early 2000s; ZEST was underpowered for fractures; no dementia-fracture meta-analysis has yet been identified. || sharpen: Verify or update the undertreatment data and lead with the more distinctive point that a yearly drip or 6-monthly injection removes the tablet-routine barrier for people with dementia.

## C05 Frailty, function and recovery (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 by score split 8 practitioners (02, 01, 16, 08, 04, 15, 18, 09), 3 public (07, 24, 19) and 3 both (14, 13, 10), so they lean towards practitioners. No 'Who gets left out' post is in the top 14, although the category note is about frailty as a label that shapes decisions unfairly. Consider swapping C05-09 (overlaps C05-03) for C05-17, adding C05-23 or C05-20 for families, and writing C05-14 and C05-10 directly to older readers. Limit the CFS cluster to about three posts (16, 24, plus 17 or 12), and make sure C05-17 and C05-25 agree: frailty can come earlier, but the CFS is not the right tool for it.
Judge allocation reason: All 25 score 36 or more with no disqualifying flag, and after collapsing overlapping pairs and clusters (C05-04/14, C05-08/20, C05-11/22, C05-03/09, the CFS cluster and the younger-adult cluster C05-05/17/25) at least 16 distinct strong ideas remain, so the category supports the maximum of 14.
- [SEL] C05-02 total 48.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Still doing it, but doing it differently :: Many older people keep doing a task by changing how or how often they do it before they report any difficulty, and this stage (called preclinical disability) is linked with later loss of ability, yet a yes or no question about difficulty misses it. || accuracy: The studies were in initially high-functioning women aged 70 to 79 in Baltimore (Fried 2001 is cross-sectional); the claim that earlier detection gives strength and balance work 'more to work with' is an inference these sources do not test. Weiss is dated 2011 in the claim and 2012 in the lead. || sharpen: State the population limit plainly and drop the 'more to work with' inference unless an intervention study supports it, keeping the single history question as the payoff.
- [SEL] C05-01 total 47.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk low | Better than on admission, worse than before the illness :: Recovery measured from the day of admission can look like steady improvement while the person still leaves hospital less able than they were two weeks before they became ill. || accuracy: Confirm from the Covinsky 2003 full text that 23%, 12% and 20% are percentages of all 2,293 patients (not of the 35% who declined); the data come from US hospitals in the 1990s, so the era should be stated. || sharpen: Lead with the roughly 23 in 100 who lost function before admission and never regained it on the ward, name the cohort's era, and give the one discharge-letter line (two weeks before: X; now: Y).
- [SEL] C05-07 total 47.5 | public | Care that works | public_explanation | risk low | Frailty can change in both directions :: Frailty often changes over time and some people become less frail; in pooled studies over about four years, roughly 1 in 7 older people improved, and exercise to build strength combined with enough protein has the most consistent evidence for delaying or reversing frailty. || accuracy: Improvement (about 14 in 100) must sit beside worsening (about 29 in 100), which was twice as common; most improvement among frail people was to pre-frail, not to no criteria; Travers 2019 ranked strength training with protein supplementation 'most effective and easiest to implement' from few trials, which is not the same as 'most consistent evidence' or 'enough protein'. || sharpen: Put the worsening figure next to the improvement figure in the first two lines and describe the Travers finding exactly (strength training plus protein supplementation, limited trials).
- [SEL] C05-16 total 46.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | The frailty score counts dependence, whatever its cause :: Clinical Frailty Scale levels are defined by how much help a person needs, from whatever cause, and the scale's guidance says that in dementia the degree of frailty generally corresponds to the degree of dementia, so the number alone does not say whether the problem is physical or cognitive. || accuracy: Quote the CFS version 2.0 wording exactly on dementia (degree of frailty corresponds to degree of dementia) and on CFS 6 (minimal help with dressing); check licence terms before reproducing scale text. || sharpen: Close with a one-line model referral entry, for example 'CFS 6 (moderate dementia; walks independently)', so the habit is concrete.
- [SEL] C05-24 total 46.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | What the frailty number in a letter means :: 'CFS' in a letter refers to the Clinical Frailty Scale, which runs from 1 (very fit) to 9 (terminally ill); levels 4 to 8 describe increasing need for help, and the score is meant to describe how the person was about two weeks before an illness. || accuracy: Level 4 ('living with very mild frailty') describes symptoms limiting activities without needing daily help, so 'levels 4 to 8 describe increasing need for help' is wrong at level 4; confirm the two-week and level 9 wording in the version 2.0 text. || sharpen: Correct the level 4 description and add one line on what the score is and is not used for, so the three questions have context.
- [SEL] C05-19 total 45.5 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk low | An occupational therapist, a nurse and a handyman :: In a randomised trial of 300 older adults on low incomes who had difficulty with daily tasks, up to 10 home visits from an occupational therapist, a nurse and a home repair worker, working on goals each person chose, reduced difficulty with basic daily activities at 5 months compared with social visits. || accuracy: The 30% figure (RR 0.70) is relative and needs the absolute ADL difficulty scores from the full text; the instrumental-activity result was not statistically clear; CAPABLE is not routinely available in the UK. || sharpen: Give absolute scores and make the UK action specific (ask the GP or council for an occupational therapy assessment of the person and the home) without implying the programme itself is on offer.
- [SEL] C05-08 total 45.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Where comprehensive geriatric assessment has shown benefit :: Comprehensive geriatric assessment in hospital has high-certainty trial evidence that more people are alive and living at home months later, while for frail older people living at home, trials showed little or no difference in death or care home admission and only low-certainty evidence of fewer unplanned admissions. || accuracy: RR 1.06 is a small absolute gain that must be given per 100 from the summary-of-findings table; the community reduction in unplanned admission is low certainty and should be stated as 'may'. || sharpen: Add absolute effects per 100 for hospital and community, trim the card to three rows, and keep C05-20 free of evidence claims so the two do not repeat each other.
- [SEL] C05-04 total 44.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Which frailty? :: Different frailty tools label different people as frail: in one English cohort, 35 published frailty scores often disagreed about which individuals were frail, so results from a study using one tool can't be assumed to apply to people identified with another. || accuracy: The 4.0% to 59.1% spread in Collard 2012 reflects different populations as well as different definitions, so it should not be attributed to definition alone; verify the kappa range 0.10 to 0.83 in Aguayo 2017. || sharpen: Lead with the ELSA finding that the same people were classed differently by different scores, and give the prevalence range only once across C05-04 and C05-14.
- [SEL] C05-15 total 44.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Frailty, disability and many conditions are three different things :: Frailty, disability and having several long-term conditions overlap but are distinct, and in a large US cohort many frail people had no disability, so each needs its own assessment and none should stand in for another. || accuracy: The CHS overlap percentages must come from the Fried 2001 full text; CHS excluded people with some conditions from the frailty analysis, so the 'lifelong disability' opening is conceptual and not a CHS finding. || sharpen: Build the Venn only from verified CHS figures and label the lifelong-disability line as a conceptual point, consistent with C05-17.
- [SEL] C05-14 total 43.5 | both | Reading the evidence | public_explanation | risk medium | Most people over 85 living at home are not frail :: Frailty becomes more common with age, but in pooled community studies about 1 in 10 people aged 65 and over were frail, and even among those aged 85 and over most were not, so age alone does not tell you who is frail. || accuracy: The 85-and-over figure (about 1 in 4) must come from the Collard full-text age table; the figures are for people living at home and are definition-dependent, and broader frailty-index definitions give higher prevalence at 85 and over. || sharpen: Put 'living at home' in the headline, verify the age-band figures, and name the definitions in one short line on the visual.
- [RES] C05-18 total 43.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk low | A frailty score from the GP record counts what was coded :: The electronic frailty index used in UK general practice is calculated from 36 deficits coded in the GP record; it predicts outcomes across populations, but it can only count what has been recorded, and its developers list limitations of the original version that a newer version addresses. || accuracy: Check whether eFI2 has replaced the original eFI in UK GP systems by September 2026, or the critique may describe an outdated version; 'people who seldom see the GP score low' is a consequence of design, not a measured finding. || sharpen: State which version is in use now and present under-coding as a design consequence, closing on 'assess the person'.
- [RES] C05-09 total 43.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk low | Full marks on the Barthel, and still unable to manage at home :: The Barthel index scores 10 basic activities and none of the instrumental tasks needed to live at home, such as shopping, cooking, managing medicines and money, so a person can score full marks on a ward and still be unable to manage alone. || accuracy: The Barthel ceiling-effect source is unsure; do not imply that assessment in the home setting improves outcomes, since trial evidence for pre-discharge home visits is limited. || sharpen: Use one concrete example (a person scoring full marks who cannot manage their tablets or shopping) and make clear how it differs from C05-03 if both run.
- [RES] C05-13 total 43.0 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | What people living with frailty want from emergency care :: When people living with frailty were interviewed in their first 72 hours of emergency hospital care, the outcomes they wanted were about autonomy (information, control and feeling secure) and about functioning, which routine acute care measures do not record. || accuracy: Qualitative study of 22 people; the claim that routine acute measures do not record these outcomes needs a source or should be framed as a description of common metrics (time in department, admission, length of stay); the severe-frailty finding is cut off in the abstract. || sharpen: Use the participants' own themes on the visual and make the closing a question staff can ask in the first conversation.
- [   ] C05-10 total 42.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Telling someone they are frail :: Many older people dislike the word frail and hear it as a judgement on who they are, yet they want to know their risks and what might prevent or improve them, and that information can be given without relying on the label. || accuracy: Schoenborn 2018 is one US study of 29 people in focus groups; Warmoth 2016 and the Age UK/BGS report are unverified; 'frail people were more receptive' is from that single small study. || sharpen: Move it to Teaching round or Ask this, since it concerns wording rather than a treatment decision, and name the study size in the post.
- [   ] C05-03 total 41.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | 'Independent' needs a list of names :: 'Lives alone, independent' can hide regular help from family, neighbours or services; asking who does each task (shopping, meals, medicines, money, laundry, transport) gives a truer picture of function and a more accurate frailty score, and shows how fragile the arrangement is. || accuracy: The national survey figure on unpaid help is unsourced, and 'the question that predicts a crisis' is a predictive claim with no evidence behind it; CFS 5 and 6 wording must be checked against the version 2.0 text. || sharpen: Cut the survey claim and the 'predicts a crisis' wording, and shrink the table to four tasks plus the 'who covers?' row so it reads at phone size; differentiate it from C05-09.
- [   ] C05-06 total 41.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | The measure that found the benefit :: In a randomised trial of a geriatric assessment team for frail older people, goal attainment scaling, which scores progress on each person's own goals, was the only measure that detected clinically important change; standard function and quality-of-life scales did not. || accuracy: One 2003 secondary analysis of one trial; goal attainment scaling is unblinded and depends on how goals are set, so its greater responsiveness may partly reflect bias, and 'the measure that found the benefit' assumes the benefit was real. || sharpen: Reframe from 'found the benefit' to 'the only measure that changed', state the bias risk beside the finding, and present goal attainment scaling as an addition to standard scales.
- [   ] C05-17 total 41.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | A frailty scale built for older people :: The Clinical Frailty Scale is not widely validated in younger people or in people with stable disabilities affecting one body system, such as cerebral palsy or a learning disability, and using it for them can record lifelong dependence as frailty. || accuracy: The NICE NG159 amendment dates and wording need a published source because the NICE page cannot be opened here; 'dependence caused by a stable lifelong disability is not frailty' overstates, since people with lifelong disabilities can also become frail (as C05-25 shows). || sharpen: Source the NICE change from a published report and rephrase to 'should not be scored as frailty on the CFS', so it agrees with C05-25.
- [   ] C05-21 total 41.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | criticism | risk medium | 'Off legs' needs a cause :: Labels such as 'acopia', 'social admission' and 'off legs' describe a sudden loss of function in an older person without explaining it, and people given these labels have often been found to have acute, treatable illness. || accuracy: Kee and Rippingale 2009 has no abstract and must be read in full before claiming most labelled patients had acute illness; Oliver 2008 was not found; the Curran survey findings are unverified. || sharpen: Read Kee 2009 in full, keep the replacement note on the visual as the payoff, and make the argument distinct from E133 and E153 by focusing on the specific words.
- [   ] C05-23 total 39.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | When you and your parent disagree about what they can do :: Older people and their relatives often give different accounts of what the older person can manage; in one study relatives tended to report more disability than the person did, and both differed from what was observed, so watching the task being done is the fairest way to settle it. || accuracy: Magaziner 1997 studied people after hip fracture in Baltimore, so it does not show a general pattern for all older people and relatives; one observed attempt is also imperfect evidence of everyday ability; Elam 1991 is unverified. || sharpen: Say the evidence comes from people recovering from hip fracture and present observation as settling the question for that task, not as the final word.
- [   ] C05-25 total 39.5 | public | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk medium | Frailty often comes earlier for adults with learning disabilities :: In a large Dutch study of adults aged 50 and over with intellectual disabilities, frailty was more common and began earlier than in the general population, and higher frailty scores were followed by increases in care needs. || accuracy: The 6.6% vs more than 43% 'least frail group' figure is an awkward metric for the public; Evenhuis 2012 prevalence is unverified; the Dutch cohort was drawn from learning-disability services; 'treatable decline attributed to the lifelong disability' is a judgement the studies did not test. || sharpen: Replace the least-frail icon arrays with a verified plain prevalence comparison (for example 50 to 64-year-olds with learning disabilities against much older people in the general population) and say who was studied.
- [   ] C05-12 total 38.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | If the frailty score will change the decision, score it carefully :: The Clinical Frailty Scale was designed to summarise a geriatric assessment but is now scored by many staff without frailty training; a published classification tree helps less experienced staff score it close to expert ratings. || accuracy: Theou 2021 is a single-centre study of 115 patients on a geriatric ward; check who completed the tree, since the study compared tree scores with geriatricians' scores and may not show that the tree improves scoring by untrained staff; the 88.5% figure is agreement between the tree and junior doctors, not junior doctors' accuracy. || sharpen: Frame the tree as a structured aid with reported agreement, not as proof that non-specialists score accurately with it, and make 'record how the score was reached' the main instruction.
- [   ] C05-20 total 38.5 | public | Care that works | public_explanation | risk low | What a comprehensive geriatric assessment should give you :: A comprehensive geriatric assessment is a team process that looks at medical problems, medicines, mood and memory, mobility and daily tasks, and home and support, and it should end with a plan that says what will be done, by whom, and when it will be reviewed. || accuracy: 'A named person responsible for follow-up' is a judgement, not part of the Cochrane definition, and should be marked as a reasonable request; the BGS toolkit and Parker 2018 are unverified. || sharpen: Leave the evidence to C05-08 and make this a pure preparation and follow-up checklist written directly to older people and families.
- [   ] C05-22 total 38.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Before emergency surgery, frailty told more than age :: In a UK study of 934 people aged 65 and over having emergency abdominal surgery, frailty before admission predicted who would need more care at discharge more strongly than age did. || accuracy: Keep odds ratios out of 'times more likely'; confirm the frailty definition behind 20.2% (CFS 5 or more) and the denominator for 37.4% (survivors to discharge). || sharpen: Lead with the roughly 37 in 100 who needed more care after discharge, and choose either this or C05-11 (or pair them explicitly) since they rest on the same study.
- [   ] C05-05 total 37.5 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Frailty before 65, and more often in poorer areas :: Frailty is found in middle age as well as later life, and in UK Biobank it was linked with socioeconomic deprivation and with having several long-term conditions, so services that only look for frailty from 65 can miss people in poorer areas who become frail earlier. || accuracy: UK Biobank used a modified frailty phenotype in healthy volunteers, and 'frailty' at 37 to 45 largely reflects chronic illness; the opening has an unfilled number (3 in 100), the 65-and-over contract age limit is unverified, and the Barnett 10 to 15 year wording is 'unsure'. || sharpen: Pick one message (frailty by this definition appears in middle age and is linked with deprivation), give 3 in 100 frail and 38 in 100 pre-frail with the healthy-volunteer caveat, and drop the CFS and Barnett side claims.
- [   ] C05-11 total 36.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Told you are too frail for an operation? Ask which parts could change :: Frailty raises the risks of surgery, but some of the problems that add to it can be assessed and sometimes improved before planned surgery, and UK guidance from 2021 recommends identifying frailty and involving geriatric assessment throughout the surgical pathway. || accuracy: The CPOC/BGS guideline full text cannot be opened here and the named optimisable factors are unverified; optimisation or prehabilitation evidence is mixed and largely irrelevant to emergency surgery, so the post should not imply frailty risk is usually modifiable. || sharpen: Verify the guideline's named factors, limit the modifiable-risk point to planned surgery, and vary the opening so it does not echo E134 ('too frail for rehab').

## C06 Medicines and prescribing burden (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 11)
Judge balance note: Top 14 by rank: 4 practitioners (C06-18, C06-04, C06-13, C06-19), 5 public (C06-29, C06-15, C06-17, C06-20, C06-32), 5 both (C06-11, C06-22, C06-08, C06-25, C06-01): well balanced, and mostly teaching, explanation or good care rather than complaint. Avoid selecting overlapping pairs together without distinct angles: C06-11 and C06-16 (leaflets), C06-02 and C06-13 (PRIME discharge harm), C06-05 and C06-17 (planning stops), C06-21 and C06-32 (kidney-risk drug classes). Check cross-category overlap before finalising: C06-22 with C11 sleep, C06-24 and C06-25 with C07, C06-10 with C19, C06-20 with C15, C06-28 with C04, C06-08 with C10.
Judge allocation reason: 30 of 32 candidates score 36 or more with no disqualifying flag (C06-09 duplicates C06-05; C06-14 scores 35), and well over 14 of them are distinct, well-evidenced and useful, so the category merits the maximum. Note: the weights as given sum to 11, so the true maximum is 55, not 50.
- [SEL] C06-18 total 54.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Swollen ankles after amlodipine: the prescribing cascade :: Ankle swelling after starting a calcium channel blocker such as amlodipine is a recognised side effect, and treating it with a loop diuretic adds a second drug with its own harms; the first step is to reconsider the first drug. || accuracy: Absolute differences are small (about 14 versus 7 in 1,000 at 90 days), so do not imply the cascade is very common; the 'possible third drug' step on the visual should be labelled illustrative. || sharpen: Give the absolute figures in the post and close with the alternatives (lower the dose, switch the drug, or reconsider the need for it) rather than only 'reconsider the first drug'.
- [SEL] C06-11 total 52.5 | both | Reading the evidence | public_explanation | risk low | What 'common' means in a medicine leaflet :: In UK medicine leaflets, 'common' means up to 1 in 10 people, 'uncommon' up to 1 in 100 and 'rare' up to 1 in 1,000, and people given the words alone tend to overestimate the risk by a large margin. || accuracy: 'Common' covers 1 in 100 to 1 in 10, so 'up to 1 in 10' is only the upper bound; check the Knapp 2004 sample and figures; participants were reading hypothetical information, so the result shows how the words are read, not what people later did. || sharpen: Close with a translation the reader can use at the kitchen table: 'common' means at most 10 in 100 people, and often fewer.
- [SEL] C06-22 total 50.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | A booklet that helped people stop long-term sleeping tablets :: In a Canadian trial, sending long-term benzodiazepine users aged 65 or over a booklet on the risks with a tapering plan led 27 in 100 to stop within 6 months, compared with 5 in 100 without it; a later pharmacist-led version had similar results. || accuracy: EMPOWER enrolled long-term benzodiazepine users taking them for sleep or anxiety, so 'sleeping tablets' is loose; stopping was measured through pharmacy renewals; both trials were in Quebec. || sharpen: Name the medicine group accurately and add one line on what the booklet contained, so readers know what to ask for.
- [SEL] C06-29 total 50.0 | public | What gets counted | criticism | risk low | A medicines review you were not part of :: In interviews, UK GPs and pharmacists said most medication reviews took place with little or no input from the patient and medicines were rarely stopped, so a review recorded in your notes may not have involved you, and you can ask for one that does. || accuracy: Qualitative interviews with 23 professionals report their accounts, not how often reviews exclude patients; the post must say so. || sharpen: Keep the framing on workload and systems rather than individual GPs, and close with the three questions so it reads as a request.
- [SEL] C06-08 total 49.5 | both | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | A used patch still contains medicine :: Strong pain patches such as fentanyl or buprenorphine need three habits: take the old patch off before putting a new one on, keep heat sources off the patch, and fold used patches sticky sides together before safe disposal. || accuracy: Residual drug in used patches varies by product and should be described as 'a substantial amount' unless a specific figure is sourced; MHRA advice is on gov.uk pages that may not be openable here, so pair it with SPCs or published studies. || sharpen: Add what to watch for (unusual drowsiness, slow or shallow breathing) and what to do, so carers know the warning sign as well as the three checks.
- [SEL] C06-04 total 48.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Fewer inappropriate medicines, same admissions :: In the OPERAM trial, a structured medicines review reduced inappropriate prescribing in older inpatients but did not show fewer drug-related readmissions, so changing a medicines list and changing outcomes are separate steps that each need measuring. || accuracy: Verify that OPERAM reported a reduction in inappropriate prescribing as an outcome before the opening says 'cut'; the 86 in 100 figure is the proportion with inappropriate prescribing in the intervention arm, not a reduction, and the visual must not imply otherwise. || sharpen: Close with one specific test readers can apply to the next deprescribing trial, such as whether the outcome measured was one patients value.
- [SEL] C06-13 total 48.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Doctors could not tell who would be harmed by their medicines after discharge :: In an English cohort, more than a third of older people had medication-related harm within 8 weeks of discharge, and discharging doctors' predictions of who would be harmed did not match who was, so medicines follow-up after discharge should not depend on clinical hunch. || accuracy: PRIME 'medication-related harm' included non-serious events and harm linked with non-adherence, so explain what the 37 in 100 counts; 85% of predictions were by doctors with under 5 years' experience, so 'whatever their experience' is weakly supported. || sharpen: Say what counted as harm and how many needed healthcare, and close with a named follow-up route such as a community pharmacy check after discharge.
- [SEL] C06-15 total 48.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | The medicines that are not tablets :: When giving a medicines list, include eye drops, inhalers, patches, creams, injections given at a clinic or every few months, medicines supplied by a hospital clinic, and anything bought from a shop or pharmacy, because these are easily left off. || accuracy: Tam 2005 figures need checking; the claim that hospital-supplied medicines are missing from GP records needs a UK source; tie the St John's wort interaction to a named source. || sharpen: Lead with the most surprising example (beta-blocker eye drops can slow the heart, or a six-monthly bone injection that will not be in the bag) to strengthen the opening.
- [SEL] C06-17 total 48.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | Ask what would make you stop it :: When a new medicine is started, ask what it is meant to achieve, how you will both know whether it is working, when that will be checked, and what would make it worth stopping. || sharpen: Add one worked example (a new blood pressure tablet: the goal, a check in a few weeks, stop if dizzy on standing) so the four questions feel concrete.
- [SEL] C06-25 total 48.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | When a good HbA1c is a warning :: For frail older people with diabetes, an HbA1c that looks well controlled while they take insulin or a sulfonylurea can come with frequent low blood sugars, and guidelines support relaxing targets and reducing these medicines. || accuracy: Lipska 2015 uses US NHANES data from 2001 to 2010 and shows potential overtreatment, not measured low blood sugars; a low HbA1c can also reflect anaemia or altered red cell turnover; the hypoglycaemia to falls link needs a source. || sharpen: Add a UK source for targets in frailty and one question families can ask: how many low sugars have there been, and is the dose still right?
- [SEL] C06-01 total 47.5 | both | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | Before a tablet is crushed :: Difficulty swallowing tablets is common and often unspoken, and crushing, splitting or opening some products (modified-release, enteric-coated and others) changes how they work, so ask how tablets are being taken and check with a pharmacist before altering any of them. || accuracy: Serrano Santos 2016 error rates (57.3% vs 30.8%) come from a small observational study and need checking against full text; 'no tablet should be split' overstates, since scored tablets are designed to be halved; crushing usually makes use off-licence, which should be stated accurately. || sharpen: Open with one concrete example (a crushed modified-release tablet can release the whole dose at once) and say that crushing with the person's agreement is different from hiding medicine in food.
- [RES] C06-19 total 47.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | The kidney number to dose from :: For some medicines, including direct oral anticoagulants, doses should be calculated from creatinine clearance using the Cockcroft-Gault equation, because eGFR can overestimate kidney function in small, older people with low muscle mass and lead to overdosing. || accuracy: Check the MHRA 2019 wording on which body weight to use and which patient groups it covers; some SPCs now allow eGFR for some drugs, so limit 'wrong number' to drugs whose dosing is based on creatinine clearance. || sharpen: Name the triggers precisely (age 75 or over, low body weight or muscle mass, a DOAC or narrow-margin drug) so readers know when to calculate.
- [RES] C06-20 total 47.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | Parkinson's medicines on the person's own times :: People with Parkinson's often take their medicines at precise, individual times; in hospital, ask for doses at the person's own times, ask whether they can keep taking their own, ask for a plan if they are unable to swallow or are nil by mouth, and ask that anti-sickness drugs that block dopamine are avoided. || accuracy: Lertxundi 2017 figures (almost a third of admissions, 4-day longer stay, higher mortality) are observational and need checking; domperidone is often the preferred anti-sickness drug, so avoid implying all anti-sickness drugs are unsafe. || sharpen: Add the safe alternative the ward can use and a line on planning doses when the person is nil by mouth before surgery.
- [RES] C06-24 total 47.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Daily aspirin 'just in case' after 70 :: For people aged 70 or over who have never had a heart attack or stroke, a large trial found daily low-dose aspirin did not help them live longer free of disability and increased serious bleeding; people who take aspirin because of a past heart attack, stroke or stent should not stop without advice. || accuracy: The mortality difference was unexpected and not the primary outcome, so do not headline it; the 65 or over threshold applied only to US Black and Hispanic participants; verify NICE NG238 wording on aspirin. || sharpen: Keep the two-column distinction as the core and give serious bleeding as natural frequencies (about 38 versus 28 in 1,000 over about 5 years).
- [   ] C06-32 total 47.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Ibuprofen with a water tablet and a blood pressure tablet :: People who take a water tablet together with an ACE inhibitor or ARB (for example ramipril or losartan) should check with a pharmacist before taking ibuprofen or other anti-inflammatory painkillers, because the three together are linked with a higher risk of kidney injury, especially in the first month. || accuracy: Rate ratios from a nested case-control design; absolute risk is low (about 7 per 10,000 person-years overall); must not imply ibuprofen with only one of these drugs is shown safe. || sharpen: Give the absolute context in the post and name what to use instead (paracetamol for most people, or ask the pharmacist).
- [   ] C06-27 total 47.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Heartburn after stopping an acid tablet can be a rebound :: After stopping a proton pump inhibitor (an acid-reducing tablet such as omeprazole or lansoprazole), heartburn can return for a few weeks as a temporary rebound, even in people who never had heartburn, so a gradual step-down with a plan is more likely to succeed than stopping and restarting at the first symptom. || accuracy: The trial was in healthy volunteers after 8 weeks of treatment; the claim that step-down succeeds more often than stopping lacks trial support and should be phrased as guideline advice. || sharpen: Say plainly that some people need long-term acid tablets and should not stop without advice, and give the rebound duration seen in the trial.
- [   ] C06-28 total 46.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Tablets that should not be taken together :: Some tablets block each other's absorption when swallowed together: levothyroxine works best on an empty stomach and kept apart from calcium, iron and antacids by several hours, and weekly bone tablets such as alendronate need plain water, an empty stomach and staying upright afterwards. || accuracy: Separation intervals for levothyroxine vary between sources (30 to 60 minutes before breakfast; 2 to 4 hours from calcium or iron), so quote the SPC wording and advise checking personal timings with a pharmacist. || sharpen: Lead with the common real combination, a thyroid tablet and calcium taken together at breakfast, as a single concrete case.
- [   ] C06-31 total 46.0 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | The Tayside programme that cut high-risk prescribing :: In Tayside, a programme combining education, searchable lists of patients on specific high-risk NSAID and antiplatelet combinations, and payment for review reduced that prescribing, and admissions for gastrointestinal bleeding and heart failure fell. || accuracy: Admission outcomes were secondary; kidney injury admissions did not show a clear difference; check whether gains persisted after the incentives ended before implying a lasting effect. || sharpen: Open with the three design features (a few named combinations, lists of patients, paid time to review) and add the post-incentive finding if it can be sourced.
- [   ] C06-06 total 45.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Most older people say they would stop a medicine if their doctor said they could :: In a US population survey, 92 in 100 older adults said they would be willing to stop one or more regular medicines if their doctor said it was possible, so prescribers should offer the option rather than assume reluctance. || accuracy: The survey measured hypothetical willingness conditional on a doctor saying stopping was possible; the prescriber-reluctance premise needs a primary source, not Reeve's introduction; the adjusted odds ratio must not become 'times more likely'. || sharpen: Make the conditional the point: willingness depended on the doctor raising it, so the practical move is for the prescriber to offer the option.
- [   ] C06-30 total 45.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Look for the missing medicine too :: A good medicines review looks for missing medicines as well as unnecessary ones; common omissions in older people include a laxative alongside a regular opioid and anticoagulation for atrial fibrillation when it is indicated and wanted. || accuracy: Verify START version 3 wording for laxatives with regular opioids and anticoagulation in atrial fibrillation; avoid repeating bone-protection examples (E095, E097). || sharpen: Open with one concrete omission and the claim that a review judged by the number of drugs removed will miss it.
- [   ] C06-03 total 44.5 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Count the times of day, not only the tablets :: The number of separate times each day a person has to take medicines is part of treatment burden in its own right, and a review can often reduce it even when no medicine is stopped. || accuracy: The 12-month reduction was about half a dosing time per day (a modest effect), and falls were higher in the intervention arm (IRR 2.20); the post must not imply simplification improved outcomes, and residential aged care in Australia differs from home care. || sharpen: Make the claim about what to count (dosing times as treatment burden) and present SIMPLER as showing feasibility with an unexplained falls signal, not as support for the recommendation.
- [   ] C06-07 total 44.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Write why it was stopped :: When a medicine is stopped or changed in hospital, the discharge letter should say why and what would justify review, because without the reason the next prescriber may simply restart it. || accuracy: Verify NICE NG5 wording on recording reasons for changes; the claim that medicines stopped in hospital are restarted after discharge has no identified source and should be dropped if none is found. || sharpen: Add the receiving side: GPs and pharmacists can query any stop without a reason, and patients can ask for the reason to be written on the letter.
- [   ] C06-10 total 44.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Medicines for a future the person may not have :: Many older people near the end of life are still taking preventive medicines whose benefits take years to appear, and reviewing them with the person or family when goals shift towards comfort is part of good care. || accuracy: Kutner 2015 did not formally show noninferiority on 60-day mortality, so stopping must not be called proven safe; the Morin 2017 figures (30.3% to 47.2%; 15.8% on a statin) need checking; Swedish and US data may not reflect UK practice. || sharpen: Open with the rise to about 47 in 100 taking ten or more medicines in the last month of life, and check against C19 and C07 candidates before selection.
- [   ] C06-16 total 44.5 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | The leaflet counts side effects, not benefits :: Medicine leaflets list side effects with frequencies but usually give no figure for how likely the medicine is to help or how long that takes, so people deciding whether to keep taking a medicine see only half the picture; ask the prescriber or pharmacist for the benefit in numbers. || accuracy: Verify the QRD template wording; the claim that the imbalance leads people to stop useful medicines has no direct source and must be marked as a concern; the drug facts box trials were US general-population studies. || sharpen: Run as a pair with C06-11 or merge them, and give one real worked benefit figure so readers see what answer they are asking for.
- [   ] C06-12 total 44.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Same medicine, different-looking tablet :: Generic tablets can change colour or shape when the pharmacy's supplier changes; if tablets look different, check the name and strength on the box, or ask the pharmacist, before stopping them or taking extra. || accuracy: Kesselheim 2014 is a US observational study reporting odds ratios, which must not become 'times more likely'; the UK claim about changing manufacturers between dispensings needs a source. || sharpen: Give a UK-relevant example (the same generic from a different manufacturer) and keep the single practical check on the label.
- [   ] C06-05 total 43.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | A trial off, planned like a trial on :: Stopping a long-term medicine can usually be treated as a planned trial: agree what to watch for, who will watch, when to look again and what would mean restarting. || accuracy: Graves 1997 is a small, retrospective, single-clinic US study from 1997; 'for most medicines a stop can be reversed' needs qualifying for drugs with withdrawal effects or where restarting carries risk. || sharpen: Keep the four-question card and rely less on the 1997 percentages, so it stands as public decision support clearly distinct from C06-09 and C06-17.
- [   ] C06-23 total 43.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Ask whether it is being taken before deciding it has failed :: Between a third and a half of medicines prescribed for long-term conditions are not taken as recommended, often for reasons the person can explain, so ask about medicine-taking without blame before increasing a dose or adding a drug. || accuracy: The NICE 'a third to a half' figure dates from 2009 and older data; the claim that escalating doses on paper causes harm needs a source or should be dropped. || sharpen: Make it specific to older people: include swallowing, dexterity, eyesight and timing as reasons, and give one no-blame question verbatim.
- [   ] C06-02 total 41.5 | public | Who gets left out | decision_support | risk medium | The medicines handover that assumes someone at home :: People leaving hospital on new medicines, especially those who live alone, should ask how many days of each new medicine they are taking home, who will arrange the next supply and by when, and whether their community pharmacy has been told what changed. || accuracy: The 413 of 1,116 figure is cited to the Beers-criteria paper; the incidence paper is Parekh 2018 BJCP. The Discharge Medicines Service is England-only and must not be implied for Scotland; discharge supply length varies and must not be given as one figure. || sharpen: Lead with the three questions in nation-neutral wording and move service-specific facts to the LinkedIn version so the post holds in Scotland as well as England.
- [   ] C06-21 total 41.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Which tablets to pause when you are ill, and when to restart :: People taking metformin, ACE inhibitors or ARBs, water tablets, SGLT2 inhibitors or anti-inflammatory painkillers should ask their GP or pharmacist in advance which to pause during an illness that stops them keeping fluids down, and when to restart. || accuracy: Sick day guidance has limited evidence and Think Kidneys warned of harms; the medicine lists differ between cards and SGLT2 inhibitors appear only on newer versions; most sources are web pages that may not be openable. || sharpen: Lead with the restart instruction as the overlooked point, and restrict the medicine list to the current Scottish card.
- [   ] C06-26 total 41.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | criticism | risk medium | A blister pack is an answer to one kind of problem :: Multi-compartment compliance aids (blister packs, dosette boxes) help some people with some adherence problems, are unsuitable for some medicines, make tablets harder to identify and doses harder to change, and should follow an assessment of what the problem actually is. || accuracy: Check which NICE guideline (NG5 or NG67) covers compliance aids and its wording; RPS 2013 is older guidance; the dexterity claim is unsourced. || sharpen: Reconcile explicitly with E169 and open with the decision question, then give the branch most people miss (a pack does not help someone who doubts the medicine).
- [   ] C06-14 total 35.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Stopping psychotropics reduced falls; keeping them stopped was the hard part :: In a small blinded trial, gradual withdrawal of psychotropic medicines in older people reduced falls, and the authors reported that permanent withdrawal was very difficult to achieve, so deprescribing for falls needs follow-up over months. || accuracy: Small 1999 trial (93 people) with attrition; the restart figure the headline depends on is only in the full text; the relative hazard must not be converted to absolute risk; overlaps C01 falls candidates and E071. || sharpen: Anchor on current guidance (World Falls Guidelines, STOPPFall) and use Campbell only as a supporting example, or move the idea to C01.
- [   ] C06-09 total 39.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Stopping has side effects too :: Stopping a medicine has its own adverse effects, mostly the original condition returning and sometimes withdrawal reactions, so deprescribing should be tapered where needed, done one change at a time where possible, and followed up. || EXCLUDED: duplicate of C06-05 (kept the higher score) || accuracy: The 26 in 100 headline comes from an old, small, retrospective VA study; inferring 'one change at a time' from an association with number of drugs stopped is weak; the D-PRESCRIBE withdrawal-symptom figure needs verifying. || sharpen: If kept, merge with C06-05 as its practitioner version, or reframe around the specific drug classes that need tapering, using current sources.

## C07 Treatment decisions and proportional care (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 split into 5 public (32, 17, 07, 24, 11), 5 both (27, 05, 30, 23, 02) and 4 practitioner (29, 12, 03, 18), with 'do not undertreat because of age' posts (30, 23, 17, 27) balancing the 'less treatment' ones. Overlaps to resolve at allocation: 32, 27 and 11 with C19; 29, 22 and 28 with C06; 08 with C13; 06 and 33 with C20; 16 and 09 with C09. Within this set, take at most two of the blood pressure group (03, 10, 15, 22), one or two of the surgical consent group (01, 21, 25) and one of the time-burden pair (07, 14). If C19 takes 32 and 27, the next C07 choices are 01, 26, 10 and 06. Note that the weights add to 11, so the real maximum total is 55, not 50.
Judge allocation reason: All 33 clear 36 and 19 score 45 or more with no disqualifying flag; even after moving the ones better placed in C06, C19, C13 or C20, at least 14 strong C07 posts remain, and proportional care is central to the brief and missing from the earlier collection.
- [SEL] C07-32 total 52.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | If you have an ICD, ask about switching off the shocks :: An implantable cardioverter-defibrillator can give shocks near the end of life that no longer help, and its shock function can be switched off painlessly without an operation, so people with an ICD can discuss in advance when that would be right. || accuracy: Figures for shocks in the last days of life vary by study (Goldstein 2004; Kinch Westerdahl 2014) and need verifying before any number is used; check whether the 2013 BHF guidance has been updated; keep the claim to the shock function, since pacing continues after deactivation. || sharpen: Keep the tone about planning rather than expectation, verify one figure on shocks near death for the caption, and add who to ask (the heart failure, cardiology or device clinic team).
- [SEL] C07-27 total 51.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | A limit on one treatment is not a limit on care :: A decision not to offer one treatment, such as intensive care or CPR, applies only to that treatment; pain relief, other treatments, help with eating, rehabilitation and company should still be offered on their merits, and a good plan says what will be done. || accuracy: Beach and Morrison 2002 used case scenarios given to physicians (a vignette survey), not observed orders; the BMA, Resuscitation Council UK and RCN guidance concerns DNACPR specifically, so the intensive care opening needs its own source or should be reframed around CPR or ReSPECT. || sharpen: Reframe the opening around a DNACPR or ReSPECT decision where the guidance applies, describe Beach and Morrison as a scenario survey, and keep the long 'continues' column as the visual.
- [SEL] C07-05 total 51.0 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | A trial that counted years lived without disability :: ASPREE, a large trial of daily aspirin in healthy older people, made its main outcome whether people stayed alive without dementia or lasting physical disability; aspirin did not improve it and increased serious bleeding. || accuracy: State verified ASPREE figures: 19,114 participants, median 4.7 years, no difference in disability-free survival, major haemorrhage about 3.8% versus 2.8%, all-cause mortality 5.9% versus 5.2% with the authors' caution that the mortality finding was unexpected; PREVENTABLE may not have reported by September 2026, so describe it only as ongoing unless verified. || sharpen: Lead with the outcome choice in plain words, give the bleeding result as N in 100 over about 5 years, and keep the line for people taking aspirin after a heart attack or stroke prominent on the visual as well as the caption.
- [SEL] C07-30 total 50.0 | both | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk medium | No upper age limit for emergency stroke treatment :: Trials of clot-busting treatment for stroke included many people over 80 and showed benefit in them, and UK stroke guidance sets no upper age limit, so anyone with stroke symptoms should call 999 at once, whatever their age. || accuracy: IST-3 enrolled 3035 people, of whom 1617 (53%) were over 80, across both arms, so say 'enrolled', not 'treated'; eligibility still depends on time since onset, brain imaging and pre-stroke function; confirm IST-3 is still the largest thrombolysis trial given recent tenecteplase trials; check the 2023 guideline wording on age. || sharpen: Change 'treated' to 'enrolled', add one line that eligibility depends on timing and scans rather than age, and keep the 999 instruction as the close.
- [SEL] C07-23 total 49.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | A geriatric assessment before chemotherapy :: In a cluster randomised trial of people aged 70 and over starting cancer treatment, giving oncologists a geriatric assessment with management recommendations reduced serious side effects without shortening survival. || accuracy: GAP70+ enrolled 718 patients; grade 3 to 5 toxicity was about 51% versus 71%; the intervention was a geriatric assessment summary plus management recommendations given to oncologists, so the effect belongs to the whole package, in people with advanced cancer starting a high-risk regimen; ASCO guidance is US. || sharpen: Give the toxicity result as about 51 versus 71 in 100 and add a sentence on how to ask for a geriatric assessment in UK services where it is not routine.
- [SEL] C07-29 total 49.5 | practitioners | Weighing it up | criticism | risk low | When a good HbA1c signals overtreatment :: In older people with diabetes and poor health, a low HbA1c achieved with insulin or a sulfonylurea often signals overtreatment, because the risk of low blood sugar and falls is immediate while the benefit of tight control takes years. || accuracy: Lipska 2015 used US NHANES 2001 to 2010 data; check the exact proportions of people in poor health with HbA1c below 7% and of those on insulin or sulfonylureas; the hypoglycaemia link with falls is observational; the UKPDS legacy benefit was in newly diagnosed middle-aged people, not frail older adults. || sharpen: Add the specific action (review HbA1c and drug class together, reduce the sulfonylurea or insulin first) with the UK frailty guidance target range, and state the UKPDS population and time frame.
- [SEL] C07-02 total 48.5 | both | Weighing it up | public_explanation | risk medium | Every preventive treatment has a waiting time :: Screening tests and preventive medicines take time before they help anyone, while their inconvenience and side effects can start straight away, so how long a person is likely to live and what they want from that time belong in the decision. || accuracy: 'Before it helps anyone' overstates the concept: time to benefit is the time until a set absolute benefit is reached (Lee 2013 about 10 years to prevent 1 cancer death per 1000 screened; Yourman 2021 about 2.5 years to prevent 1 major cardiovascular event per 100 treated, in 50 to 75 year olds; Deardorff 2022 about 12 months to prevent 1 non-vertebral fracture per 100 women), and benefit builds gradually before that; trial populations were younger or fitter than many of the readers. || sharpen: Replace 'before it helps anyone' with 'before the benefit shows up in trials' and show only two or three verified examples, each with its outcome, denominator and trial age range written beside it.
- [SEL] C07-12 total 48.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | QRISK above 10% at 78 :: At older ages, age alone puts most people above the 10% QRISK threshold, so the threshold no longer separates who is likely to benefit; the statin decision then rests on time to benefit, other illnesses, frailty and the person's preferences. || accuracy: 'Almost every 78-year-old, whatever their cholesterol' needs documented QRISK3 outputs; for women with very favourable profiles the score may sit close to 10% in the mid seventies, so 'most' is safer unless verified. NG238 also allows statins below 10% by informed preference, so the threshold is not a strict gate in either direction. || sharpen: Build the chart from reproducible QRISK3 outputs with inputs listed, and name the three things to weigh instead: time to benefit, other illnesses and frailty, and what the person wants.
- [SEL] C07-07 total 48.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | Ask how many days a month a treatment will take :: The time a treatment takes, counted as days with appointments, tests, infusions, travel and waiting, is a real cost to older people and their families, and it can be asked about before agreeing. || accuracy: Contact-day evidence comes from cancer trials (for example the CO.17 secondary analysis); do not transfer its proportion of days to other treatments, and treat the dialysis schedule (three sessions a week of about four hours) as typical in-centre practice that varies with home and incremental dialysis. || sharpen: Give one verified contact-days figure from the cancer literature as the anchor and name the carer's time explicitly, keeping dialysis as a generic example only.
- [SEL] C07-17 total 48.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | When screening invitations stop, symptoms still need checking :: Routine screening invitations end at set ages, but a new symptom such as a lump, bleeding or a change in bowel habit needs a GP appointment at any age, and some screening can still be requested after the invitations stop. || accuracy: Programme ages differ by nation (England breast to the 71st birthday with self-referral after; England and Scotland bowel 50 to 74 with kits on request after); NHS and Cancer Research UK web pages could not be opened here, so verify ages, self-referral routes and the share of cancers diagnosed at 75 and over (about a third) from an opened document. || sharpen: Add one sentence that requesting screening after invitations stop is itself a choice to weigh with the GP, so the post separates 'report symptoms at any age' from 'keep screening' without pushing self-referral on everyone.
- [RES] C07-03 total 47.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Read the exclusion list before applying the target :: The main trial behind lower blood pressure targets excluded people living in care homes and people with dementia, so applying its target to them is an extrapolation that needs judgement. || accuracy: In the UK, NICE NG136 targets were not derived from SPRINT (NICE did not adopt the SPRINT target), so 'the trial behind lower blood pressure targets' fits US and European guidance, not NICE; SPRINT's standing-pressure exclusion was below 110 mmHg after 1 minute standing, and SPRINT used unattended automated measurement that reads lower than routine clinic measurement. || sharpen: Say which targets SPRINT informed, add the unattended-measurement point, and close on a practical test: would this person have been eligible for the trial, and what is their standing pressure?
- [RES] C07-06 total 47.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Count the days at home :: Days alive and at home after an operation or admission capture readmissions, rehabilitation stays and new care home moves that length of stay and 30-day mortality miss. || accuracy: DAH30 validation (Myles 2017) was in an Australian surgical cohort and home-time measures correlate with other outcomes; the scenario of a service keeping mortality low while patients spend much of the next month in hospital must stay an illustrative example, not a finding about any service. || sharpen: Tie it to a treatment decision (days at home as the figure to ask about before high-risk surgery) or move it to C20, and add one verified figure on how DAH30 tracks disability.
- [RES] C07-11 total 47.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | Will the doctor at 3am know what you would want? :: Wishes about emergency treatment help at night or at the weekend only if they are recorded where out-of-hours doctors, ambulance crews and hospitals can see them, such as a Key Information Summary in Scotland or a ReSPECT form. || accuracy: Which services can view the Key Information Summary needs an opened source (official web pages could not be opened here); Scottish terminology has moved from anticipatory care planning to future care planning, and ReSPECT is used in many but not all UK areas, so check current names and coverage. || sharpen: Use the current Scottish term alongside KIS and ReSPECT, and add one sentence on exactly what to ask the GP to record, whether the wish is for more treatment or less.
