- [   ] C07-18 total 47.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | How to talk about stopping screening :: Older adults in US studies were often open to stopping cancer screening but disliked being told they might not live long enough to benefit, preferring explanations based on their other health priorities and the balance of benefit and harm. || accuracy: Schoenborn 2017 was a qualitative study of about 40 US older adults; the phrasing preferences come from a separate US national survey (Schoenborn 2018, JAMA Oncology research letter), so quote its most and least preferred explanations exactly and state the US population. || sharpen: Show the survey's preferred and least-preferred phrasings verbatim on the card, and add a line that honest prognosis should still be offered when the person wants it.
- [   ] C07-24 total 47.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Dialysis or conservative kidney care :: For older people with kidney failure, conservative kidney management (care without dialysis that treats symptoms and protects remaining kidney function) is a recognised option alongside dialysis, and for some people with frailty or other serious illness dialysis adds little time while taking up a lot of it. || accuracy: Kurella Tamura 2009 studied US nursing home residents (about 58% had died and 13% had kept their previous function at 12 months), not older people generally; survival comparisons of dialysis and conservative care are observational and confounded, so 'adds little time' must become 'observational studies suggest smaller survival differences' in named groups. || sharpen: State the Kurella Tamura population in the same sentence as its figures, replace 'adds little time' with the observational finding for a named group, and list what conservative care actively provides.
- [   ] C07-01 total 46.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Ask what a normal day looks like three months after surgery :: Before a major operation, an older person can ask the surgical team to describe what everyday life is likely to look like during recovery, for example at three months, which a discussion of operative complications usually leaves out. || accuracy: The three-month framing needs its source stated: Lawrence 2004 found recovery of basic daily activities took about 3 months and of household and community activities about 6 months after major abdominal surgery in people aged 60 and over, not for all operations; Fried 2002 figures come from 226 seriously ill US patients and should carry that population. || sharpen: Anchor the three-month question to Lawrence 2004 and use it as the single public post on surgical consent, choosing it over C07-21 or C07-25 so the argument about recovery of daily life appears once per audience.
- [   ] C07-26 total 46.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Setting up a time-limited trial :: A time-limited trial is an agreement to give a treatment for a set time with named signs of improvement, a review date and a plan for either result, and it helps avoid both stopping too early and continuing without benefit. || accuracy: Chang 2021 was a before-and-after quality improvement study in three US ICUs in people with advanced medical illness, not a randomised trial and not specific to older people; the claim that time-limited trials prevent premature withdrawal is the rationale, not a measured finding. || sharpen: Open with the vague plan it replaces ('let's see how it goes') and label the Chang evidence as a US ICU before-and-after study in the caption.
- [   ] C07-10 total 45.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | Base the target on the standing reading :: NICE advises measuring standing as well as seated blood pressure in people with hypertension aged 80 and over, and treating to a target based on standing blood pressure when there is a significant postural drop. || accuracy: Check NG136 wording: the standing measurement is after at least 1 minute standing, a significant drop is a systolic fall of 20 mmHg or more, and treating to the standing reading applies to people with a postural drop or symptoms, not to everyone aged 80 and over; NICE web pages could not be opened here, so use an opened copy or the BMJ guideline summary. || sharpen: Open with the consequence (a seated reading above target in someone whose pressure falls on standing) and state the 20 mmHg definition and 1-minute timing on the flow diagram.
- [   ] C07-08 total 45.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Eating and drinking with acknowledged risks :: When swallowing becomes unsafe and treatment will not make it safe, continuing to eat and drink with the risks understood and planned for is a recognised choice made with the person, and nil by mouth or heavily modified diets carry harms of their own. || accuracy: Robbins 2008 compared chin-down posture with two thickened liquids and had no thin-liquid control, so it cannot show that thickened fluids fail to reduce pneumonia; say instead that evidence that thickened fluids prevent pneumonia is limited. The RCP 2021 careful hand feeding guidance centres on advanced dementia and progressive conditions. || sharpen: Replace the Robbins claim with 'there is little evidence that thickened fluids prevent pneumonia' and give families one request: a documented eating and drinking with acknowledged risks plan agreed with the speech and language therapist.
- [   ] C07-21 total 44.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Material risk for an older patient :: Under the Montgomery judgment, a material risk is one a reasonable person in the patient's position, or this particular patient, would consider significant, and for many older people that includes losing independence, a long recovery, or not returning home. || accuracy: The Montgomery materiality test (para 87) is quoted correctly, but McCulloch v Forth Valley Health Board [2023] UKSC 26 held that which alternatives are 'reasonable' is judged by the professional practice test, so the duty to discuss no treatment should rest on GMC guidance; 'falls short of what Montgomery requires' is a legal interpretation to soften. || sharpen: Soften the legal conclusion, cite McCulloch alongside Montgomery, and choose between this and C07-01 so the surgical-consent argument is not made twice.
- [   ] C07-22 total 44.0 | both | Weighing it up | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Stopping one blood pressure tablet after 80 :: In a UK trial of people aged 80 and over whose blood pressure was controlled on two or more medicines, stopping one kept blood pressure controlled at 12 weeks for most people, with results similar to usual care. || accuracy: Verify OPTIMISE figures: 569 randomised; systolic below 150 mmHg at 12 weeks in about 86% (reduction) versus 88% (usual care); about two thirds of the reduction group stayed on the reduced regimen; GPs selected participants they judged suitable for reduction, which limits generalisation. || sharpen: Open with the question families ask (do all these blood pressure tablets still need taking?) and put the 86 versus 88 in 100 figures, the two-thirds figure and the 12-week limit in consecutive sentences.
- [   ] C07-28 total 43.5 | public | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Muscle aches and statins: what a trial on each person found :: In a UK trial where people who had muscle symptoms on statins alternated statin and placebo without knowing which, symptoms were similar in both periods and most chose to restart; statins cause a small share of muscle symptoms, but most are not caused by the statin. || accuracy: StatinWISE used six blinded 2-month periods in random order, not alternating months; 200 were randomised and 151 completed, mean age about 69; general practice cannot routinely offer a blinded placebo, so the practical option is an unblinded stop and restart, which is a weaker test. || sharpen: Describe the design correctly, say that a blinded trial is not routinely available so an unblinded rechallenge is the practical version, and consider moving it to C06.
- [   ] C07-16 total 42.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Write down why the person said no :: When an older person declines a treatment, recording their reasons in their own words, checking what they understood, noting whether pain, fear, hearing or timing played a part, and offering to revisit respects the decision and catches reasons that can be addressed. || accuracy: The 'unwise decision' principle is section 1(4) of the Mental Capacity Act 2005 (England and Wales); the Adults with Incapacity (Scotland) Act 2000 principles are different (benefit, least restrictive option, past and present wishes, consultation, residual capacity), and the Scottish unwise-decision point comes from its Code of Practice, so do not attribute it to the Act's principles. || sharpen: Separate the English and Scottish legal bases correctly and use one example of a correctable factor (a hearing aid not in) to show how the fuller note protects both the decision and access to care.
- [   ] C07-09 total 41.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Open with what the person understands :: Structured serious illness conversations start by asking what the person understands about their health and how much they want to know, then share information, then discuss goals, worries and trade-offs. || accuracy: The Serious Illness Care Program trial was in US oncology outpatients and did not improve its primary outcomes (goal-concordant care and peacefulness); the reduction in anxiety and depression symptoms was a secondary outcome and should be labelled as such. || sharpen: Open with a concrete clinical moment rather than a principle, and state plainly that the trial missed its main outcomes, so the post teaches the sequence without overselling the guide.
- [   ] C07-14 total 41.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | Count the appointments before booking them :: For older people with several conditions, each follow-up appointment costs travel, waiting, energy and often a relative's time, so each should be justified by a decision it could change, and several can sometimes be combined. || accuracy: Quote NG56 accurately on assessing treatment burden and agreeing who coordinates care; links between treatment burden and poorer adherence or quality of life are observational and should be described as associations. || sharpen: Add one verified figure on appointment load in multimorbidity, and select either this or C07-07 rather than both, since they make the same time-burden argument for different audiences.
- [   ] C07-33 total 41.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | What the 3D trial found and did not find :: A large UK trial of patient-centred reviews for people with several long-term conditions did not improve quality of life scores, but did improve patients' reports of how person-centred and coordinated their care was. || accuracy: 3D trial: 1546 patients in 33 practices; no clear difference in EQ-5D-5L at 15 months; improved patient-centred care measures; only about half of intervention patients received the full planned reviews (check the process evaluation); do not suggest the quality of life measure was wrong without evidence. || sharpen: Name one patient-reported measure that improved and the proportion who received the full reviews, and consider C20 for it, since its main point is how to read a null trial.
- [   ] C07-13 total 40.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Can this choice be undone? :: Some treatment choices can be tried and stopped if they do not help, and others are hard or impossible to reverse; knowing which kind a decision is tells you how much time and information it needs. || accuracy: Some medicines should not be stopped abruptly (steroids, beta-blockers, antiepileptics, some antidepressants), so 'a medicine can usually be stopped and restarted' needs qualifying; the GMC end-of-life guidance dates from 2010 with a 2022 update. || sharpen: Give one concrete example in each column and a tapering caveat, or fold it into C07-26 as that post's public version.
- [   ] C07-19 total 40.5 | both | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk medium | At 80, how many years are left? :: Average life expectancy at 80 in the UK is close to a decade for women and a little less for men, and the range around the average is wide, so age alone is a poor basis for deciding someone is too old for a treatment. || accuracy: ONS period life expectancy at 80 is roughly 9 to 10 years for women and 8 to 9 for men, but the exact figures need an opened ONS source (ONS pages could not be opened here); the Walter and Covinsky quartiles come from 1990s US life tables and must not be merged with UK figures; the anti-ageism position is ethical judgement plus data and should be marked as judgement. || sharpen: Use current opened period figures, keep the dated US quartiles clearly separate, and close on a question to ask: which parts of my health, rather than my age, change this decision?
- [   ] C07-20 total 39.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Higher risk, bigger absolute benefit :: Because heart attacks and strokes are more common in older people, a treatment with a similar relative effect can prevent more events per 100 people treated at 80 than at 50, provided the person lives long enough to benefit and harms do not rise more. || accuracy: The 2021 BPLTTC individual participant meta-analysis appears to show relative effects that weaken with age, with no clear reduction in the 85 and over group (hazard ratio close to 1, wide uncertainty), so the 85 versus 55 example uses the age where the relative effect is least certain; CTT 2019 found a trend to smaller proportional reductions at older ages; HYVET's primary stroke reduction (about 30%) was of borderline significance, while death and heart failure fell clearly. || sharpen: Use the 75 to 84 group where relative effects held, say plainly that relative effects look smaller at the oldest ages, and correct the HYVET stroke claim before the arithmetic point can stand.
- [   ] C07-25 total 39.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Best case, worst case, most likely :: The Best Case/Worst Case tool helps surgeons describe high-risk operations to older people as scenarios of what life will look like in the best, worst and most likely outcomes, with and without surgery, so the person can choose according to what they value. || accuracy: Evidence for Best Case/Worst Case is mainly pre-post and simulation work in the US; check whether the multisite stepped-wedge trial has reported and what it found before saying the tool improves decisions. || sharpen: Fold it into C07-01 as the clinician-facing version, or verify the trial result first and lead with the drawn two-bar graphic.
- [   ] C07-15 total 38.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | The surgery reading and the home reading :: Blood pressure is often higher in clinic than at home, so NICE recommends home or 24-hour readings to confirm high blood pressure, and older people can ask whether their treatment is based on them. || accuracy: 'White-coat effect is common in older people' needs a specific source; 'treatment decisions are safer with home readings' is a reasonable inference, not a trial finding; state the NICE home method (paired readings twice daily for at least 4, ideally 7, days, discarding the first day) from an opened copy. || sharpen: Add a specifically geriatric point (the lower home target for people 80 and over and the postural drop), or drop it in favour of C07-10 to avoid a third blood pressure post.
- [   ] C07-31 total 38.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Patient Priorities Care: two questions :: Patient Priorities Care helps older people with several conditions name specific goals and the care they are willing and able to do, and clinicians then align treatment with them; in a non-randomised trial it was associated with lower perceived treatment burden and fewer unwanted treatments. || accuracy: Tinetti 2019 was a non-randomised comparison in one Connecticut health system; report which outcomes improved (treatment burden score, medicines or tasks stopped) and which did not change, and avoid implying generalisability to UK primary care. || sharpen: Put the two questions in plain words as the visual, state the non-randomised design and the outcomes that did not change, or merge with C07-14 as one treatment-burden post.
- [   ] C07-04 total 37.0 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | When you stop a treatment, write down why :: A decision to stop or withhold a treatment survives the handover to the next team only if the letter records the reason, what would justify restarting, and who will review it. || accuracy: Evidence that medicines stopped in hospital are restarted because the reason was not recorded is thin and may rest on local audits; Kripalani 2007 covers discharge communication deficits in general and is nearly 20 years old, so the causal chain (missing reason leads to restart) needs a direct source or should be presented as reasoning. || sharpen: Merge with C07-16 into one 'record the reason' teaching post, or move it to C06 or C15, and find a direct source on post-discharge restarting before relying on it.

## C08 Dementia: living with it and caring well (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 (03, 15, 06, 24, 29, 17, 22, 08, 10, 25, 21, 27, 07, 28) split 3 practitioners, 5 both and 6 public, which is a good spread. Two imbalances: medicines take 5 of the 14 (03, 15, 17, 08, 07), and no Care that works post makes the top 14 while Reading the evidence has 5. Consider swapping 07 or 17 for C08-26 (WHELD) or C08-04 (mealtimes) to add good care and daily practice. Driving (19), money (20) and dementia in hospital (09) sit in scope but fall below the top 14, mainly because official UK pages could not be opened; 09 is the strongest of these and could replace 28 if hospital care is wanted.
Judge allocation reason: 31 of 33 candidates reach 36 or more with no disqualifying flag and 22 reach 44 or more (note: the stated weights give a maximum of 55, not 50), so the category supports the 14 cap; weaker or out-of-scope items (C08-11, C08-14, C08-19, C08-20, C08-22, C08-23) could move to C09, C15, C17 or C19 if other categories need them.
- [SEL] C08-03 total 51.0 | practitioners | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Stopping donepezil because the dementia is now severe :: Severity alone is not a reason to stop a cholinesterase inhibitor such as donepezil; in the DOMINO-AD trial, stopping donepezil in moderate to severe Alzheimer's disease was followed by more care home moves in the first year, though not over the next three years. || accuracy: The care home finding comes from secondary and post hoc analyses, and 'roughly doubled the risk' turns a hazard ratio (2.09) into a times-more-likely statement, which house rules forbid; give the absolute proportions moved to a care home in year 1 by arm if the paper reports them, and state that the trial was in community-dwelling people with moderate to severe Alzheimer's disease. || sharpen: Replace the hazard ratio with absolute numbers per group moved to a care home in the first year, and add one line on when stopping is clearly right (intolerable side effects, slow pulse or faints, last weeks of life).
- [SEL] C08-15 total 50.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | The replacement drug: mirtazapine for agitation :: When doctors avoid antipsychotics for agitation in dementia, mirtazapine is sometimes used instead; in the UK SYMBAD trial it did no better than placebo at 12 weeks, and there were more deaths with mirtazapine (7 vs 1, of marginal statistical significance). || accuracy: The 7 vs 1 deaths was a post hoc comparison with p = 0.065 and small numbers, so it must read as uncertain, not as a proven harm; the claim that falls in antipsychotic prescribing come with rises in other sedating drugs needs its own source or should be cut; confirm the care setting of SYMBAD participants. || sharpen: Keep the mortality panel but give the plain reading ('too few deaths to be sure, and no benefit to set against them'), and either source the substitution pattern or drop it.
- [SEL] C08-06 total 49.0 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | The trial where people's own goals improved and the standard scores did not :: In the GREAT trial, cognitive rehabilitation at home helped people with early dementia make large gains on everyday goals they chose themselves, while quality of life, mood and cognition scores showed no difference. || accuracy: Goal attainment was rated by participants and carers who knew their allocation, and therapy directly targeted those goals, so a large gain on them with no change on general scales is partly expected; state this rather than implying the standard scales failed; verify the d values (0.97, 0.94) and who rated them. || sharpen: Name two verified goal types from the trial and add one sentence on why self-rated goals in an unblinded trial need cautious reading; that makes the 'what gets counted' argument more credible, not less.
- [SEL] C08-24 total 49.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Exercise in dementia: fitter, but not a treatment for memory :: In the UK DAPA trial of 494 people with mild to moderate dementia, four months of moderate to high intensity exercise improved fitness but did not slow cognitive decline; cognitive scores in the exercise group were slightly worse at 12 months, by an amount too small to notice. || accuracy: Check that DAPA is the 'largest UK' trial before saying so; the 1.4-point ADAS-cog difference should be read as no benefit and possibly slight harm, not proven harm; the secondary outcomes need verifying. || sharpen: Say 'a large UK trial' unless 'largest' is verified, and close with a positive instruction: offer exercise for strength, balance and enjoyment, with a named way to find a programme.
- [SEL] C08-29 total 48.5 | both | Who gets left out | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Who the new Alzheimer's antibody drugs were tested on :: When the lecanemab trial's rules were applied to older people with early Alzheimer's disease in a US population study, only 19 of 237 (about 8 in 100) would have been eligible, mainly because of other health conditions and brain scan findings. || accuracy: The Mayo Clinic sample was 97.5% White and from one US county; trial eligibility differs from licensed or clinical eligibility (UK labels differ, for example by ApoE4 status); CDR-SB and ARIA figures must be quoted exactly from Clarity AD. || sharpen: Keep the funnel and add a plain reading of what the exclusions were (other illnesses, scan findings), closing with a question an older person can ask: would someone like me have been in the trial?
- [SEL] C08-17 total 48.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Before an antipsychotic, ask about Lewy body disease :: People with dementia with Lewy bodies or Parkinson's disease dementia can have severe reactions to antipsychotics, so before prescribing one, ask about visual hallucinations, fluctuating alertness, acting out dreams in sleep, and slowness or stiffness. || accuracy: McKeith 1992 is a small, older study, so give its sensitivity reaction frequency exactly or describe it in words; the four-question checklist is a prompt, not a validated screen, and hallucinations and parkinsonism also occur in other dementias; NICE allows cautious antipsychotic use in Lewy body disease with specialist input. || sharpen: Keep the dream-enactment opening and word the footer as 'any yes: consider Lewy body disease and seek specialist advice before an antipsychotic', quoting the NICE caution exactly.
- [SEL] C08-08 total 47.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Dementia tablets and a slow pulse :: Donepezil, rivastigmine and galantamine can slow the heart and cause fainting; in a large Ontario study, people with dementia taking them had more hospital visits for fainting, slow heart rate and pacemaker insertion, so new dizziness, faints or falls should prompt a pulse check. || accuracy: Gill 2009 is observational with possible confounding by indication; rates are per 1000 person-years and must be translated accurately; confirm the age range of the Ontario cohort; the hip fracture association must not be written as caused by the drug. || sharpen: Frame the pulse check as a question to ask at review ('Has anyone checked the pulse since the tablet started?') and credit the SmPC for the pharmacology, keeping the cohort data as a link, not a cause.
- [SEL] C08-10 total 47.5 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Count the doctors: continuity of GP care in dementia :: In an English study of 9,324 people with dementia, those who saw the same GP most consistently had fewer medicines, fewer potentially inappropriate prescriptions, less incontinence and fewer emergency admissions over a year; the study shows a link, not proof of cause. || accuracy: Observational with plausible reverse causation (people with more complex needs see more GPs); the incontinence (OR 0.42) and admission (OR 0.90) results are odds ratios and must not become 'X times less likely'; the claim that practices rarely measure continuity has no source; in England every patient already has a named accountable GP, so the request should be to see the same GP. || sharpen: Change the family action to 'ask to book with the same GP each time and keep a note of who you saw', and lead the visual with the absolute medicine counts rather than the odds ratios.
- [SEL] C08-22 total 47.5 | public | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | What families should be told to expect in advanced dementia :: In a US study of 323 care home residents with advanced dementia, most developed eating problems and many had fevers or pneumonia over 18 months, and residents whose families understood this expected course were much less likely to have burdensome treatments near the end of life. || accuracy: The adjusted OR 0.12 is an association in a US nursing home cohort; the angle's 'knowing what to expect changed the care people received' is causal and must become 'was linked with'; UK applicability must be stated. || sharpen: Open on the verified eating-problem probability (about 86 in 100 over 18 months) and close with the question families can ask: what do you expect to happen next, and what will you offer when it does?
- [SEL] C08-21 total 47.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | If someone with dementia goes missing, report it at once :: There is no rule that a person must be missing for 24 hours before police will take a report, and for people with dementia longer time missing is linked with harm, so report quickly and keep a prepared form with a recent photo and places they may head for. || accuracy: The 'no 24-hour rule' needs an opened police document or a published source; the figures (281 incidents, 5% significant harm, 0.5% of the regional dementia population) must be checked against Bantry White and Montgomery 2015, which covers one UK region; check whether guidance says 999 or 101. || sharpen: Keep the myth-correction opening, bring 'most people are found unharmed' forward to avoid alarm, and name the Herbert Protocol with a note that local names differ.
- [RES] C08-25 total 47.0 | public | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Music and dementia: what it helps and what it does not :: A 2025 Cochrane review of 30 trials found that music-based therapy probably improves depressive symptoms slightly and may reduce overall behavioural problems at the end of treatment, but probably does not reduce agitation or aggression, and no lasting effect was shown after sessions ended. || accuracy: The review covers structured music-based interventions of at least five sessions, mostly in institutions, so it does not describe casual listening at home; certainty levels must be quoted exactly from the 2025 update. || sharpen: Add one line on what was tested (structured sessions, mostly in care homes) so readers do not apply it to the radio, and end with how to use music for enjoyment and mood.
- [RES] C08-27 total 47.0 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | When a person with dementia asks for someone who has died :: When a person with dementia asks for a parent or partner who died long ago, repeating the news can bring fresh grief each time; a UK inquiry set out a range of responses, from gentle truth to responding to the feeling behind the question, and asked that any choice serve the person. || accuracy: The inquiry's range of responses and its recommendations must be quoted from the Mental Health Foundation 2016 report itself; the claim that repeated news of a death causes fresh grief needs a source. || sharpen: Keep the opening question and make the test 'what would help this person now?' the centre of the visual, with one clearly labelled illustrative example of answering the feeling behind the question.
- [RES] C08-07 total 45.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Antipsychotics in dementia: the numbers both ways :: For severe agitation, aggression or psychosis in dementia, antipsychotics help some people a modest amount on average, and in trials about 1 more person in 100 died over 10 to 12 weeks than with placebo, so they are for severe distress or risk of harm, short term, with review. || accuracy: Schneider 2005 gives 3.5% vs 2.3% deaths (about 1 extra in 100 over 10 to 12 weeks) in trial populations that differ from many people treated in practice; a verified absolute benefit figure is hard to derive from standardised effect sizes, so the icon array must not imply a measured benefit count; the stroke claim needs a specific source. || sharpen: If no absolute benefit figure can be verified, describe benefit in words (small on average, some people clearly better), keep the icon array to deaths only, and end on the two questions families can ask.
- [   ] C08-16 total 45.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Antidepressants for depression in dementia: what the largest UK trial found :: In the HTA-SADD trial, sertraline and mirtazapine were no better than placebo for depression in Alzheimer's disease and caused more adverse reactions, and NICE advises against routinely offering antidepressants for mild to moderate depression in mild to moderate dementia. || accuracy: The Cochrane certainty grading must be quoted exactly; the NICE recommendation covers mild to moderate depression in mild to moderate dementia only, so the post must not imply antidepressants are never appropriate, for example in severe depression or a pre-existing mental illness. || sharpen: Separate it from C08-15 by leading on the NICE recommendation and a verified non-drug first step, and state where an antidepressant may still be reasonable.
- [   ] C08-23 total 45.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Feeding tubes in advanced dementia: benefits and burdens :: For people with advanced dementia who are eating less, tube feeding has not been shown to prolong life, prevent aspiration pneumonia or heal pressure sores, and careful help to eat by hand for comfort is the recommended approach. || accuracy: The evidence is observational (no randomised trials), so 'has not been shown' is correct and 'no benefit' would not be; cite the current Cochrane version; the AGS statement is US, and the current UK RCP guidance wording needs checking. || sharpen: Separate it from C08-22 by focusing on the single decision at the hospital moment, and add the sourced burdens (restraint, displacement, hospital visits) to the comparison card.
- [   ] C08-28 total 45.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Behind distressed behaviour: boredom and loneliness :: In a study of care home residents with dementia and behaviour problems, the most common unmet needs were boredom or lack of stimulation, loneliness and need for company, and lack of meaningful activity, so a plan for distress should include company and occupation. || accuracy: A single US study of 89 residents in six Maryland nursing homes; unmet needs were assessed by researchers and are associated with behaviour, not proven causes; the claim about time without activity needs a source. || sharpen: Add the scale of the finding (about three unmet needs per resident) and state explicitly that this question comes after physical causes are checked, then give the care-plan question.
- [   ] C08-01 total 45.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | One antipsychotic prescription, followed for 12 weeks :: When an antipsychotic is started for severe distress in a person with dementia, the plan should name the target symptom, the checks in the first fortnight (drowsiness, stiffness, slower walking, falls) and a review within six weeks at which stopping is the first question. || accuracy: NICE NG97 supports lowest dose for shortest time and reassessment at least every 6 weeks, but the week 1 to 2 checks and the week 12 checkpoint are the post's own structure and must be labelled as suggested practice, not guidance; the Cochrane withdrawal review (Van Leeuwen 2018) is low certainty and mostly excludes people with severe symptoms, so 'can be withdrawn without worsening' needs that qualifier. || sharpen: Separate it clearly from C08-07 and C08-17 by making it the drug-chart version: a one-line template (target symptom, dose, review date, plan to stop) that a prescriber can copy.
- [   ] C08-09 total 45.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Home or a care home: name both sets of risks :: When a care home is proposed for a person with dementia in hospital, both options carry risks and benefits, and the person's own wishes and legal rights belong in the discussion alongside falls and safety. || accuracy: Harrison 2017 describes 100 people in one Scottish setting (verify); the claim that discharge guidance advises against long-term care decisions from acute beds needs an opened official document; the legal principles differ between England and Wales (MCA 2005) and Scotland (AWI 2000) and must be stated separately. || sharpen: Use the verified finding that there were no established standards for these decisions, and close with a specific request: ask whether the long-term decision can wait until after the acute stay.
- [   ] C08-11 total 45.0 | practitioners | Who gets left out | teaching | risk medium | When a person with dementia has nobody to speak for them :: In England and Wales, when a person who lacks capacity faces a serious medical treatment decision or a long-term move and has no family or friend to consult, the NHS body or local authority must instruct an Independent Mental Capacity Advocate. || accuracy: The IMCA duty applies to serious medical treatment and long-term accommodation (hospital stays over 28 days, care home stays over 8 weeks) in England and Wales only; the Scottish s259 right of access to advocacy is not a duty to instruct for specific decisions; drop the variation-in-rates claim unless sourced. || sharpen: Open on the practical trigger (nobody to consult, not counting paid carers) and add the accommodation time thresholds so staff know when the duty starts.
- [   ] C08-04 total 44.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Eating difficulty in dementia is often not a swallowing problem :: When a person with dementia stops eating well, the difficulty may be at any stage of a meal (noticing the food, recognising it, getting it to the mouth, chewing, or swallowing), and each stage needs a different response. || accuracy: The five-stage meal model is a teaching synthesis, not a validated staging; the claim that swallowing problems predominate in later stages has no located source (Kai 2015 not found); the post must not imply that swallow referral is wrong when there is coughing, choking or chest infection. || sharpen: Add one explicit line on when a swallow assessment is still needed (coughing, choking, wet voice, chest infections), so watching the meal reads as the first step rather than a substitute for dysphagia assessment.
- [   ] C08-26 total 44.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | What worked in 69 care homes: training, activity and a medicines review :: In the WHELD trial in 69 UK nursing homes, training staff in person-centred care and social interaction, with an antipsychotic review, improved residents' quality of life, agitation and other symptoms by small amounts and cost less than usual care. || accuracy: Effect sizes (0.23 to 0.30) are small and quality of life was proxy-rated (DEMQOL-Proxy); about a third of randomised residents did not complete 9 months; verify the cost result and its uncertainty. || sharpen: Translate the small effect sizes into plain words and name the one component most homes could start with, so managers get a first step, not only a trial summary.
- [   ] C08-31 total 44.0 | public | Care that works | public_explanation | risk medium | When the person insists nothing is wrong :: Reduced awareness of one's own difficulties is a common feature of Alzheimer's disease and comes from the illness itself, so arguing about the diagnosis rarely helps, and support can be arranged around the task rather than the label. || accuracy: Reduced awareness is multifactorial (neurological, psychological, social), so 'comes from the illness itself' overstates a single mechanism; prevalence varies widely by method; the communication advice is carer guidance, not trial evidence, and must be labelled as such. || sharpen: Say 'can come from the illness itself', give one verified prevalence range, and offer the task-based approach explicitly as practical advice.
- [   ] C08-18 total 43.5 | public | Ask this | good_care | risk low | Ask about cognitive stimulation groups :: NICE recommends offering group cognitive stimulation therapy to people with mild to moderate dementia, and trials of the 14-session programme found benefits for thinking and quality of life. || accuracy: The 2023 Cochrane review gives lower certainty for quality of life than for cognition, so 'benefits for thinking and quality of life' needs qualifying; availability varies by area and 'often available' needs a source or should be dropped. || sharpen: State the size of the cognitive benefit in plain words, give the programme format from Spector 2003 (14 sessions over 7 weeks), and say plainly that access varies by area.
- [   ] C08-32 total 43.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Coloured plates and a small study :: In a small study of nine men with advanced Alzheimer's disease, changing from white to high-contrast red plates and cups was followed by more eating and much more drinking, a follow-up found high-contrast blue worked too, and low-contrast colours did not. || accuracy: Nine men in one unit; the 25% and 84% increases are means from a tiny sample and invite overreading; replication has not been checked; the study size should appear in the first line. || sharpen: Search for later replications; if none, drop the percentages from the visual and keep 'ate and drank more in a study of 9 people' with the 'try it and note intake before and after' instruction.
- [   ] C08-20 total 42.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk high | Two sums of money a dementia diagnosis can unlock :: In Great Britain, a person with dementia may qualify for Attendance Allowance, which does not depend on income or savings, and for a council tax discount or exemption for severe mental impairment, but both have to be claimed. || accuracy: In Scotland, Pension Age Disability Payment has replaced Attendance Allowance for new claims, so a 'Great Britain' framing is wrong for Scottish readers; gov.uk pages could not be opened and rates change each April; the severe mental impairment disregard needs a doctor's certificate plus a qualifying benefit, and the 25% discount applies only in particular household situations. || sharpen: Build it on primary legislation (Social Security Contributions and Benefits Act 1992 s64; Local Government Finance Act 1992 Sch 1) with separate lines for England and Wales and for Scotland, and leave out weekly rates.
- [   ] C08-12 total 41.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | Whose rating counts: the person or the family? :: When people with dementia and their family carers rate the person's quality of life, the ratings often differ, and family ratings are shaped by the carer's own strain and the person's mood, so a proxy rating should not replace asking the person. || accuracy: The direction of the self versus proxy difference needs confirming in full text; in advanced dementia self-report is often not possible, so proxies are sometimes the only option; note the tension with C08-26, whose WHELD primary outcome was proxy-rated. || sharpen: Give one verified direction-and-size finding and a concrete rule (report self and proxy ratings separately, never merge them) so the post ends on an instruction, not only a critique.
- [   ] C08-19 total 40.0 | public | Weighing it up | public_explanation | risk medium | Driving after a dementia diagnosis: the law and the plan :: In Great Britain, a person diagnosed with dementia must tell the DVLA; the diagnosis does not always mean stopping at once, but because giving up driving is linked with depression and reduced social life, a plan for getting about should be made before the last drive. || accuracy: DVLA and gov.uk pages could not be opened here, so the legal duty and the fine amount need an opened official document or a published paper; Northern Ireland uses the DVA; Chihuri 2016 pools observational studies, so the depression figure is a link, not a cause. || sharpen: Lead with the less-known facts (a diagnosis does not always mean stopping at once; the insurer must also be told) and make the 'list the trips that count and how each will happen' plan the centre of the visual.
- [   ] C08-02 total 39.5 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Dementia care designed around a carer who is not there :: Many people with dementia live alone, and some have no family carer at all, yet trials and services often require or assume a carer, so the evidence and the support fit them least well. || accuracy: The Eichler 2016 figures (51% living alone, 9% with no carer) come from a German primary care cohort and need checking in full text; do not generalise them to the UK without a UK figure; the Miranda-Castillo unmet-needs claim is unverified. || sharpen: Lead with a verified figure for how often dementia trials require a study partner, then give the single test question for any service plan: who does this step if nobody else lives there?
- [   ] C08-14 total 39.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Who at home knows the medicines have changed? :: When a person with dementia leaves hospital, medicine changes recorded in the discharge letter may not reach the person who fills the pill organiser, so families should ask for the changes in writing and ask whether the community pharmacy has been told. || accuracy: Evidence is not dementia-specific; the NHS Discharge Medicines Service is England only and its page could not be opened; Nazar 2016 is observational and its readmission finding needs checking; Scotland and Wales have different arrangements. || sharpen: Anchor it in a verified figure on medicine discrepancies after discharge in older people, keep the two exact questions, and say the pharmacy referral route differs by nation.
- [   ] C08-05 total 36.5 | public | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | What 'living well with dementia' is linked with :: In a large meta-analysis, better quality of life in dementia was linked with relationships, social engagement and daily function, and poorer quality of life with depression and poorer physical health, with mostly small associations, so a memory score says little about how well someone is living. || accuracy: Figures must come from the corrected version (2020 corrigendum); the 'gain' line that life after diagnosis depends on things people can work on is a causal reading of correlational data; the draft opening has an unverified N and no reader action. || sharpen: Open on the concrete finding that memory scores had only a weak link with quality of life, and close with one supportable action, such as asking for mood and physical health to be reviewed at follow-up, not only memory.
- [   ] C08-33 total 36.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | A year of support after diagnosis: Scotland's standard and its reach :: Scotland's standard says people newly diagnosed with dementia should receive at least a year of post-diagnostic support from a named link worker, and the published measure counts how many of the estimated new diagnoses were referred, which shows how far the support reaches. || accuracy: Public Health Scotland referral and completion percentages are unverified and the official publication may not be openable here; the estimated-cases denominator must be explained in plain words. || sharpen: Lead with the entitlement for people in Scotland (a named link worker for at least a year) and add the verified reach figure with its denominator and year.
- [   ] C08-30 total 35.0 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Activities matched to what a person can still do :: Activities matched to a person's past interests and current abilities, and set up so they can succeed, can reduce distress and ease carers' days; a pilot trial of an occupational therapy approach, the Tailored Activity Program, found fewer behavioural symptoms at home. || accuracy: The pilot had 60 families and a wait-list control; later, larger trials had mixed results that the core claim does not reflect; the NICE wording on tailored activities needs checking. || sharpen: Either find and state the result of a larger Tailored Activity Program trial or reframe it as practical advice, clearly labelled, and consider merging its core point into C08-28.
- [   ] C08-13 total 32.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | What people with dementia want services to measure :: When UK researchers asked people living with dementia, carers and professionals which outcomes are important for community support, the 54 outcome items fell into four areas: managing dementia symptoms, quality of life, a friendly neighbourhood and home, and independence. || accuracy: The final core outcome set (Reilly 2020) was not found, so only the Harding 2019 long-list domains are verified; the contrast claim that services report mainly activity measures needs a source. || sharpen: Either verify the final core outcome set and name two specific items, or fold the neighbourhood-and-home point into C08-06 or C08-12 as supporting material.

## C09 Communication, capacity and supported decision-making (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 by score are practitioner-heavy (9 practitioner, 3 public: C09-11, C09-09, C09-02; 2 both: C09-22, C09-27), against a brief that asks this category to address older people directly. For a balanced 14, swap C09-20 (better placed in C20), C09-23 (no older-person angle) and one of C09-01 or C09-12 for public posts C09-15, C09-24 and C09-10 (or C09-16 or C09-29), giving roughly 6 practitioner, 6 public and 2 both. Avoid pairing C09-14 with C09-26 (both lean on Sessums 2011), C09-13 with C09-31, or C09-03 with C09-30. Most legal posts rest on the England and Wales Mental Capacity Act; every one needs an accurate Scottish line, and at least one Scotland-specific post (C09-06 or C09-22) should be included.
Judge allocation reason: All 32 candidates clear about 36 with no disqualifying flag and 22 score 44 or more; capacity and supported decision-making is absent from the earlier collection, so the category can take the maximum without repetition.
- [SEL] C09-26 total 49.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | An MMSE score is not a capacity assessment :: Cognitive screening scores help only at the extremes: in a systematic review, MMSE scores of 20 to 24 did not change the likelihood that a patient lacked capacity, so a decision-specific assessment is always needed. || accuracy: Verify the MMSE bands and likelihood ratios against the Sessums abstract (cut-points are quoted differently in secondary sources); a high score lowers but does not rule out incapacity, especially with executive impairment. || sharpen: Add that even a score above 24 does not rule out incapacity for a complex decision, linking to observed function.
- [SEL] C09-18 total 48.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Elderspeak and refusal of care :: Talking to older people with dementia in a sing-song, 'sweetheart', 'shall we' style is linked with more resistance to care, and a three-session staff training course reduced both in a cluster randomised trial in nursing homes. || accuracy: Verify CHAT effect sizes and whether the fall in resistiveness (not only elderspeak) persisted at 3 months; Williams 2009 is observational sequence analysis in 20 residents, so 'more likely after' is association. || sharpen: Lead with the ordinary-kindness framing so staff recognise their phrases without feeling accused, and give only verified CHAT figures.
- [SEL] C09-28 total 48.5 | practitioners | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Covert medicines: a decision with rules :: Giving medicines hidden in food or drink to someone who lacks capacity to refuse them can be justified, but only after a capacity assessment, a best-interests decision involving the prescriber, a pharmacist and family, a written plan for each medicine and a set review date. || accuracy: NICE SC1 wording on who attends the best-interests discussion must be quoted exactly; in Scotland the Mental Welfare Commission guide requires AWI authority (s47 certificate with a covert plan); the NICE and CQC pages were not openable, so a published paper or opened document is needed. || sharpen: Give the Scottish route alongside NICE SC1 and keep 'stop the medicines not worth hiding' prominent.
- [SEL] C09-08 total 48.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Assess the decision against the options that exist :: When assessing someone's capacity to decide whether to go home, they must be given the realistic options, including the support that would be in place, because a person can only weigh what they have been told. || accuracy: Baker J's wording in CC v KK must be quoted from the judgment, not paraphrased; it is a first-instance England and Wales judgment; Foulkes 2024 studied acquired brain injury, not older people. || sharpen: Quote the judgment's words and add that the care home option must also be described honestly, including its own drawbacks.
- [SEL] C09-11 total 48.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | A welfare power of attorney waits until you are unable to decide :: A health and welfare power of attorney (England and Wales) or welfare power of attorney (Scotland) can be used only for decisions you are unable to make yourself; while you can decide, the decision is yours and staff should ask you. || accuracy: Check the AWI subsection (s16(5)) and include the 'attorney reasonably believes' limb in both jurisdictions; OPG guidance pages were not openable, so rely on statute text. || sharpen: Add what to say if staff turn to the attorney first ('She can decide this; please ask her'), and keep the financial-powers footnote.
- [SEL] C09-09 total 47.0 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Going with a parent to an appointment, without taking over :: When you go with an older relative to an appointment, you help most by prompting them to speak and asking their opinion; in a US study, patients whose companions did this were more likely to take an active part in decisions. || accuracy: Clayman 2005 is a cross-sectional US study of 93 patients, so companion behaviours are linked with, not shown to cause, patient involvement; 'clinicians turn to the companion' needs its own source. || sharpen: State the association plainly and add one before-the-appointment step, such as agreeing with your parent which questions they want to ask themselves.
- [SEL] C09-12 total 46.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Ask them to show you as well as tell you :: Some people, often with impairment of planning and self-monitoring (executive function), can describe how they would manage at home yet are unable to do it; for decisions about living at home, watching a real task, often with an occupational therapist, shows whether the person can use what they know. || accuracy: 'Frontal lobe paradox' is a loosely used, contested label and the listed sources are uncertain; the case law on 'executive capacity' needs a named judgment. || sharpen: Source 'executive capacity' to a named Court of Protection judgment or the 39 Essex Chambers guidance note rather than the contested label, and keep the step of putting the observations to the person.
- [SEL] C09-23 total 46.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Teach-back: what the newest review found :: A 2026 meta-analysis of 18 randomised trials found no clear effect of teach-back (asking patients to explain back what they have been told) on their knowledge, with low to very low certainty evidence of better confidence and short-term adherence. || accuracy: The trials were not in older people specifically; 'no clear pattern' at very low certainty is an evidence gap, not evidence that teach-back fails; the NHS recommendation source is unidentified. || sharpen: Add the older-patient angle (recall falls with age; teach-back as a check in decision conversations) and keep 'not shown' rather than 'does not work'.
- [SEL] C09-01 total 46.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | A capacity finding has a decision and a date :: A finding that someone lacks capacity applies to one decision at one time, so an old finding copied into a new admission, referral or care plan needs a fresh assessment before anyone relies on it. || accuracy: The Scotland paragraph must describe AWI correctly (incapacity is decision-specific under s1(6); s47 certificates are treatment-specific and time-limited); the claim that records drift into 'lacks capacity' as a label is an observation to phrase as a risk, not a finding. || sharpen: Give the exact wording to write ('Lacks capacity to decide X; assessed by [role] on [date]; review when Y') and add the Scottish s47 equivalent.
- [SEL] C09-32 total 46.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Running an interpreted consultation for a big decision :: For a significant conversation with someone who has limited English, book a professional interpreter, brief them first, allow extra time, look at the patient and speak to them as 'you', and give information in short sections; professional interpreters are associated with better communication and care than ad hoc ones. || accuracy: Flores 2012 error rates need verifying before any number is used; Karliner 2007 is not specific to older people; NHS England 2018 guidance applies to primary care commissioning in England. || sharpen: Add the older-person angle (hearing loss alongside an interpreter; cognitive tests through interpreters) and verify any error-rate number.
- [RES] C09-02 total 45.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | Ask for the clinic letter to be written to you :: You can ask for your hospital clinic letter to be written to you in plain language, with a copy to your GP, and national guidance from the Academy of Medical Royal Colleges recommends this. || accuracy: 'Entitled to ask' overstates guidance: the Academy of Medical Royal Colleges recommends patient-addressed letters but this is not a legal right; the Moorfields figures (about 2 in 100 addressed to patients) come from one eye hospital and must not be generalised to all clinics. || sharpen: Say you can ask for a letter written to you or a copy of the GP letter, keep the Moorfields figure labelled as one eye hospital, and add that you can say if you would rather letters did not come to your home.
- [RES] C09-22 total 45.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Talking Mats: a low-tech way to include people with dementia :: Talking Mats, a picture-based framework developed in Scotland, helped people with dementia and their family carers feel more involved and more satisfied in discussions about daily living than their usual way of talking. || accuracy: Evidence is one small within-couple comparison (18 couples), not randomised or long-term; 'cheap' and 'low-tech' are uncosted, and Talking Mats is a licensed resource that needs training, so naming it risks reading as endorsement. || sharpen: Say how people get access (training, cost) in neutral terms and keep the 18-couple limitation in the caption.
- [RES] C09-27 total 45.5 | both | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk medium | Deaf patients who use BSL need an interpreter, not a notepad :: For many Deaf people who use British Sign Language, English is a second language, so written notes are an unreliable substitute for a qualified BSL interpreter in health conversations. || accuracy: The BSL Acts recognise the language but do not themselves create a right to an interpreter; the duty comes from the Equality Act 2010 and, in England, the Accessible Information Standard; Emond 2015 and SignHealth details need verifying. || sharpen: Retitle to avoid the 'X, not Y' shape and name the Equality Act as the basis for asking.
- [   ] C09-20 total 45.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Hearing loss is missing from communication research :: A systematic review found that fewer than a quarter of studies of doctors' communication with older patients mentioned hearing loss at all, although hearing loss affects most people over 80. || accuracy: The review covers studies from 2000 to about 2016; the 'most people over 80' prevalence figure must come from a primary US survey, not the review's introduction. || sharpen: Add the date limitation and one bedside habit (ask 'Can you hear me clearly?' and use an amplifier before any decision conversation); consider moving to C20 if that category is short.
- [   ] C09-10 total 45.0 | public | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Keep talking to someone who is dying :: Some people who are dying and no longer respond may still hear; a small EEG study found brain responses to sound in unresponsive hospice patients, so saying who you are and explaining care before touching is worth continuing. || accuracy: The number of unresponsive patients was small and must be given; whether 'most' showed mismatch responses needs checking; brain responses to tones are not evidence of hearing words or understanding. Possible overlap with C19 candidates. || sharpen: Give the exact number of unresponsive patients, keep the 'what it did not test' panel, and add that relatives who did not talk have not failed anyone.
- [   ] C09-15 total 44.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | Ask: capacity to make which decision? :: If you are told your relative 'lacks capacity', ask which decision this refers to; capacity is judged decision by decision, and the person may still be able to make many other choices, which they should be helped to make. || sharpen: Add a concrete example of the everyday decisions the person still makes after a finding about where to live, and the follow-up question 'What help will she get to make the others?'
- [   ] C09-24 total 44.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | When you are asked what she would want :: If a doctor asks what your relative would want, you are being asked about their wishes and values; unless you hold legal authority, such as a welfare power of attorney, the treatment decision rests with the clinical team, not with you. || accuracy: GMC wording that relatives should not be given the impression they decide is unverified; Scotland's route (s47, consultation with any welfare attorney or guardian) differs; Wendler and Rid are mostly US studies. || sharpen: Add the Scottish route and invite relatives to bring what the person said, in the person's own words.
- [   ] C09-29 total 44.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Two kinds of advance document :: In England and Wales, an advance statement of wishes guides decisions but is not binding, while a valid and applicable advance decision to refuse treatment is legally binding, and refusing life-sustaining treatment needs particular formalities; Scotland's law differs. || accuracy: Scotland's position must be phrased carefully (no statute; advance directives must be taken into account under AWI principles and are likely to be treated as binding at common law, but untested); an advance decision can refuse but never demand treatment. || sharpen: Write the Scottish paragraph carefully and add giving copies to the GP and family so the document is found when needed.
- [   ] C09-17 total 44.0 | both | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk low | Remembering the decision later is a different question :: In England and Wales a person needs to retain the relevant information only long enough to make the decision, so forgetting the conversation afterwards does not by itself mean they lacked capacity; notes and reminders can help them hold on to it. || accuracy: For decisions carried out day after day (medicines, living at home), inability to retain can bear on using and weighing, so forgetting is not always irrelevant; the Code paragraph on memory aids needs checking. || sharpen: Add one line that memory can matter for decisions that must be carried out repeatedly, and move the post to Teaching round or Ask this rather than Who gets left out.
- [   ] C09-16 total 44.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | When you recover, the decisions come back to you :: If decisions were made for you while you were too unwell to make them, they become yours again once you recover, and you can ask what was decided, why, and whether it still stands. || accuracy: A DNACPR decision based on a clinical judgement that CPR would not work is not the recovered person's to reverse, though they can discuss it and seek a second opinion; the GMC wording is unverified; 'nobody routinely hands these back' is unsupported. || sharpen: Separate decisions that return to the person (a stopped medicine, a care home referral) from resuscitation decisions, and phrase the hand-back gap as a question to ask rather than a finding.
- [   ] C09-19 total 44.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | What helps people with Alzheimer's follow what you say :: For people with Alzheimer's disease, short simple sentences and repeating or rephrasing helped understanding in small studies, while speaking more slowly on its own did not. || accuracy: The studies are small and old (15 people in 1997; 18 couples in 2003); 'slow speech did not help on its own' must not become 'slow speech is wrong'; for people who are also hard of hearing, clear speech (often slower) helps, which C09-04 relies on. || sharpen: State the sample sizes in the post and reconcile with hearing advice so the two posts do not contradict each other.
- [   ] C09-25 total 44.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Relatives predict treatment wishes correctly about two times in three :: In a systematic review, relatives and chosen proxies predicted patients' treatment preferences correctly about two times in three, and having discussed the preferences beforehand did not improve accuracy. || accuracy: Shalowitz 2006 used hypothetical scenarios in mostly US studies; some later structured advance care planning trials improved patient and surrogate agreement, so 'prior discussion did not improve accuracy' applies to the observational studies reviewed, not to structured planning. || sharpen: Add that structured planning conversations improved agreement in some later trials, so the message is 'write it down and talk it through'.
- [   ] C09-30 total 42.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Before assessing capacity, remove the obstacles :: The law requires all practicable steps to help someone decide before concluding they are unable to; a short routine (hearing aid in, glasses on, pain treated, right time of day, interpreter, quiet room, key points written, a trusted person present, enough time) covers most of them, and recording which were used shows it was done. || accuracy: 'Doing them changes the result for some people' is unsupported; Dupuis 2015 (sensory loss and MoCA scores) is uncertain and concerns cognitive tests, not capacity findings. || sharpen: Cut the checklist to five or six items for phone legibility and support or remove the 'changes the result' claim.
- [   ] C09-31 total 42.5 | practitioners | Weighing it up | criticism | risk medium | A capacitous refusal is where the work starts :: When an older person with capacity refuses help at home, the refusal should be respected, and the work continues: exploring the reasons, offering different help, checking whether they can put their decisions into practice, and staying in contact; reviews of serious harm from self-neglect have repeatedly found cases closed too early. || accuracy: SAR thematic findings need exact wording from Preston-Shoot; Care Act and SARs are England-specific; the House of Lords report is from 2014; 'the case is often closed' is a generalisation. || sharpen: Quote the SAR findings exactly and add the Scottish equivalent (Adult Support and Protection) so Scottish readers are included.
- [   ] C09-21 total 42.5 | practitioners | Who gets left out | teaching | risk medium | Aphasia and capacity assessment :: People with aphasia after a stroke have difficulty with language, which is different from difficulty with judgement, and many can take part in decisions when information is given in a form they can use; speech and language therapists can help, but are often not asked. || accuracy: Aphasia after stroke can coexist with cognitive impairment, so 'keep their judgement' must be 'may keep'; aphasia prevalence and Jayes 2026 survey details need verifying. || sharpen: Change the opening to 'may keep their judgement' and give one action: ask the speech and language therapist before the capacity conversation.
- [   ] C09-14 total 42.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Incapacity goes unnoticed when people agree :: Across inpatient studies, about a quarter of medical inpatients lacked capacity for treatment decisions and clinicians recognised it in fewer than half; people who agree to what is proposed are the least likely to be checked. || accuracy: 'People who agree are least likely to be checked' has no identified source; the Raymont recognition figure must be verified; 'consent forms signed by people unable to consent' is an inference; these inpatient samples include acute illness. || sharpen: Replace the unsourced agreement claim with the verified recognition figure and say that the presumption of capacity still stands.
- [   ] C09-04 total 41.5 | both | Small detail, real effect | good_care | risk low | At night, the hearing aids are out :: To speak to someone at night who has taken out their hearing aids, put light on your own face, get level and close, say their name first and use fewer words; for anything more than a quick check, offer the hearing aids and glasses first. || accuracy: Supporting studies are old and tiny (Picheny 1985, a handful of listeners; Sumby and Pollack 1954 in noise, not at night); 'raising the voice distorts speech' needs a named source; prevalence figures are US survey data. || sharpen: Cut the laboratory studies to one sourced line, open with the four actions, and say that waking someone to put aids in is for conversations that need an answer.
- [   ] C09-05 total 41.5 | both | What gets counted | public_explanation | risk medium | Recorded as large print, sent in small print :: In England, NHS and social care services must identify, record, flag, share and meet a person's information and communication needs; the flag is where the duty starts, and you can ask for letters and information in a format you can use. || accuracy: England only; the date and content of the revised standard are unverified; the link from unmet formats to missed appointments is asserted without a source; NHS England pages were not openable in this project. || sharpen: Anchor the claim to the current published version of the standard and one published figure on unmet formats, and give the exact request wording ('Please send my letters in 20-point print, as recorded').
- [   ] C09-06 total 41.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Moved to a care home without lawful authority :: When an adult who lacks capacity to decide where to live moves from hospital to a care home, someone needs lawful authority for that move; a Mental Welfare Commission for Scotland review of moves in spring 2020 found some had none. || accuracy: The count of unlawful moves and its denominator must be verified from the report; the review covers March to May 2020 only and practice may have changed; the report is on the Commission site, which may not be openable here. || sharpen: Give the verified count with its denominator, say plainly that the review covered the first pandemic months, and end with the three questions at the care home door.
- [   ] C09-03 total 40.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | First impressions in a capacity assessment :: How a person looks, sounds or behaves at first meeting tells you little about their capacity for a particular decision; the law in England and Wales says so, and research shows staff skip communication screening when a person seems fine. || accuracy: Jayes 2020 is a small case series (4 of 8 unscreened patients), too thin for a headline; the 'social facade' source is unidentified; Sessums recognition figure overlaps C09-14 and C09-26. || sharpen: Drop the case-series numbers from the headline and lead with the two-way statute point and one concrete pair of examples (a fluent person unable to weigh, a deaf person fully able).
- [   ] C09-07 total 40.0 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | A short visit leaves little time for choice :: Offering choices and asking consent during personal care takes time, especially for people with dementia or hearing loss, so very short home care visits push care workers towards doing things to a person rather than with them. || accuracy: NICE NG21 applies in England and its exact wording on visit length must be quoted; the prevalence of 15-minute visits has no identified published source; the link from short visits to loss of choice is argument, not a measured finding. || sharpen: Quote the NG21 recommendation exactly, drop the 15-minute prevalence claim unless a published figure is found, and add what a family can ask the provider for.
- [   ] C09-13 total 40.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Agree what would reopen the decision :: When an older person with capacity chooses to live with a risk, a useful plan records what they understood, what would make the risk smaller and which specific changes would prompt everyone to talk again. || accuracy: The review-trigger method is good practice drawn from guidance, not a tested intervention; the 2007 Department of Health guide is old and England-specific. || sharpen: Address the older person directly ('If you choose to stay at home...'), show a worked trigger list, and say the method is good practice rather than trial-tested.

## C10 Pain (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: Top 14 by score: 7 public (12, 06, 30, 09, 25, 03, 16), 3 both (10, 15, 27), 4 practitioners (22, 26, 13, 18), which is a good audience spread. The weakness is topic: 10 of the 14 are about medicines (two gabapentinoid posts, 06 and 26, need spacing), knee and hip arthritis recurs (15, 16, 30), and no 'Care that works' or 'Who gets left out' post makes the top 14. Consider swapping one medicine post (18 or 13) for C10-17 (ESCAPE-pain) or C10-21 (pain on movement) to include a non-drug approach, and pick only one of C10-04 and C10-14 if either is used.
Judge allocation reason: Fourteen candidates score 44 or more and 29 of 31 clear about 36 with no disqualifying flag, so the category can fill the maximum with strong, well-sourced ideas; the tail (C10-01, 02, 07, 08, 20) is weaker or overlaps.
- [SEL] C10-12 total 48.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Order strong painkillers before the weekend :: UK law lets a pharmacist make an emergency supply of many prescription medicines at a patient's request, but not of most controlled drugs, which include strong opioid tablets and patches, tramadol, gabapentin and pregabalin, so people taking them should order repeats well before they run out, especially before weekends and public holidays. || accuracy: Schedule 5 preparations (co-codamol, dihydrocodeine, low-strength oral morphine solution) can be supplied in an emergency, so 'most strong painkillers' must be precise; Scottish and English urgent-supply schemes still follow the controlled drug restriction but should be checked; legislation and MHRA pages may not be openable here. || sharpen: Verify the Schedule 5 exceptions and the four-nation position, and close with a concrete step: note the run-out date when the prescription or discharge supply is given.
- [SEL] C10-06 total 47.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | Swollen ankles after a nerve pain tablet :: Ankle swelling is a recognised side effect of gabapentin and pregabalin, and studies of older adults found loop diuretics (water tablets) started more often after these medicines than before, a sign that the swelling is sometimes treated with a second drug instead of a review of the first. || accuracy: A sequence symmetry ratio of 1.23 shows a modest excess at population level and does not show that any one person's swelling is drug-caused; ankle swelling has many causes (heart failure, amlodipine, venous disease) that still need assessment; the Read 2021 lead is unverified. || sharpen: Keep the sequence ratio out of the public caption, say 'some' rather than 'often', and add what the prescriber might do instead (lower the dose, stop, or look for another cause).
- [SEL] C10-10 total 47.0 | both | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | Liquid morphine: check the strength on the bottle :: Oral morphine liquid is supplied in a standard strength and a concentrated strength that holds [N to verify] times as much morphine in each millilitre, so doses should be written and checked in milligrams as well as millilitres, and families giving it at home should know which strength they have. || accuracy: Strengths (10 mg in 5 ml and 100 mg in 5 ml, a tenfold difference) must be verified from the BNF or SPC; NPSA RRR05 (2008) covers opioid dosing errors generally and may be archived, so a published paper on concentrate errors would strengthen the claim. || sharpen: Put the verified 'ten times' in the headline and give families one question to ask at every new bottle: which strength is this?
- [SEL] C10-22 total 47.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Leg pain that eases when sitting :: In older adults with leg pain on walking, having no pain when seated and relief on bending forward (for example leaning on a shopping trolley) make lumbar spinal stenosis much more likely, which helps separate it from circulation problems and hip arthritis. || accuracy: Lumbar spinal stenosis and peripheral arterial disease often coexist in older people, so the history points towards a cause but does not exclude the other; pulses or ankle brachial pressure still need checking; likelihood ratios must not be turned into 'times more likely'. || sharpen: Add that circulation problems and stenosis often coexist, so pulses are still checked, and keep the likelihood ratios as 'strong pointers' in plain words.
- [SEL] C10-30 total 47.0 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Knee replacement: ask about pain afterwards :: Knee replacement relieves pain for most people, but in the best-quality studies about 1 in 5 reported troublesome pain in the joint months to years later (fewer after hip replacement), so the decision should include an open discussion of that chance and of non-surgical options first. || accuracy: 'Unfavourable long-term pain outcome' was defined differently across studies; the about 1 in 5 figure is for knee in the best-quality studies; the post must also say most people get substantial relief so it does not discourage surgery that would help. || sharpen: Pair the 1 in 5 with the fact that most people get good relief, and give the two questions to ask the surgeon.
- [SEL] C10-09 total 46.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Paracetamol in more packets than one :: Paracetamol is an ingredient of co-codamol, co-dydramol and many cold and flu remedies, so taking one of these with plain paracetamol can exceed the safe daily dose; in a Scottish liver unit series, people whose excess doses built up over time were older than those with single overdoses, and pain relief was the commonest reason. || accuracy: Craig 2012 studied all adults admitted with severe paracetamol liver injury, not older people, and 'older' there is relative within that series; the post must not imply that double-dosing in older people commonly causes liver failure; the BNF caution for adults under 50 kg needs exact wording. || sharpen: State that the Scottish series was of adults of all ages, and add the low body weight caution that applies to many frail older people.
