- [SEL] C10-25 total 46.0 | public | Weighing it up | public_explanation | risk medium | Morphine near the end of life and the fear of shortening it :: Families sometimes worry that morphine given near death will shorten life; a review of palliative care studies found that doses rise as death approaches because symptoms change, but no evidence that starting or increasing opioids adjusted to symptoms brings death forward, so this fear should not stop relief of pain or breathlessness. || accuracy: The evidence is observational; 'studies have not found that doses adjusted to symptoms shorten life' is supportable, 'morphine does not shorten life' is not; Thorns 2000 and Portenoy 2006 details are unverified. || sharpen: Keep 'studies have not found' wording, and give families the exact question to ask the nurse when they think a dose is needed; coordinate with C19-19.
- [SEL] C10-03 total 45.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Night cramps and quinine: a small benefit with rare serious harms :: Quinine reduces the number of night-time leg cramps only modestly and can rarely cause serious harm, so UK advice is to use it only when cramps are frequent and disrupt sleep, after other causes have been looked for and stretching has been tried, and to stop it if a trial has not helped. || accuracy: The Cochrane figures (28% fewer cramps, 10% lower intensity) are relative and need absolute numbers; the stretching claim rests on one small trial (Hallegraeff 2012) and a Cochrane review of non-drug treatments for cramps found the evidence insufficient; the MHRA 2010 advice may not be openable here, so a published source repeating it is needed. || sharpen: Give the quinine effect as absolute numbers of cramps over two weeks, and present stretching as a low-risk thing to try rather than a proven treatment.
- [SEL] C10-15 total 45.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Paracetamol for knee and hip arthritis: what the trials found :: In a Cochrane review of 10 trials, paracetamol reduced hip and knee arthritis pain by only about 3 points on a 0 to 100 scale more than a dummy tablet, well below the change people usually notice, and NICE no longer recommends it routinely for osteoarthritis, so stopping it when it is not clearly helping is reasonable. || accuracy: The findings apply to knee and hip OA only and must not be read as 'paracetamol does not work' for acute pain or fractures; abnormal liver tests were more common with paracetamol but of uncertain importance, so no liver-harm claim should be made. || sharpen: Keep the scope in the first line (knee and hip arthritis), show the 3-point gain against the 9-point noticeable change on one drawn scale, and coordinate with C10-01, which uses the same review.
- [SEL] C10-26 total 45.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Pregabalin for sciatica: what the trial found :: In a placebo-controlled trial, pregabalin did not reduce sciatica leg pain more than placebo at 8 weeks or at 1 year and caused more side effects, dizziness most often, and NICE advises against gabapentin and pregabalin for sciatica; in older people dizziness and drowsiness also raise the risk of falls. || accuracy: PRECISE enrolled adults with a mean age in the mid-50s, so the application to older people rests on side-effect profiles and should be stated; the NICE NG59 recommendation covers sciatica, not all neuropathic pain. || sharpen: State the trial participants' ages and add what to offer instead for sciatica; space it from C10-06.
- [RES] C10-16 total 45.5 | public | What gets counted | public_explanation | risk low | The knee X-ray is a poor guide to the pain :: Knee X-ray findings match pain poorly: many people with arthritis on the X-ray have little pain and many with knee pain have near-normal X-rays, so NICE advises diagnosing osteoarthritis from symptoms in people aged 45 and over without routine imaging, and an X-ray should not decide how much a person can do. || accuracy: Bedson and Croft give very wide ranges that depend on definitions, and severe radiographic change does relate to pain on average; the post should say the X-ray is a poor guide, not no guide, and imaging still has a role in atypical cases and surgical planning. || sharpen: Add one line on when imaging does help (unusual features, planning surgery), and give the person a question to ask instead of 'what does the X-ray show?'.
- [RES] C10-13 total 44.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Count the as-required doses before writing the discharge opioid :: The number of as-required opioid doses a person took in the 24 hours before discharge shows how much opioid they still need, yet in a large US surgical study more than a third of patients took none in that period and almost half of those were still prescribed opioids to take home. || accuracy: Chen 2018 studied surgical patients with a mean age of 52, so the extension to older medical inpatients is judgement; zero as-required doses in hospital does not rule out need at home when activity increases. || sharpen: State the surgical, mean-age-52 population in the caption, and present 'none used' as a prompt to ask about pain at home rather than an automatic no.
- [RES] C10-18 total 44.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Tramadol is more than a weak opioid :: Tramadol also raises serotonin and noradrenaline, so in older people it brings risks that other opioids pose less (serotonin toxicity with some antidepressants, seizures, low sodium, low blood sugar); in UK primary care data, starting tramadol was linked with about twice the rate of hospital admission for low sodium compared with starting codeine. || accuracy: The hyponatraemia hazard ratio has a wide interval (1.08 to 3.86) from few events, so give the absolute rates (4.6 vs 1.9 per 10,000 person-months) alongside 'about twice'; the hypoglycaemia paper is a nested case-control study with a different design and effect size and is marked unsure. || sharpen: Give absolute rates beside the relative one, and turn the footer into a concrete pre-prescribing check: antidepressant list, sodium, seizure history.
- [   ] C10-27 total 44.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Treat the insomnia as well as the pain :: In older adults with arthritis pain and insomnia, telephone CBT for insomnia improved sleep and tiredness for a year while its effect on pain was small and did not last, so insomnia deserves treatment in its own right rather than being left until the pain is controlled. || accuracy: The trial compared CBT-I with education, not with sleeping tablets, so 'rather than a sedative' is judgement and must be marked as such; the same trial is used by C11-09. || sharpen: Coordinate with C11-09 so only one post uses McCurry 2021, and mark the sedative comparison as judgement or remove it.
- [   ] C10-19 total 43.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Reduced kidney function changes the opioid :: Morphine is broken down into active products that the kidneys clear, so when kidney function is reduced they build up over days and can cause drowsiness, muscle twitching (myoclonus), hallucinations and slow breathing; the dose, the interval or the choice of opioid should change. || accuracy: Oxycodone also accumulates in renal impairment, so the alternatives named should be limited to those the cited guidance supports (fentanyl, alfentanil, with advice); King 2011 PMID is unverified. || sharpen: Open with the observable sign (new twitching or drowsiness a few days into morphine) rather than the pharmacology, and keep the eGFR caveat for people with little muscle.
- [   ] C10-21 total 42.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk medium | Score the pain when moving :: In arthritis, after fractures and after surgery, pain is often worst on moving, turning or walking, so a pain score taken while the person lies still can look low while pain stops them getting up; asking about pain during the activities they need to do gives a truer picture. || accuracy: Most evidence on movement-evoked pain comes from postsurgical trials in adults of all ages; the claim that it relates more closely to function should cite Corbett 2019 or Srikandarajah 2011 specifically; the ANZCA reference is unverified. || sharpen: Give a concrete recording example (a 'pain on standing' line beside 'pain at rest') and link it to the therapy session the person is about to have.
- [   ] C10-11 total 41.5 | both | Who gets left out | evidence_interpretation | risk low | In pain, visited, and lonely :: In the English Longitudinal Study of Ageing, people with persistent musculoskeletal pain were more likely to be lonely yet less likely to be socially isolated than people without pain, so a person in pain who has visitors may still be lonely and is worth asking directly. || accuracy: Both odds ratios sit close to 1 (loneliness 1.15, CI 1.01 to 1.31; isolation 0.87, CI 0.75 to 0.99), the analysis is cross-sectional, and mean age was 65.8 with participants from age 50, so the 'lonely but less isolated' paradox is fragile and must not be stated as pain making people lonely. || sharpen: Describe the finding as a modest link from one survey and lead with the practical question ('Do you feel lonely?') rather than the paradox.
- [   ] C10-17 total 41.0 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | A group class for knee and hip pain :: ESCAPE-pain, a group programme that combines exercise with plain explanation of arthritis and ways of coping, improved function and pain in UK trials for people with chronic knee pain, and in one trial cost less than usual outpatient physiotherapy for similar results. || accuracy: The cost comparison comes from one trial of 64 people (Jessep 2009); Hurley 2007 details and the hip version's evidence are unverified; the effect on pain was smaller than on function. || sharpen: Lead with what happens in a session and the verified 12 sessions over 6 weeks, and present the cost result as from one small trial.
- [   ] C10-05 total 41.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk low | Ask where else it hurts :: Many older people with persistent pain hurt in more than one place, and in a Boston cohort pain in two or more sites was linked with a higher rate of falls than pain in one site, so a pain assessment should record every site, for example on a body outline the person marks. || accuracy: Leveille 2009 is observational; the adjusted rate ratio of 1.53 compares multisite pain with no pain, not with single-site pain, so the multisite versus single-site contrast should be given only as the age-adjusted rates (1.18 vs 0.90 per person-year); there is no evidence that recording all sites reduces falls. || sharpen: Report the falls link as rates per person per year with 'linked with', and say what an extra site should change (for example foot pain prompting footwear or podiatry review).
- [   ] C10-24 total 41.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Coming down from a long-term opioid :: Many people on long-term opioids fear their pain will worsen if the dose is reduced, yet studies of dose reduction (mostly low quality) more often reported improved pain, function and quality of life; reduction works best when it is slow, agreed with the person and supported, because abrupt or forced stopping carries its own risks. || accuracy: Frank 2017 rated the evidence very low quality, so 'more often reported improved pain' must say so plainly; Agnoli 2021 is observational; the SPACE trial excluded most older adults; any tapering rate is unverified. || sharpen: Say in plain words that the studies were small and weak, and keep both sides equal in length: pain often does not worsen with slow reduction, and fast or forced reduction carries real risk.
- [   ] C10-04 total 40.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Ibuprofen for arthritis: more relief, more risk :: For older people with arthritis, anti-inflammatory tablets (NSAIDs) relieve pain more than paracetamol but carry more risk to the stomach, kidneys, heart and blood pressure, so the choice should be made openly: gel first where it fits, then the lowest dose for the shortest time with stomach protection and checks, and avoided by people for whom the risks are highest. || accuracy: da Costa 2017 pooled adult OA trials and was not an older-adult analysis; Solomon 2010 is observational with confounding by indication, so 'refusing NSAIDs leaves people on opioids with more harm' must stay as a link, not a cause. || sharpen: Lead with a concrete dilemma and drop the gel strand so it does not repeat C10-14, keeping this post on the oral NSAID decision and its checks.
- [   ] C10-14 total 40.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | A gel before tablets for a painful knee :: For knee arthritis, NICE recommends an anti-inflammatory gel as the first medicine to offer; rubbed into the skin over the joint it eases pain for some people while much less of the drug reaches the bloodstream than with tablets. || accuracy: NICE NG226 places the topical NSAID alongside core treatments (exercise, weight, information), not as a stand-alone first step; the Cochrane NNT is [N to verify]; the 'much less reaches the bloodstream' claim needs the pharmacokinetic source (Kienzler 2010) verified. || sharpen: Replace the NICE opening with a concrete one about the knee and the tube of gel, give the verified number needed to treat in plain words, and coordinate with C10-04 so only one post carries the gel point.
- [   ] C10-31 total 40.0 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Psychological treatments for pain help in later life too :: A meta-analysis of 22 trials in older adults (mean age about 72) found that psychological treatments for persistent pain, mostly based on CBT, gave small benefits in pain, in unhelpful thoughts that pain will be unbearable, and in confidence in managing pain, with group sessions working best, so age is no reason to leave them off the list. || accuracy: Effects were small (standardised mean differences about 0.18) and physical function did not clearly improve; 'group sessions worked best' is a subgroup finding and should be hedged. || sharpen: Lead with the fear ('A referral to psychology does not mean your pain is imagined') and give the size of benefit in plain words.
- [   ] C10-23 total 40.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Ask about the shingles vaccine :: Shingles becomes more common with age and can leave nerve pain (postherpetic neuralgia) that lasts months after the rash; in trials in people aged 70 and over, the recombinant shingles vaccine prevented about 9 in 10 cases of shingles and of postherpetic neuralgia, and it is offered free on the NHS to people in eligible groups. || accuracy: UK eligibility has changed in phases and differs by nation, and official UKHSA and Public Health Scotland pages were not openable here, so the eligibility statement lacks an opened source; 9 in 10 is relative efficacy and needs an absolute figure. || sharpen: Verify current eligibility in each UK nation from an opened source and give the absolute number of cases prevented per 1,000 vaccinated beside '9 in 10'.
- [   ] C10-28 total 39.5 | public | What gets counted | public_explanation | risk medium | Say what the pain stops you doing :: A pain score records how much it hurts but not what it costs a person, so telling the clinician which activities pain has stopped (walking to the shop, sleeping through, getting into the bath, seeing friends) gives them a goal to treat towards and a way to judge whether treatment has worked. || accuracy: Fayaz 2016's 62% figure for people 75 and over is a pooled estimate from few age-stratified studies; the under-reporting claim rests on small qualitative work (Gammons and Caswell 2014, unverified). || sharpen: Keep it as a public preparation card and differentiate it from C10-01 and C10-21, which make the goal and function point for clinicians.
- [   ] C10-29 total 38.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | More pain, more opioids, in poorer areas :: In England, chronic pain is more common and more severe in more deprived areas, and opioid prescribing is higher there, so people with the fewest resources may be more often managed with medicines that help persistent pain least. || accuracy: Mordecai 2018 is area-level (ecological) data, so 'people with the fewest resources are more often managed with opioids' is an individual-level inference it cannot support; Todd 2018 details are unverified; 'medicines that help least' overstates the evidence. || sharpen: Verify Todd 2018, keep the area-level caveat, and replace 'medicines that help least' with 'limited benefit for long-term pain'.
- [   ] C10-02 total 37.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | The first two days on a new strong painkiller :: When an older person starts an opioid, gabapentin or pregabalin, side effects such as drowsiness, unsteadiness, sickness and constipation can appear within days, so the family should ask before leaving which changes should make them call, and which number to ring. || accuracy: The 'first two days' window is unsupported: constipation and accumulation effects can take longer to appear, and the early-fall-risk studies are observational; the bowel threshold is still [N to verify]; the gabapentinoid respiratory depression warnings (MHRA 2017 and 2019) need exact dates and wording. || sharpen: Drop the two-day frame unless a source supports it, name the medicines in the first line, and give the call-back signs with a verified bowel threshold.
- [   ] C10-01 total 36.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | The trial average and the person in front of you :: Trials report the average benefit of a painkiller across many people and do not show whether one particular person will benefit, so a new medicine for persistent pain is best started as a planned personal trial with an agreed goal, a review date and a plan to stop it if the goal is not met. || accuracy: A trial average does not show hidden large responders, and a one-person trial is also moved by natural fluctuation and regression to the mean; the post should not imply that a person who feels better on a drug has proved it works. The Opioids Aware wording on trials and the Abdulla 2013 PMID are unverified. || sharpen: Open with a concrete situation (a tablet for knee pain started with no review date) and give the goal, review date and stop rule in the first lines rather than a statement about trial averages.
- [   ] C10-08 total 36.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | criticism | risk low | Counting pain scores did not improve pain care :: When a US health system required a 0 to 10 pain score at every visit, a before-and-after review in one clinic found no improvement in how pain was assessed or treated, and many patients with substantial pain received no new treatment, so services should count what happens after a high score, not only whether a score was recorded. || accuracy: Single US clinic, 600 visits, published 2006; 'no new therapy at that visit' does not equal undertreatment, since some people were already on a suitable plan; applicability to UK wards and care homes now is uncertain. || sharpen: Reframe the finding as 'no documented plan after a high score', state the single-clinic 2006 limits in the post, and add one concrete audit question a ward can use.
- [   ] C10-07 total 35.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | The ache in the first weeks of exercise :: Exercise reduces knee and hip arthritis pain over weeks, and some aching when starting is common and usually harmless, so knowing in advance what level of discomfort is acceptable, and how long the benefit takes, helps people keep going long enough to benefit. || accuracy: The pain-monitoring model cited (Thomee 1997) came from young women with patellofemoral pain, not older adults with OA; the NICE NG226 wording on discomfort and the benefit timescale are unverified; the 'settles by next morning' rule needs a source in older adults. || sharpen: Anchor the acceptable-ache rule to a programme or trial in older adults with OA (for example ESCAPE-pain materials) and give a verified timescale, or merge with C10-17.
- [   ] C10-20 total 35.0 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Offer a word scale as well as numbers :: Some older people find a 0 to 10 pain score hard to use and give more consistent answers on a verbal scale (no pain, mild, moderate, severe), and UK guidance on assessing pain in older people advises choosing a scale the person can use and keeping to it. || accuracy: The claim that verbal scales give 'steadier' answers is a reliability claim that Herr 2004 and Closs 2004 may not support as phrased (they mainly report completion and preference); the large-print advice attributed to Schofield 2018 is unverified. || sharpen: Frame it as choosing a scale the person can use and keeping to it, and consider merging with C10-21 into one assessment post.

## C11 Sleep (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 by score are practitioner-heavy: 6 practitioner (C11-21, C11-11, C11-22, C11-02, C11-29, C11-15), 5 both (C11-16, C11-03, C11-23, C11-28, C11-07) and only 3 public (C11-13, C11-10, C11-24). To keep substantial public coverage, swap one of the nocturia pair and C11-25 or C11-08 for public candidates C11-27 (care home nights), C11-05 (stopping after years) or C11-26 (carer's nights). No 'Who gets left out' post is in the top 14, so C11-09 or C11-26 would add that series. The sleeping-tablet cluster (C11-16, C11-03, C11-23, then C11-25, C11-05, C11-12) should be capped at three or four.
Judge allocation reason: 30 of 31 candidates clear the ~36 bar (only C11-19 is near it at 37; note that the stated formula's true maximum is 55, not 50) and about 18 distinct candidates score 44.5 or more with no disqualifying flag, so sleep supports the maximum; fill it by taking one from each overlapping pair (C11-11 or C11-18; C11-15 or C11-29) and no more than three or four sleeping-tablet posts.
- [SEL] C11-16 total 53.5 | both | Weighing it up | evidence_interpretation | risk low | About twenty-five minutes a night :: In trials in people over 60, sleeping tablets added on average about 25 minutes of sleep a night, while memory and thinking side effects and daytime tiredness were more common. || accuracy: Glass 2005 pooled trials of at least five nights (short-term use) published 1966 to 2003. The 25.2 minutes is a subjective total sleep time. Do not turn the ORs (cognitive events 4.78, fatigue 3.82) into 'times more likely'. Psychomotor events were reported as P>0.05, so 'unsteadiness' needs care. Use NNT 13 and NNH 6 only after confirming them in the full text. || sharpen: Set the 25 minutes against NNT and NNH icon arrays taken from the full text, and say the trials were short, so longer-term benefit is less certain.
- [SEL] C11-21 total 53.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Acting out dreams :: Shouting, punching or kicking during dreams in later life can be REM sleep behaviour disorder, which is strongly linked with later Parkinson's disease or dementia with Lewy bodies; it deserves assessment, bedroom safety and a careful conversation. || accuracy: Postuma 2019 drew on 24 specialist sleep centres: polysomnography-confirmed idiopathic RBD, 82.5% men, mean age 66. Conversion covers Parkinson's disease, DLB and MSA. Dream enactment reported in the community will predict less. Sleep apnoea can mimic RBD. || sharpen: Keep the Ask, Assess, Protect, Talk card and say that the 73.5% at 12 years comes from specialist-centre patients, so it is not a personal prognosis for everyone who acts out dreams.
- [SEL] C11-03 total 52.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | The leaflet that changed the decision :: In two Quebec trials, sending older people clear written information about benzodiazepine risks and a tapering plan led far more of them to stop than usual care. || accuracy: EMPOWER: 27% versus 5% had discontinued at 6 months (risk difference 23 points, NNT 4), with dose reduction in a further 11%; the outcome was pharmacy refill records, not confirmed abstinence. D-PRESCRIBE sedative-hypnotic subgroup: 63/146 (43.2%) versus 14/155 (9.0%); 29 of 77 (38%) who tapered reported withdrawal symptoms. Both were Quebec community-pharmacy cluster trials. || sharpen: Show the 27 versus 5 in 100 icon arrays, say that 62 in 100 recipients raised it with their doctor or pharmacist themselves, and close with what an older reader can ask their pharmacist for.
- [SEL] C11-13 total 50.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Hearing aids out, smoke alarm on :: In a sleep study of adults with hearing loss, a standard high-pitched smoke alarm woke only about half, while a lower-pitched alarm or a bed or pillow shaker woke far more; flashing strobes woke few. || accuracy: The participants were 38 adults aged 18 to 80 with mild to moderately severe hearing loss, tested in slow-wave sleep in a laboratory, so this is not an older-adult sample. What fire services offer varies by area. Check whether 520 Hz alarms are available in the UK before telling people to buy one. || sharpen: Say plainly who was studied, add one concrete UK route (a fire and rescue home fire safety visit), and consider moving it to C14 if the sleep category is crowded.
- [SEL] C11-11 total 50.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | The sleep hygiene booklet was the comparison :: Sleep hygiene advice on its own is weak treatment for chronic insomnia; the parts that work are stimulus control, sleep restriction and full CBT for insomnia. || accuracy: HABIT's mean age was 55.4 (range 19 to 88), so it is not an older-adult trial. ISI at 6 months was 10.9 versus 13.9. The AASM advice against sleep hygiene alone is a conditional recommendation. || sharpen: Bring in Buysse 2011 (mean age 71.7, response 67% versus 25%) as the older-adult evidence and run this or C11-18, not both.
- [SEL] C11-22 total 50.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Restless legs: iron and the medicines list first :: Before treating restless legs in an older person, check iron stores and look for medicines and habits that worsen it; dopamine agonists are no longer suggested for standard use because of worsening over time. || accuracy: The AASM 2025 iron thresholds (ferritin 75 ng/mL or lower, or transferrin saturation below 20%) are consensus that has 'not been empirically tested'. The guideline also conditionally suggests opioids. It is a US guideline, and NICE CKS may still list dopamine agonists, so say where UK practice differs. || sharpen: Open on the surprising point that a ferritin reported as normal can still be low for restless legs, and label it clearly as a 2025 US guideline.
- [SEL] C11-23 total 50.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Benzodiazepines and dementia: two studies side by side :: A Quebec case-control study linked benzodiazepine use with Alzheimer's disease, but a Seattle cohort found no rise in risk with heavier use, so the dementia link is uncertain; the case for reducing them rests on better-established harms. || accuracy: Billioti de Gage 2014: adjusted OR 1.51 (1.43 after adjusting for anxiety, depression and insomnia). Gray 2016: HR 1.07 (0.82 to 1.39) at the highest use, and the most recent year of use was excluded to limit reverse causation. Several meta-analyses of observational studies report a pooled association; leaving them out would look like choosing two studies to fit. || sharpen: Say that pooled observational studies show an association, explain reverse causation in one plain sentence, and keep the do-not-stop-suddenly instruction.
- [SEL] C11-28 total 48.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Treating insomnia and later depression :: In a trial of older adults with insomnia, CBT for insomnia roughly halved new or recurrent major depression over three years compared with sleep education. || accuracy: Irwin 2022: 12.2% versus 25.9% (HR 0.51). The protocol was amended to extend follow-up from 24 to 36 months, and only 52% to 57% completed 36 months. At 24 months completion was 73% (CBT-I) versus 87% (sleep education). Single site, 83% White, and people with a major health event in the past year were excluded. || sharpen: Show the 12 versus 26 in 100 icon arrays over 'up to three years', and name the uneven drop-out in the limitation strip.
- [SEL] C11-10 total 48.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Your CPAP machine goes to hospital with you :: If you use CPAP for sleep apnoea, take the machine, mask, tubing and power lead into hospital, including for planned surgery, and tell staff you use it. || accuracy: The SASM 2018 intraoperative guideline has not been read. The recommendation to continue CPAP around surgery is in the ASA 2014 and SASM 2016 guidance, which should be opened before citing. UK hospitals may require an electrical safety check of personal devices. || sharpen: Add 'ask whether the ward needs to safety-check your machine' and cover emergency admissions, where a relative may need to bring the machine in.
- [SEL] C11-02 total 47.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | The 2am sleep call: read the drug chart times first :: Before prescribing a sleeping tablet at night, check whether the timing of existing medicines explains the sleeplessness or waking. || accuracy: The donepezil SPC advises evening dosing. Moving the dose to the morning when sleep is disturbed is common practice but needs a citable source. The BNF and SPCs were not openable, so the diuretic, steroid and donepezil timing claims need published sources. Parkinson's overnight wearing-off advice must match the NG71 wording. || sharpen: Lead with the annotated drug chart and keep only the two or three timing fixes with citable sources; say that moving a dose is a plan for tomorrow night, while tonight's call is for pain, bladder and noise.
- [RES] C11-07 total 47.5 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | The care home sleep trial that changed the days :: A five-day non-drug programme in nursing homes changed night-time sleep measures very little but reduced daytime sleeping and increased social and physical activity. || accuracy: The intervention ran for five days, in four Los Angeles nursing homes; residents were 90% non-Hispanic white, and the trial dates from 2005. The only night-time effect was a small change in the length of awakenings (10.6 to 9.8 minutes versus 9.8 to 13.8 in controls), which should be described, not called a failure. || sharpen: Open on the verdict-depends-on-the-measure point and show the 32 to 21 versus 32 to 30 bars, stating the five-day duration plainly on the chart.
- [RES] C11-24 total 47.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Melatonin: realistic expectations :: Prescribed prolonged-release melatonin is licensed in the UK for short-term use in people aged 55 and over with insomnia, with modest effects; in Alzheimer's disease it may make little or no difference to sleep, and melatonin bought online can contain very different amounts from the label. || accuracy: Cochrane 2020 found low-certainty evidence that melatonin up to 10 mg may have little or no effect in Alzheimer's disease (total night sleep MD 10.7 min, CI crossing zero); the same review found some benefit for trazodone and orexin antagonists, so do not imply nothing helps. Melatonin is prescription-only in the UK, so the content-variability studies describe North American supplements. The Circadin SPC was not openable. || sharpen: Add that melatonin is prescription-only in the UK, so products bought online are unregulated, and give the reader a review-date question.
- [RES] C11-29 total 47.5 | practitioners | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Desmopressin for night-time urination: the sodium cost :: Desmopressin reduces urine made overnight but carries a high risk of low sodium in older people, so it needs careful selection and early sodium checks and is best avoided in frail older adults. || accuracy: Fralick 2019: 146 versus 11 per 1,000 person-years (HR 13.2) for the older desmopressin formulation, in a US commercial insurance database. The outcome was a coded hyponatraemia diagnosis, and only 20% had a baseline sodium. The newer low-dose formulations were not studied. || sharpen: Say the data are for the older formulation, and add the practical monitoring rule (baseline sodium, early recheck) from an openable source.
- [   ] C11-15 total 47.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Count the volume, not only the trips :: A frequency-volume chart over a few days separates making too much urine at night from a bladder that holds less, and the causes and treatments differ. || accuracy: The ICS 2002 definition of nocturnal polyuria (more than a third of 24-hour urine at night in older people) needs the source opened. NICE CG97 and NG123 were not openable. The CPAP and nocturia meta-analysis pooled low-quality before-after studies. || sharpen: Give the one-third rule with a filled, labelled illustrative chart a clinician can copy, and keep antimuscarinic harms to one line so it does not repeat E141.
- [   ] C11-25 total 47.0 | practitioners | Weighing it up | criticism | risk medium | Quetiapine is not a sleeping tablet :: Low-dose quetiapine and other sedating antipsychotics are sometimes used as sleeping tablets in older people despite limited evidence for insomnia and known harms, including higher risk of stroke and death in people with dementia. || accuracy: The stroke and death warnings apply to people with dementia; extending them to all older people needs its own source. Harms of low-dose quetiapine need a specific checked cohort study (the lead is 'unsure'). || sharpen: Keep the statement card, add a practical review step (record the reason, agree a slow taper), and cite a checked source on harms at low dose.
- [   ] C11-08 total 46.0 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Does the 2am check need to happen? :: Hospital patients report sleeping less and waking more than at home, often because staff wake them, and overnight observations and medicines for stable patients can be timed to protect sleep. || accuracy: Wesselius 2018 covered adults aged 18 and over (median age 68) and asked about one night by self-report; 718 patients (35.8%) said staff woke them. SIESTA was one US centre, a before-after design with a comparison unit, not specific to older people; order changes rose on both units. The NEWS2 12-hourly minimum for a score of 0 needs an openable copy. || sharpen: Make the ask a specific order written by the day team (protected sleep with observations clustered, unless clinically indicated) so night staff are backed rather than blamed.
- [   ] C11-14 total 46.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Test the saying: older people need less sleep :: In healthy adults most age-related change in measured sleep has happened by about 60; after that only sleep efficiency keeps falling, so marked new sleep trouble in later life is worth looking into. || accuracy: Ohayon 2004 pooled mostly small polysomnography samples published from 1960 to 2003. 'Most change has happened by 60' paraphrases 'only sleep efficiency continued to decrease after 60'. The NSF recommends 7 to 8 hours at 65 and over versus 7 to 9 for younger adults, so the saying is partly true and the post has to say so. || sharpen: Test the saying fairly: somewhat lighter, more broken sleep is normal, a new change is a symptom, and then list the checkable causes.
- [   ] C11-04 total 44.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | The first sleep question is about the clock :: An older person who spends much longer in bed than they can sleep will spend long periods awake in bed, and asking bedtime and getting-up time is how you find this. || accuracy: The claim that time in bed grows after retirement, illness or a care home move has no source listed. Sleep restriction needs caution in people at high falls risk or who drive, and is unsuitable for some conditions. HABIT's mean age was 55, so cite Buysse (mean 71.7) for older adults. || sharpen: Keep the clock question as the hook with a labelled illustrative time-in-bed bar, and make the teaching point the history question, not the trial, to stay distinct from C11-11.
- [   ] C11-27 total 44.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Ask a care home about the night :: When choosing or reviewing a care home, ask what happens at night: bedtimes and who decides them, how night checks are done, how continence care is given, and how noise and light are managed. || accuracy: Alessi's 339 of 492 is a US screening sample, not a prevalence figure for UK homes. The Schnelle 1993 and 1999 leads are unverified. CQC Regulation 9 applies in England only. || sharpen: Cut to four questions with the best evidence (bedtimes, night checks, continence care, noise and light) and cite the care standard for each UK nation.
- [   ] C11-09 total 44.0 | both | Who gets left out | good_care | risk medium | CBT for insomnia down the phone :: CBT for insomnia delivered by telephone improved sleep in older adults, so people without internet access need not be left with only a sleeping tablet. || accuracy: At 12 months, 67/119 (56.3%) versus 33/128 (25.8%) were in remission. The control group had education only, also by telephone, so the trial did not compare phone with digital CBT-I. All participants had osteoarthritis pain and belonged to one US health plan. The Ofcom internet-use figure was not openable. || sharpen: Make the claim about access (a phone is enough, no internet needed) without implying phone beats apps, and name a UK route an older person could ask about.
- [   ] C11-31 total 44.0 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Offer the warm drink first :: On a medical ward, offering older patients a warm drink, a back rub and relaxation audio before any sleeping tablet reduced sedative use from about half of patients to under a third. || accuracy: McDowell 1998 compared a pre-intervention rate of 51/94 (54%) with 34/111 (31%) in a different cohort on one ward in the 1990s. Chronic users refused the protocol more often (64% versus 41%) and still received hypnotics more often. Back rubs and relaxation tapes are dated. || sharpen: Update the routine for today's wards (a warm caffeine-free drink, relaxation audio, offered before any tablet) and state the before-after caveat plainly.
- [   ] C11-05 total 43.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Years on a sleeping tablet: stopping is still possible :: People who have taken sleeping tablets for many years can often stop with a slow, supervised reduction, and adding CBT for insomnia improves the chance of success. || accuracy: Morin 2004: n=76, mean age 62.5 (not an old-age sample), 10-week programme; 85%, 48% and 54% were drug-free after the initial intervention in the taper plus CBT, taper and CBT arms. 'No significant withdrawal symptoms' comes from a small trial, while D-PRESCRIBE found 38% of those tapering reported withdrawal symptoms; the two need reconciling in plain words. || sharpen: Speak directly to the long-term user and say that sleep may take months to improve after stopping, so the ask is for a supervised plan with CBT, not willpower.
- [   ] C11-01 total 41.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk medium | Who helps between bedtime and breakfast? :: Discharge planning that assesses daytime tasks can miss how often a person will need help overnight, and who will give it. || accuracy: The McCurry 2007 'two-thirds of dementia carers' figure is a background estimate in the review abstract, not a finding; the Pesonen nocturia-falls link (pooled RR 1.20) is observational and very low certainty as a cause; NICE NG27 was not openable, so the discharge-guidance claim needs a published copy. || sharpen: Rest the post on one checkable fact (night-time urination is common and linked with falls) and make the checklist card the post, with one named question for the discharge meeting.
- [   ] C11-26 total 41.5 | public | Who gets left out | decision_support | risk medium | The carer's nights :: Many family carers of people with dementia have disturbed sleep, and their own nights should be part of a carer's assessment and support plan. || accuracy: McCurry 2007's 'two-thirds' figure is a background estimate in a review, not a study finding. The link between night-time disturbance and care home admission needs a primary cohort (Hope 1998 is marked unsure). Carer assessment law differs across the UK nations. || sharpen: Replace the two-thirds figure with a primary study of carer sleep, and give the carer the exact sentence to use in the assessment.
- [   ] C11-12 total 41.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | What is still working at breakfast :: Some sleeping tablets still affect balance, reactions and driving the next morning, and older people often clear them more slowly. || accuracy: In Glass 2005 the psychomotor adverse event result was reported as P>0.05, so 'unsteadiness more common' needs care. The zopiclone SPC and FDA zolpidem pages were not openable. The zolpidem dose change was a US regulatory action and should not be presented as UK advice. || sharpen: Base the driving claim on an openable study of next-day driving impairment with zopiclone, and keep the timeline and three questions as the card.
- [   ] C11-17 total 41.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Reading the CBT versus zopiclone trial :: A small Norwegian trial in adults averaging 61 found CBT improved sleep efficiency more than zopiclone at 6 weeks and 6 months, and zopiclone did not differ from placebo on most outcomes. || accuracy: Sivertsen 2006: n=46, mean age 60.8. The placebo arm was followed only to 6 weeks, so the 6-month comparison is CBT versus zopiclone only. || sharpen: Fold this into C11-16 or C11-11 as a supporting trial; on its own it gives the reader little reason to care now.
- [   ] C11-30 total 41.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | The bedtime drink :: Alcohol can help people fall asleep but tends to disrupt sleep in the second half of the night, and can worsen snoring, sleep apnoea and restless legs. || accuracy: The Ebrahim 2013 review draws largely on young healthy volunteers. The claim about age and blood alcohol has no source. Stopping suddenly is unsafe for people who drink heavily every day. || sharpen: Add one sentence that anyone who drinks heavily every day should get advice before stopping, and find a checked source on older people and alcohol.
- [   ] C11-20 total 40.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Sleep apnoea in older people may not look sleepy :: Sleep apnoea is common in older people but may show as night-time urination, poor concentration or unrefreshing sleep more than obvious sleepiness, so ask the bed partner. || accuracy: Polysomnography finds very high rates of apnoea in older people (HypnoLaus), much of it of uncertain clinical significance, so 'consider OSA even without sleepiness' risks overdiagnosis. The claim that older people report less sleepiness rests on a review marked 'unsure'. NICE NG202 was not openable. || sharpen: Balance the prompt with evidence on who benefits from CPAP in later life (for example the PREDICT trial) so the post does not push testing for everyone who snores.
- [   ] C11-06 total 38.5 | public | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk high | The bedroom at 2am in a heatwave :: Hot nights shorten sleep, older adults' sleep appears more affected, and people in hot flats without cooling or money for it are most exposed. || accuracy: Minor 2022 used wristband sleep-tracker data; its users are unlikely to represent poor or isolated older people. The 'lower-income' finding is about countries, not low-income individuals. The paper is not PubMed-indexed and UKHSA guidance was not openable, so the practical measures have no opened source. || sharpen: Find an openable heat-health source for the bedroom measures or recast them as questions, and do not let the tracker data stand in for evidence about poor older people in hot flats.
- [   ] C11-19 total 37.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Dozing in the chair: ask about the medicines :: Falling asleep in the day without meaning to is common in later life but is not explained by age alone; medicines, sleep apnoea, low mood and other illness are common causes worth checking. || accuracy: The claim that daytime sleepiness in later life comes from illness, medicines and sleep disorders rather than age has no checked source (the leads are 'unsure'). The BNF was not openable for the drug list. The Glass fatigue finding covers hypnotics only. || sharpen: Find one checked epidemiological source on causes of daytime sleepiness in older people and add that sleepiness which is new or getting worse is a reason to see the GP soon.
- [   ] C11-18 total 46.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Two sessions and two phone calls :: Brief behavioural treatment for insomnia, delivered by a nurse in two sessions and two phone calls, helped most older adults in a US trial, and nurse-delivered sleep restriction worked in English general practice. || EXCLUDED: duplicate of C11-11 (kept the higher score) || accuracy: Buysse 2011: n=79, primary outcome at 4 weeks (response 67% versus 25%, NNT 2.4), maintained at 6 months. HABIT's mean age was 55, so it does not show effect in older adults. || sharpen: If kept, make it the older-adult service-model post (who can deliver it and how short it is) and drop HABIT; otherwise merge Buysse into C11-11.

## C12 Continence, bladder and bowels (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 split well by audience (5 practitioners: C12-09, C12-18, C12-05, C12-19, C12-33; 4 public: C12-08, C12-22, C12-36, C12-03; 5 both: C12-23, C12-17, C12-11, C12-32, C12-20), but by topic they lean towards women (C12-20, C12-22, C12-32, C12-36) and urine infection (C12-17, C12-18, C12-22, C12-23), with only one bowel post (C12-19) and one men's post (C12-33). Consider swapping C12-20 or C12-32 for C12-26 (bowel leakage, public) and adding C12-16 or C12-15 for care homes; the Who gets left out series has no entry in the top 14 (C12-38 is the best candidate for it).
Judge allocation reason: 36 of 38 candidates reach about 36 and 19 reach 44 or more with no disqualifying flag (the stated formula's true maximum is 55, not 50), and continence is almost untouched by the earlier collection, so the category supports the maximum even after folding C12-10 into C12-17 and C12-29 into C12-05.
- [SEL] C12-09 total 50.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | criticism | risk low | Continent at home, incontinent at discharge :: In a study of continent adults aged 70 and over, using pads or catheters in hospital was linked with new incontinence at discharge, so helping people to the toilet should be the default. || accuracy: One Israeli cohort with self-reported incontinence at discharge; sicker, less mobile patients are more likely to receive pads or catheters, so the odds ratios are associations open to confounding; the claim about documentation reflecting products is unsourced. || sharpen: Say in the post that sicker people are more likely to get pads or catheters, so the study shows a link and not proof of cause, and frame the fix as help to the toilet and answered call bells.
- [SEL] C12-23 total 50.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Cranberry: who the evidence covers :: The 2023 Cochrane review found cranberry products probably reduce repeat urine infections in women with recurrent infections, but may make little or no difference for older people living in care homes or other institutions. || accuracy: The institutional result has wide uncertainty (RR 0.93, 0.67 to 1.30) and its certainty grade needs checking; do not assert that care homes routinely give cranberry without a source. || sharpen: Show the institutional result with its uncertainty in plain words ('did not show a clear benefit') and add absolute numbers per 100 from the summary of findings tables.
- [SEL] C12-17 total 48.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Smell and cloudiness are poor guides to infection :: Cloudy or strong-smelling urine on its own is a poor guide to infection in older people; new urinary symptoms, fever or pain are what to report. || accuracy: Midthun 2004 is small (97 pads) and the 'about a third wrong' figure needs checking; UKHSA over-65 wording should be quoted exactly. || sharpen: Keep the named-belief opening, quote UKHSA wording exactly, and add one line on what antibiotics for harmless bacteria cost the person (side effects, C. difficile, resistance).
- [SEL] C12-18 total 48.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Why the dipstick stays in the drawer after 65 :: In people over 65, a positive dipstick often reflects harmless bacteria, so UK guidance advises against dipsticks for diagnosing urine infection; diagnosis rests on new urinary or systemic signs, with culture when infection is suspected. || accuracy: The flow must include all UKHSA over-65 signs (including new or worse confusion and rigors) or it misstates the guidance; dipstick advice differs for people with catheters; Crellin 2018 is observational. || sharpen: Put the full UKHSA over-65 sign list in the flow, keep the trimethoprim harm as an absolute excess in people on RAS blockers or spironolactone, and differentiate clearly from C12-17.
- [SEL] C12-08 total 47.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Before incontinence decides the move :: Incontinence is strongly linked with moving into a care home, so when it is driving that decision, ask whether it has been assessed and whether treatment, equipment or night-time help would change things. || accuracy: Thom 1997 figures are adjusted relative risks from a 1990s US HMO cohort; they show an association and do not show that treating incontinence prevents a move; the 'harder than behavioural symptoms' claim needs its primary source. || sharpen: Keep 'a care home can still be the right choice' prominent and state the Thom figures as an association from an older US cohort, not as evidence that treatment prevents admission.
- [SEL] C12-22 total 47.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Vaginal oestrogen is a different decision from HRT :: Low-dose vaginal oestrogen can reduce repeat urine infections after the menopause and NICE recommends considering it; it is used in the vagina at a low dose, which makes it a different decision from HRT tablets or patches. || accuracy: Cochrane evidence for vaginal oestrogen rests on two small trials; the over-the-counter 10 microgram tablet is licensed for vaginal dryness, not urine infection prevention; that women decline it fearing HRT risks is an unsourced assumption. || sharpen: Make clear the over-the-counter product is for vaginal dryness, not urine infections, and give the Cochrane result as 'fewer infections in two small trials'.
- [SEL] C12-36 total 47.0 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Looking after your own pessary :: In a UK trial, women taught to remove and replace their own vaginal pessary for prolapse had similar quality of life to women who attended clinic for it, reported fewer complications, and the approach was cost-effective. || accuracy: TOPSY was a superiority trial that found no clear difference in quality of life, so 'did as well as' overstates; about half responded at 4 years. || sharpen: Replace 'did as well as' with 'showed no clear difference in quality of life', and keep the trial exclusions on slide 3.
- [SEL] C12-03 total 46.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | The key to locked accessible toilets :: Across the UK, many locked accessible toilets open with one national key, and 'Can't Wait' cards help people ask for urgent use of a toilet; both can make going out possible for people with bladder or bowel urgency. || accuracy: Toilet count and key price change and rest on scheme websites that may not be openable from this environment; 'RADAR key' is the older name; the Yip 2013 isolation link is cross-sectional and says nothing about whether the key helps. || sharpen: Verify the toilet count and key price from an opened source or drop the number, and add one line pointing to a GP or continence service for the urgency itself.
- [SEL] C12-05 total 46.0 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Every catheter needs a leaving date :: When someone leaves hospital with a urinary catheter, the letter should say why it went in, when, and who will remove it or try without it, because longer catheter use raises infection risk. || accuracy: The Meddings halving of catheter infections came from hospital inpatient studies and should not be applied to discharge or community care; the claim that people leave hospital without a removal plan has no UK source yet. || sharpen: Keep the three-field tag but present the 'halved infections' figure as inpatient evidence only, and say who removes the catheter or runs the trial without it in a care home or at home.
- [SEL] C12-11 total 46.0 | both | Care that works | teaching | risk low | From the urge to the seat :: Getting to the toilet in time is a chain of steps (stand, walk, open the door, find the toilet, undo clothing, sit), and leaks often come from one broken link that can be fixed. || accuracy: 'Leaks often come from one broken link' is unquantified; the Brown 2000 falls link is an association and the rushing mechanism was the authors' hypothesis. || sharpen: Add the night-time version of the chain (light, commode, urinal bottle) and one sourced sentence defining functional incontinence so the post rests on more than common sense.
- [RES] C12-19 total 46.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Loose stool in a frail older person: two opposite causes :: Leaking loose stool in a frail older person can be overflow around hard, impacted stool or the result of too many laxatives; the treatments are opposite, so examine and review the laxative chart before treating. || accuracy: The two-cause frame omits infection, including C. difficile, and medicines such as antibiotics, as causes of new loose stool in frail older people. || sharpen: Add a third branch for infection (new diarrhoea needs a stool sample, including for C. difficile) so the teaching card does not miss the dangerous cause.
- [RES] C12-33 total 46.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Dribbling can mean a full bladder :: In an older man with constant dribbling or frequent small voids, the bladder may not be emptying (chronic retention with overflow); a bladder scan after he passes urine finds it, and a bladder tablet for urgency could make it worse. || accuracy: Post-micturition dribble is common and usually harmless and must be distinguished from continuous leakage; NICE CG97 wording on measuring residual volume before antimuscarinics needs checking; residual thresholds vary. || sharpen: Distinguish continuous leakage from the common after-dribble, and check the NICE CG97 wording on residual volume before any bladder tablet.
- [RES] C12-20 total 44.5 | both | Weighing it up | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Bladder training before bladder tablets :: In a trial in older women with urge incontinence, behavioural treatment reduced leaks more than oxybutynin and far fewer women wanted to switch from it, and in UK prescription data most people stopped their bladder tablet within a year. || accuracy: The Burgio intervention was biofeedback-assisted behavioural treatment (pelvic floor training and urge control), not bladder training as NICE defines it; the drug was immediate-release oxybutynin; the persistence data are industry-authored. || sharpen: Call the intervention 'behavioural treatment with pelvic floor training and urge control' rather than 'bladder training', and put the trial exclusions (no dementia, walking unaided) on the chart.
- [   ] C12-32 total 44.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Pelvic floor classes worked as well as one-to-one :: In a trial of 362 women aged 60 and over with stress or mixed leakage, 12 weeks of group pelvic floor classes reduced leaks as much as one-to-one physiotherapy at one year, which could let more women get a first-line treatment. || accuracy: A non-inferiority result, not superiority; trial in Canadian community-dwelling women without cognitive impairment, with physiotherapist-led groups; the week-4 figure comes from a 2025 secondary analysis and needs checking. || sharpen: Say 'reduced leaks about as much', give the medians in plain words, and note the trial setting and that groups were led by physiotherapists.
- [   ] C12-15 total 44.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | good_care | risk low | Count the night-time wake-ups :: In US nursing home studies, most night-time incontinence care woke residents, and individual schedules based on skin risk reduced waking without worse skin, so a care home can count its own night wakes and plan per person. || accuracy: Small 1990s US nursing home studies with older continence products; the 87% figure is unverified; 'no worse skin' comes from one small controlled study; C11 may cover night checks too. || sharpen: State the study era and setting on the post and visual, verify the 9 in 10 figure, and suggest a one-week count of night wakes a care home could run itself.
- [   ] C12-16 total 44.0 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Three days to find out who stays dry :: In nursing home research, a three-day trial of prompted toileting identified the residents who respond well, and about 4 in 10 of those screened did, so a new resident's first weeks are a good time to try it. || accuracy: A 1995 US research cohort with research staff delivering prompts; responder rates may not hold in routine care; 'a new resident's first weeks' is the post's inference, not a finding; the 41% and wet-check figures need checking. || sharpen: Present the first weeks after admission as a suggestion rather than a finding, and name the staff time prompted voiding needs so the post does not imply it is free.
- [   ] C12-25 total 44.0 | public | Reading the evidence | public_explanation | risk low | Once a day is one normal among many :: In a Bristol population study, most people did not open their bowels on a regular daily cycle; what signals a problem is hard stools, straining, or a lasting change from your own usual pattern, and a change like that should be checked. || accuracy: Heaton 1992 sampled adults aged about 25 to 69, not older people; the three-a-day to three-a-week range comes from earlier work (Connell 1965); NICE lower gastrointestinal referral now relies on FIT testing. || sharpen: State the age range of the Bristol sample, attribute the frequency range to its source, and align the 'see your GP' box with current NICE wording.
- [   ] C12-26 total 44.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | The bowel leakage people rarely mention :: Accidental bowel leakage becomes more common with age, often has treatable causes, and most people who have it never tell a doctor. || accuracy: Johanson 1996 was a 1990s US clinic sample of adults of all ages; 'most never tell a doctor' should read 'in one US survey, about 2 in 3 had not'. || sharpen: Give the one-third figure with its source and era, and end with what a GP or continence service can offer so the reader knows what to expect.
- [   ] C12-37 total 44.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | New leaking after a dementia medicine :: Cholinesterase inhibitors such as donepezil can cause or worsen urinary incontinence, and in a large cohort people started on them were more likely to be prescribed a bladder antimuscarinic, a drug with the opposite action. || accuracy: Gill 2005 shows increased dispensing (an association) that could partly reflect dementia progression or closer contact with prescribers; the absolute difference (4.5% vs 3.1%) should be given; the SmPC frequency category needs checking. || sharpen: Give the absolute figures (about 4 or 5 in 100 vs 3 in 100) and coordinate with C06 so the prescribing cascade is not posted twice.
- [   ] C12-24 total 43.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | For men, consider a sheath before a catheter :: For men who need urine collected but can empty their bladder, an external sheath avoids a tube in the bladder, was more comfortable in a randomised trial, and was linked with fewer infections or deaths than an indwelling catheter. || accuracy: The composite outcome was driven mainly by bacteriuria, the unadjusted difference was not significant, and the trial was small (75 men); 'fewer infections or deaths' overclaims. || sharpen: Describe the outcome as mostly bacteria in the urine rather than 'fewer infections or deaths', and put sizing and daily skin checks on the visual.
- [   ] C12-02 total 43.0 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | A continence service or a pad service? :: NICE describes absorbent pads as a way of coping while causes are looked for and treated, so a continence assessment should record a likely cause and a treatment plan as well as a product size. || accuracy: NICE NG123 covers women only; the pads-are-not-treatment wording must be quoted exactly and paired with CG97 for men; the claim that services report products supplied rather than outcomes is unsourced and should be dropped. || sharpen: Open on the recognisable contrast (a pad size is recorded, a cause is not) rather than 'NICE describes', and attribute the standard to NG123 for women and CG97 for men.
- [   ] C12-12 total 43.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | A catheter in the last days: a comfort decision :: In the last days of life, a urinary catheter can be a comfort measure, for example when changes and turns are painful or urine is retained, and guidelines accept comfort at the end of life as a reason for one. || accuracy: Retention near death from opioids and anticholinergic medicines needs a palliative care guideline source; HOUDINI publication details are unconfirmed; coordinate with C19 to avoid a cross-category repeat. || sharpen: Open with the concrete situation (turns for pad changes are painful in the last days) and cite one UK palliative care guideline beside the CDC indication.
- [   ] C12-14 total 43.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | If the catheter blocks at night :: If you live with a long-term catheter, find out before it happens who to call when it stops draining out of hours, and whether a spare catheter is kept at home. || accuracy: Blockage frequency and the 'spare catheter at home' advice need a source, as local practice varies; the first-check steps must match NHS or RCN wording exactly. || sharpen: Check the first steps against RCN or NHS wording, add NHS 111 as the fallback number, and make clear that carers are not expected to change the catheter themselves.
- [   ] C12-21 total 43.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | Stand still, squeeze, wait :: When a sudden urge to pass urine comes, standing still and squeezing the pelvic floor muscles a few times until the urge eases, then walking calmly, is part of bladder training and avoids a rushed walk to the toilet. || accuracy: Urge suppression was one component of multicomponent programmes and its separate effect has not been tested; the falls link is an association only. || sharpen: Say the stop-and-squeeze step is one part of a programme that works, and pair it with a next step such as a bladder diary or a continence nurse.
- [   ] C12-27 total 43.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Moisture damage or pressure damage? :: Skin damage from contact with urine or stool (incontinence-associated dermatitis) looks different from a pressure ulcer and needs different care: gentle pH-balanced cleansing, a barrier product and prompt changes. || accuracy: Evidence is mainly expert consensus; moisture and pressure damage can coexist, and superficial sacral damage may be either; the GLOBIAD citation is unconfirmed. || sharpen: Add that the two can coexist and uncertain cases need a nurse's assessment, and cite the GLOBIAD tool or IAD consensus explicitly.
- [   ] C12-01 total 42.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | Squeeze before you cough :: Tightening the pelvic floor muscles just before and during a cough or sneeze can reduce stress leakage, and in a small trial older women learned this within a week. || accuracy: Rests on a single small 1998 study (27 women with mild to moderate stress leakage, laboratory cough test); check that it was randomised as described; the absent correlation with strength is a non-significant finding, not evidence that strength is irrelevant. || sharpen: Say 'in a small 1998 study of 27 women' in the caption and pair the squeeze with a clear pointer to supervised pelvic floor training for at least 3 months, so one technique is not presented as the whole treatment.
- [   ] C12-07 total 42.0 | both | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | The bladder tablet and the teeth :: Bladder antimuscarinic tablets had the strongest link with dry mouth of the medicine groups studied in older people, and a dry mouth raises the risk of tooth decay and denture problems, so tell the prescriber and the dentist. || accuracy: 'Bladder tablets' overgeneralises: the dry-mouth link is with antimuscarinics, and mirabegron causes much less; Tan 2018 pooled heterogeneous intervention studies; the industry-authored Wagg persistence study is off-topic here. || sharpen: Say 'antimuscarinic bladder tablets' rather than 'bladder tablets', and close on a concrete review question such as 'Has anyone asked about your mouth since this tablet started?'
- [   ] C12-30 total 41.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Nocturia: measure the volumes, not only the trips :: Getting up at night to pass urine has two common patterns, making too much urine at night or a bladder that holds less, and a bladder diary with measured volumes tells them apart, which changes treatment. || accuracy: ICS thresholds differ between the 2002 and 2019 reports; two patterns simplify mixed nocturia and sleep-driven waking; NICE CG97 applies to men; nocturia is in C11's scope. || sharpen: Coordinate with C11 to avoid a repeat, allow for mixed patterns, and use the threshold from the current ICS terminology report.
- [   ] C12-34 total 41.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | After a meal, knees up, door shut :: Bowels open more easily after a meal, when sitting with the knees raised on a footstool, and in private; a bedpan in a shared bay removes all three. || accuracy: Footstool evidence is from small studies, mostly in younger adults; the bedpan claim has no source; early-morning timing comes from a 25 to 69 population sample. || sharpen: Drop or source the bedpan claim, and label the footstool evidence as small studies so the practical advice is not oversold.
- [   ] C12-06 total 40.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk high | The water tablet and the day out :: Loop diuretics such as furosemide produce most of their extra urine in the hours after a dose, so if a water tablet stops you going out, ask the prescriber whether the timing can change rather than skipping it. || accuracy: Duration of furosemide effect varies by source and product (roughly 4 to 8 hours); evidence that people skip diuretics for outings is unconfirmed; SmPC and BNF pages may not be openable here. || sharpen: Anchor the timing claim to the SmPC or BNF figure actually opened, and find one study showing people skip or delay diuretics for outings, or keep it as a question to ask without that claim.
- [   ] C12-38 total 40.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Continence care after a dementia diagnosis :: People with dementia are often moved straight to pads, but causes such as constipation, medicines, difficulty finding the toilet and not being prompted can still be addressed, and prompted toileting helped some care home residents who could follow a simple instruction. || accuracy: 'Several of the best trials excluded dementia' needs a count and names; 'often moved straight to pads' needs support beyond a small qualitative study (Drennan 2011). || sharpen: Replace 'several' with a named count of trials that excluded people with dementia, and use a stronger opening than a statement about an evidence gap.
- [   ] C12-13 total 39.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | The questions missing from the clerking :: Asking every older person on admission whether they leak urine, have to rush, get up at night and have bowel accidents finds problems that affect falls, skin, sleep and discharge, and most people will not raise them unprompted. || accuracy: Johanson 1996 and Talbot 1995 are old US samples, not older inpatients; the claim that softer wording ('leak' rather than 'incontinent') changes answers is unsupported. || sharpen: Find support for the wording point or drop it, and tie each question to one ward action so the card is more than a reminder to ask.
- [   ] C12-28 total 39.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk high | One well-fitted pad works better than two :: Layering two pads, or a pad over a bed protector, often makes leaks and sore skin more likely, because the waterproof backing of the inner layer stops urine reaching the outer one and traps heat and moisture. || accuracy: Booster pads without a waterproof backing are designed to be layered; the claim that layering makes leaks and sore skin more likely rests on service advice rather than studies. || sharpen: Exclude booster pads designed for layering, and find a published continence service or consensus source before asserting that double padding worsens leaks and skin.
- [   ] C12-35 total 37.0 | both | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk high | When an accessible toilet is not accessible enough :: People who need a hoist and a changing bench, including some older people after a stroke or with advanced illness, can only use a Changing Places toilet, and there is a national map to find them. || accuracy: Toilet count and the scope of the England building regulation rest on web sources that may not be openable here; campaign surveys are not independent evidence. || sharpen: Verify the count from an opened source or omit it, state the building regulation scope precisely, and tie the post to older people who need a hoist after stroke or in advanced illness.
- [   ] C12-31 total 36.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | Drinking less to leak less :: Cutting right down on drinks to avoid leaks is a common coping strategy that can bring other problems; NICE advises adjusting fluid intake only when it is too high or too low, and trying less caffeine for urgency. || accuracy: Harms of fluid restriction (constipation, dizziness, bladder irritation from concentrated urine) are weakly supported; Talbot 1995 is small and old; NG123 applies to women; overlaps C13 hydration. || sharpen: Keep to NICE wording on modifying high or low fluid intake and trying less caffeine, and drop the weakly supported harms.
- [   ] C12-04 total 35.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Common, often treatable, sometimes persistent :: Incontinence becomes more common with age and often improves with treatment, but average improvement in trials is not cure for everyone, so the goal may be fewer leaks, and good care continues when leaking persists. || accuracy: Median and mean reductions in leaks do not give the proportion of people still leaking; use the cure proportions the trials report; Resnick 1985 reference details are unconfirmed. || sharpen: Give it a concrete opening and report cure proportions from the trials rather than inferring them from median reductions, or fold its message into C12-20 or C12-32.
- [   ] C12-10 total 35.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Why the pharmacy UTI service has an age limit :: Pharmacy urine infection services are limited to younger women, and after 65 the guidance on diagnosis changes because harmless bacteria in the urine are common and dipsticks mislead, so older women with symptoms should contact their GP practice promptly. || EXCLUDED: duplicate of C12-17 (kept the higher score) || accuracy: The stated reason for the Pharmacy First age limit is inferred rather than sourced; age boundaries differ between England (16 to 64) and Scotland and must be checked; the symptom checklist and 'poor guides' panel repeat C12-17. || sharpen: Fold the pharmacy age-limit hook into C12-17 as one line, or drop it; if kept, source the reason for the age limit instead of inferring it.
- [   ] C12-29 total 41.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Name the reason for the catheter :: An indwelling urinary catheter should have a reason you can name, such as retention or hourly output monitoring in critical illness, and the CDC guideline lists using one in place of nursing care for incontinence as inappropriate. || EXCLUDED: duplicate of C12-05 (kept the higher score) || accuracy: The CDC guideline is US and its full text has not been opened; the Meddings halving applies to inpatient studies; 'one-point restraint' is an editorial term, not a finding; overlaps C12-05 on reason and removal plan. || sharpen: Merge with C12-05, or differentiate by focusing on nurse-led removal protocols and UK guidance, with the CDC list translated into UK terms.

## C13 Nutrition, swallowing, hydration and mouth care (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 by score (33, 14, 09, 36, 02, 11, 18, 20, 08, 32, 12, 23, 30, 26) is 6 practitioner, 6 both and only 2 public (C13-23, C13-26). Four of the top eight are evidence corrections (33, 14, 02, 20), so the set leans sceptical. To get closer to the collection's balance, swap C13-30 (a general inpatient trial) for C13-27 (dentures at night, public), and if C06 or C19 take C13-08 or C13-11, replace them with C13-17 or C13-01 (rewritten as the public twin of C13-20). No Ask this or Who gets left out post makes the top 14; C13-07 and C13-24 are the best public Ask this options if the series needs one here. Coherence: if C13-14 runs, word any selected mouth care or denture posts (16, 17, 27) as associations and do not justify mouth care by pneumonia prevention.
Judge allocation reason: 33 of 36 candidates reach 36 or more with no disqualifying flag and 17 score 44.5 or more, so the category easily fills the 14-post maximum even after setting aside the cross-category ideas (C13-08 medicines, C13-11 end of life, C13-23 senses, C13-28 weight loss). Note: with the stated weights the formula's true maximum is 55, not 50.
- [SEL] C13-33 total 51.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Red plates for dementia: what the study was :: The best-known study behind red plates for people with dementia included nine men; the idea is cheap and low risk to try, but it is not established and it doesn't replace help with eating. || accuracy: 'Not established' needs a systematic review of mealtime interventions in dementia (Abdelhamid 2016 unchecked); do not slide from 'little evidence' to 'does not work'. || sharpen: Add one line from a systematic review of mealtime interventions in dementia so 'not established' is sourced.
- [SEL] C13-14 total 51.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Mouth care and pneumonia: less certain than the slogan :: Trials of care home mouth care programmes have not shown a clear fall in pneumonia, so the firmer reasons to clean an older person's mouth are comfort, eating, finding sore spots and dignity. || accuracy: Cao 2022 also found low-certainty evidence of fewer pneumonia deaths at 24 months, and Zimmerman 2020 had a post hoc first-year reduction; both need stating so the post reads as uncertain, not as evidence of no effect. || sharpen: Include the low-certainty finding on pneumonia deaths and the post hoc first-year signal, so the claim is 'uncertain' rather than 'does not work'.
- [SEL] C13-09 total 48.5 | both | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Count the sealed packets on the tray :: Many older people can't open the cheese portions, cartons and foil-sealed pots served in hospitals, so food that arrives and leaves unopened was never available to eat. || accuracy: Participants were well community-dwelling volunteers in Australia, not inpatients; 39% refers to cheese portions in Bell 2016 and the posture study had 34 participants; UK packaging may differ. || sharpen: Open on the tray with the 4 in 10 figure, make the seated-posture finding the second action, and label the population as well older volunteers in Australia.
- [SEL] C13-36 total 48.5 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk low | 38 minutes per meal :: In a US nursing home study, one-to-one help with a meal took an average of 38 minutes per resident, compared with about 9 minutes of help in usual care, and half of residents ate more with that help. || accuracy: Simmons 2001 is a small 2001 US study in which research staff gave the one-to-one help over two days; 'half ate more' should keep 'significantly' and not be generalised to UK wards or care homes. || sharpen: Say that the 38 minutes came from research staff in a 2001 US study, and add a more recent or UK figure if one can be sourced.
- [SEL] C13-02 total 47.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | What protected mealtimes did and did not change :: Studies of protected mealtimes found no rise in what patients ate; the approach with better support is hands-on help at the meal. || accuracy: Porter 2017 was in subacute (rehabilitation) wards in Melbourne and Hickson 2011 was a single-site before-and-after study, so do not generalise to all acute wards; the claim that hands-on mealtime help raises intake needs its own hospital source (Simmons 2001 is US nursing homes; Young 2013 unchecked). || sharpen: Name the settings in the post and give one sourced figure for what mealtime assistance achieved, so the alternative is as well evidenced as the critique.
