- [SEL] C13-11 total 47.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Near the end of life, a dry mouth is eased at the mouth :: Near death, thirst and a dry mouth are usually eased by frequent mouth care and small sips, while fluids by drip or under the skin are a separate decision whose effect on comfort is uncertain. || accuracy: Evidence is mainly from people with cancer (McCann 1994, 32 patients; Bruera 2013 hospice RCT); NICE NG31 needs a published copy; the post must not imply assisted hydration is never appropriate. || sharpen: Coordinate with C19 so this runs once, and add one line that fluids by drip are a separate decision families can discuss with the team.
- [SEL] C13-18 total 47.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Feeding tubes in advanced dementia: what the evidence shows :: For people with advanced dementia who are eating less, studies have not shown that feeding tubes help them live longer or more comfortably, and careful hand feeding is the recommended approach. || accuracy: Check the certainty grading Davies 2021 gives for the pressure ulcer and pneumonia findings; all evidence is non-randomised; NICE NG97 wording needs confirming. || sharpen: Add the confirmed NICE NG97 wording and one line on what careful hand feeding looks like when the person eats very little.
- [SEL] C13-20 total 47.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Dry mouth and dark urine are poor tests for dehydration in older people :: A Cochrane review found that common bedside signs, including urine colour, a dry mouth and feeling thirsty, should not be relied on alone to detect dehydration in older people; serum osmolality is the reference test. || accuracy: 'Missing drinks between meals' came from one small study (71 people); measured serum osmolality is not always available, so a validated calculated osmolarity equation needs its own source. || sharpen: Add the validated calculated osmolarity equation with its source so practitioners have a usable test when measured osmolality is unavailable.
- [SEL] C13-08 total 46.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Nil by mouth still needs a plan for Parkinson's medicines :: When a person with Parkinson's can't swallow safely, their dopaminergic medicines still need to be given on time by another route, and that plan belongs in the same moment the nil by mouth order is written. || accuracy: Confirm that 39% in Skelly 2017 refers to scheduled doses, not patients; alternative-route guidance rests on NICE NG71 and Brennan 2010, neither yet opened; no conversion doses should appear; overlaps with C06 medicines. || sharpen: Verify the 39% denominator and add one sourced consequence of missed doses (such as worsening rigidity or swallow) so the length-of-stay finding does not undercut the urgency.
- [SEL] C13-12 total 45.5 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Safe to swallow, and still worth eating :: People with swallowing problems value enjoying food, eating with others and not feeling afraid at meals, and routine swallowing plans rarely record any of these. || accuracy: Ekberg 2002 surveyed people of mixed ages in clinics and nursing homes over 20 years ago, so do not present it as a study of older people only; Wright 2005 had 55 patients. || sharpen: Turn the three findings into three specific questions for a swallowing review, and state the survey date and mixed ages.
- [RES] C13-23 total 45.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | When food tastes of nothing :: Sense of smell declines markedly in later life, which helps explain why food can seem bland; stronger flavours, varied textures and a check of medicines and the mouth can help. || accuracy: Doty 1984 measured smell identification, not flavour perception at meals; trials of flavour enhancement on intake are mixed, so tips must be framed as things to try; 'often a loss of smell' is an attribution to soften. || sharpen: Frame flavour tips as things to try, say the evidence on intake is mixed, and coordinate with C14 on the senses.
- [RES] C13-30 total 45.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Hospital nutrition support has trial evidence: what EFFORT showed :: In a large Swiss trial of medical inpatients at nutritional risk, individualised nutrition support started within 48 hours reduced adverse outcomes and deaths at 30 days compared with standard hospital food. || accuracy: EFFORT was open-label, mortality was a secondary outcome, and participants were general medical inpatients (age profile to confirm, median about 75) in Switzerland. || sharpen: Confirm the age profile and say plainly that the trial was open-label and that deaths were a secondary outcome.
- [RES] C13-32 total 45.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Sharing the serving dish :: In a Dutch trial, nursing home residents who ate family style, serving themselves from dishes at a shared table, kept their weight and quality of life better than those given pre-plated meals. || accuracy: Nijs 2006 enrolled residents without dementia in five Dutch homes; the 6.1-unit quality of life gain needs a plain meaning; single trial from 2006. || sharpen: Explain the quality of life gain in plain words and state early that residents did not have dementia.
- [   ] C13-17 total 44.5 | both | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Nothing by mouth, and still a mouth to clean :: People fed by tube still need their teeth and mouth cleaned every day; in one study, tube-fed older people carried potentially harmful bacteria in the throat far more often than those who ate by mouth. || accuracy: Throat colonisation is not pneumonia; Leibovitz 2003 is a small Israeli study; nasogastric feeding in UK care homes is uncommon, so PEG is the more relevant case; avoid implying mouth care prevents pneumonia given C13-14. || sharpen: Justify daily mouth care by comfort and hygiene first, say colonisation is not infection, and centre the example on PEG feeding.
- [   ] C13-26 total 44.5 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | When someone with dementia pushes the toothbrush away :: A relationship-based approach tested in a randomised trial helped more nursing home residents with dementia accept mouth care, using techniques families and care workers can learn. || accuracy: 'Twice the odds' must not become 'twice as likely'; the named techniques need the intervention description paper, not only the trial abstract. || sharpen: Open on the recognisable moment of the toothbrush being pushed away and name two techniques in the caption, sourced to the intervention paper.
- [   ] C13-27 total 44.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Take the dentures out at night :: In a study of people aged 85 and over, those who slept in their dentures had a higher rate of pneumonia, and more plaque and yeast in the mouth, than those who took them out. || accuracy: Iinuma 2015 is observational despite its title ('doubles the risk'); overnight wearers may differ in health or care; the claim that dentures are lost in hospital needs a source or should be dropped. || sharpen: Avoid the paper's 'doubles' wording and add that other differences between overnight wearers and others may explain part of the link.
- [   ] C13-28 total 44.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Losing weight after 65: keep the muscle :: In a trial of older adults with obesity, weight loss combined with both strength and aerobic exercise improved physical function most, and aerobic exercise alone lost the most muscle and hip bone. || accuracy: Hip bone density fell by about 2.6% with aerobic exercise alone in Villareal 2017, not 3%; all three exercise groups also dieted; participants were volunteers able to exercise. || sharpen: Correct the hip bone figure and state that all groups dieted, so the comparison is between types of exercise.
- [   ] C13-22 total 43.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Unexplained weight loss after 60 deserves a measured weight and a plan :: In pooled primary care studies, weight loss recorded by a GP in someone aged 60 or over carried a cancer risk above the 3 in 100 level UK guidance uses to prompt investigation, yet most studies relied on a coded symptom rather than a measured weight. || accuracy: Verify the exact age and sex groups in which Nicholson 2018 found a positive predictive value above 3%; the NG12 threshold needs an opened source; overlaps with C13-03 on measured weight. || sharpen: Verify the age and sex bands for the 3% figure and keep the proportional-investigation point explicit.
- [   ] C13-25 total 43.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | More older people keep their own teeth, and mouth care has to change :: Far fewer older people have no natural teeth than a generation ago, so carers and care homes increasingly need to clean natural teeth, crowns and bridges for someone else, not only dentures. || accuracy: Adult Dental Health Survey figures are not yet verified (commonly quoted as 37% of adults with no natural teeth in 1968 and 6% in 2009, all adults, not older people only); the root decay claim needs a source. || sharpen: Verify the survey figures from an opened report and lead with the chart of falling tooth loss, keeping the brushing checklist.
- [   ] C13-34 total 43.0 | practitioners | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Plain water between meals for people on thickened drinks :: For carefully selected people on thickened drinks, allowing plain water between meals after mouth care did not increase lung complications in rehabilitation studies, and may increase how much they drink. || accuracy: Gillman 2017 found low-quality evidence in mostly rehabilitation inpatients; its title ('does not result in aspiration pneumonia') overclaims and should not be echoed. || sharpen: Take the selection criteria from the Gillman review for the diagram and phrase the result as 'did not show an increase'.
- [   ] C13-35 total 43.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | The 'diabetic diet' for someone who is frail and losing weight :: For frail older people with diabetes who are eating poorly, restricting sugar and portions adds risks of undernutrition and low blood sugar; guidance advises relaxing dietary restrictions and adjusting medicines to what the person eats. || accuracy: ESPEN, ADA and Diabetes UK wording still to confirm; the hypoglycaemia point needs a source specific to insulin and sulfonylureas. || sharpen: Confirm the guideline wording and add a sourced line on low blood sugar risk with insulin or sulfonylureas when intake falls.
- [   ] C13-06 total 42.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Watch one meal before you write 'eating and drinking well' :: A few minutes of watching an older patient eat shows problems that notes and food charts miss: a tray out of reach, sealed packets, slow chewing, coughing, a wet voice, food held in the cheek, tiring halfway. || accuracy: The silent aspiration caveat has no source found yet; the sign list needs RCSLT or stroke guidance; Simmons 2001 minutes come from US nursing homes, not hospital wards. || sharpen: Tie the observation list to a sourced set of swallowing warning signs and give the silent aspiration caveat a citable source or drop it.
- [   ] C13-15 total 42.5 | practitioners | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Nil by mouth is a treatment with side effects :: Keeping an older person with aspiration pneumonia nil by mouth 'to be safe' was linked in one cohort with poorer intake, longer treatment and loss of swallowing ability, so the order needs a reason, a review time and prompt swallow assessment. || accuracy: Maeda 2016 is a retrospective cohort whose title uses causal language ('leads to'); confounding by illness severity remains despite weighting; the title 'a treatment with side effects' overclaims; Koyama 2015 not found yet. || sharpen: Retitle to avoid the causal 'side effects' framing, state confounding by severity in the post, and consider sharing the complete-order card with C13-08.
- [   ] C13-03 total 42.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Ask for the weight six months ago before you trust the BMI :: A normal BMI can hide malnutrition in an older person; recent unplanned weight loss is the more telling measure, and the GLIM criteria set a higher low-BMI threshold for people aged 70 and over. || accuracy: GLIM low-BMI thresholds apply to Western populations and a GLIM diagnosis also needs an aetiological criterion; MUST scoring and mid-upper arm circumference guidance need an openable BAPEN copy; the Nicholson line is extraneous here. || sharpen: Lead with a labelled illustrative example of someone who lost 8 kg with a BMI still in the normal range, and say GLIM also needs a cause criterion.
- [   ] C13-13 total 41.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | The swallowing plan that forgets the tablets :: Swallowing assessments set food and drink textures, but tablets are a separate problem: residents with swallowing difficulty had far more medicine administration errors in a UK care home study, and crushing or mixing tablets can change how they work. || accuracy: Confirm whether 57.3% and 30.8% in Serrano Santos 2016 are per-administration error rates (small study, 166 residents); crushing and thickener interaction claims still need a pharmacy or laboratory source. || sharpen: Confirm the denominators and add one sourced example of a tablet that must not be crushed.
- [   ] C13-19 total 41.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Eating and drinking with acknowledged risks: doing it well :: When a person chooses to keep eating food that carries a swallowing risk, or when thickened drinks no longer fit their goals, a documented shared decision with practical risk reduction respects their choice and keeps care going. || accuracy: RCSLT 2021 and RCP 2021 documents not yet opened; capacity law differs between Scotland and England and Wales, so name both or neither. || sharpen: Obtain openable copies of the RCSLT and RCP guidance and add a labelled illustrative example to make the sequence concrete.
- [   ] C13-16 total 41.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Aspiration pneumonia needs more than a weak swallow :: In a prospective study of older veterans, needing help to eat and needing help with mouth care predicted aspiration pneumonia better than a swallowing problem alone. || accuracy: Langmore 1998 is observational in mostly male US veterans; dependence for oral care is a marker of risk, and the position that mouth care prevents aspiration pneumonia conflicts with the uncertain trial evidence in C13-14; odds ratios must not become 'times more likely'. || sharpen: Present feeding and mouth-care dependency as markers of risk from an observational study, and avoid claiming mouth care prevents pneumonia, given C13-14.
- [   ] C13-10 total 40.0 | both | Reading the evidence | public_explanation | risk low | Fewer calories, not less protein :: Older people often need fewer calories than when they were younger, but expert groups recommend the same or more protein per kilogram of body weight, and more during and after illness. || accuracy: PROT-AGE and ESPEN figures are expert recommendations above the UK reference intake; trial evidence that extra protein improves function in older people is mixed; 'most need fewer calories' needs the SACN source. || sharpen: Add a concrete, sourced example of protein in familiar foods and state that trial evidence for extra protein improving strength is mixed.
- [   ] C13-21 total 40.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Five days of eating little changes how feeding should start :: An older person who has eaten little or nothing for more than five days is at risk of refeeding problems, so NICE advises starting nutrition support cautiously and checking for higher-risk features. || accuracy: NICE CG32 wording and high-risk criteria need an opened copy; some later guidance (ASPEN 2020) argues the NICE criteria overidentify risk, which could be acknowledged. || sharpen: Add a recognisable geriatric admission scenario and check the CG32 wording against an opened copy.
- [   ] C13-24 total 40.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | Could a medicine be drying your mouth? :: Many medicines taken in later life reduce saliva, and a dry mouth makes chewing, swallowing, wearing dentures and tasting harder and raises the risk of tooth decay, so it is worth asking for a review. || accuracy: Tan 2018 reports associations; saliva protection and sugary-sweet decay claims need Delivering better oral health or another dental source. || sharpen: Name one or two common drug types in plain words and source the decay advice to Delivering better oral health.
- [   ] C13-01 total 38.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Count the cups, not the thirst :: Thirst is a weak guide to how much an older person needs to drink, so counting the cups they actually finish tells a carer more than asking whether they are thirsty. || accuracy: Hooper 2015 Cochrane found measured fluid intake was not useful as a single test for dehydration, so cup counting must be framed as making sure enough is drunk, not as a way to detect dehydration; ESPEN 1.6 L and 2.0 L figures need the full text; any cup count needs a sourced cup volume or an illustrative label. || sharpen: Merge with C13-20 as its public-facing version, framing the tally as a way to make sure enough is drunk rather than a dehydration test, with a sourced cup volume.
- [   ] C13-07 total 38.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | When will the swallow be checked again? :: Thickened drinks or soft food recommended during an acute illness are a snapshot; many swallowing problems improve, so each recommendation needs a review date and a named person to review it. || accuracy: Recovery data (87% back to usual diet by 6 months) are from first stroke (Mann 1999) and do not support 'many swallowing problems improve' after other acute illness; verify Robbins 2008 dehydration figures and that its population (dementia or Parkinson's) is stated. || sharpen: Limit the recovery statistic explicitly to stroke and keep the post on the review-date principle, citing RCSLT or IDDSI guidance on review if an openable copy exists.
- [   ] C13-29 total 38.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | A prescribed supplement only helps once it is drunk :: People drink more of their prescribed nutrition supplements in the community than in hospital, and more when the supplements are energy-dense; records should show what was drunk, not only what was prescribed. || accuracy: Hubbard 2012 was industry-linked and pooled research settings where supplement intake may exceed routine care; the energy-density link comes from unmatched studies; delivery tips lack a source. || sharpen: Say that 67% comes from research settings and cite any delivery tip, such as small volumes at medicine rounds, or drop it.
- [   ] C13-05 total 38.0 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Tell the ward how they eat, not only what they like :: The spouse or family member who has shared meals with an older person for years knows the details that make eating possible, and good wards ask for this knowledge and welcome carers at mealtimes if both wish. || accuracy: No source yet that family presence or help at hospital mealtimes improves intake; using Langmore 1998 (feeding dependency predicts aspiration pneumonia) to justify an eating handover is a stretch; This is me and John's Campaign wording unverified. || sharpen: Anchor the checklist to This is me or a similar opened source and drop the unsupported claim that family presence raises intake.
- [   ] C13-31 total 35.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk high | Who checks the teeth of people who can't get to a dentist? :: People living in care homes or unable to leave home often go without routine dental checks, and a national review in England found oral health often missing from care home care plans. || accuracy: The CQC 'Smiling matters' figures are unverified and the report was not openable; without them the post rests on NICE NG48 alone, which does not show a gap. || sharpen: Hold until the CQC figures are verified from an opened copy; otherwise fold the three questions into C13-25.
- [   ] C13-04 total 33.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Is there a menu choice for small appetites? :: Hospital menus are planned to meet nutrition standards when a portion is eaten, so a person who eats half of each meal gets roughly half; UK hospital food standards include energy-dense options for people at nutritional risk, and families can ask for them. || accuracy: The central mechanism (menu nutrient analysis assumes the full portion is eaten) has no source; UK hospital food standards were not openable and differ between the four nations, so 'ask for the options meant for small appetites' may not match what a given hospital offers. || sharpen: Proceed only if an openable UK food standard confirms energy-dense provision for nutritionally vulnerable patients; otherwise fold the request into C13-09 or C13-29.

## C14 Hearing, vision and the senses (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 split 7 practitioner, 6 public and 1 both, which is balanced. Series are spread across Teaching round, Reading the evidence, Small detail, Ask this, What gets counted and Weighing it up, but none is from Who gets left out or Care that works. Three of the top 14 are about cataract or glasses (C14-22, C14-09, C14-13). Consider swapping one of them for C14-30 (Deaf BSL users) or C14-03 (sight loss certification for people living alone) to cover exclusion, and C14-29 (smell) would widen the senses covered. C14-15, C14-26 and C14-19 overlap the palliative, falls and medicines categories, so check they are not also picked there.
Judge allocation reason: All 32 candidates score 36 or more with no disqualifying flag (27 still clear the bar if it is rescaled for the formula's true maximum of 55), and the top 14 cover hearing, vision, smell and balance for both audiences without duplication.
- [SEL] C14-26 total 51.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Dizzy when rolling over in bed: test for BPPV :: Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo (brief spinning set off by lying down, rolling over or looking up) is common and often unrecognised in older people, can be diagnosed with the Dix-Hallpike test, and is often treatable within minutes with a repositioning manoeuvre. || accuracy: Oghalai 2000 was one US inner-city clinic sample, so 9% is not a general prevalence and the link with falls is cross-sectional. || sharpen: Add a line on adapting the test (side-lying test or support) for people with neck or back problems, which makes it usable in frail older people.
- [SEL] C14-10 total 49.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | Sudden hearing loss in one ear needs seeing today :: Hearing that drops suddenly in one ear over hours to a few days needs same-day medical assessment, because sudden loss from the inner ear or nerve is treated as urgent and should not be assumed to be wax. || accuracy: NICE NG98 says to be seen within 24 hours by ENT or an emergency department; match that wording rather than a same-day GP appointment, and keep the uncertain benefit of steroids stated. || sharpen: Give the reader the exact sentence to say on the phone, and name ENT or the emergency department as the destination.
- [SEL] C14-20 total 49.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | Seeing things after sight loss :: People with sight loss, for example from macular degeneration, can see vivid images such as faces, patterns or people that are not there while knowing they are not real; this is Charles Bonnet syndrome, it is linked to the sight loss, and it is worth mentioning to a doctor so other causes can be considered. || accuracy: Check the '1 of 16' figure in Teunisse 1996; the 60 of 505 figure was from a visually impaired clinic population, not all people with sight loss. || sharpen: Add one sentence telling eye clinics to mention Charles Bonnet syndrome at diagnosis of sight loss, which puts the position into action.
- [SEL] C14-15 total 48.5 | public | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Is hearing the last sense to go? :: A small study of brain recordings found that many unresponsive people close to death still showed brain responses to sounds, which suggests hearing may keep working in some people near the end of life, though it does not show they understood; it is reasonable for families to keep speaking to the person. || accuracy: The EEG study was very small; check in the full text how many unresponsive patients showed each response ('most' may overstate it), and do not imply awareness or understanding. || sharpen: Give the actual numbers of patients in the study, and end on the practical line about saying who you are and keeping a hearing aid in while comfortable.
- [SEL] C14-22 total 48.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | New glasses, more falls: a trial worth knowing :: In a Sydney trial of frail older people, eye examinations with new glasses and other treatment increased falls rather than reducing them, which suggests that changes to glasses in frail people need care and a settling-in period. || accuracy: The intervention included referral for cataract surgery and other treatment, not only new glasses; adaptation to new glasses is the authors' hypothesis, and 'a smaller prescription change' is an inference the trial did not test. || sharpen: Put the Harwood cataract result beside it in the visual, so readers see that the type of vision correction made the difference, not vision correction itself.
- [SEL] C14-11 total 47.5 | both | What gets counted | teaching | risk medium | Reading your own post :: Standard measures of independence such as the Barthel Index have no item on reading, yet for a person with sight loss, reading their own letters, bank statements and medicine labels privately is part of independence that low vision services can often restore with magnifiers, lighting and reading apps. || accuracy: Low vision aids improve reading in trials, but the Cochrane review found low-certainty evidence and no clear best device, so avoid 'restore'; instrumental activity scales (Lawton) do include finances and medicines, so name the Barthel specifically. || sharpen: Add medicine labels and the privacy of bank letters to the visual, and close with the single assessment question to add.
- [SEL] C14-16 total 47.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Loud enough, still unclear :: Age-related hearing loss usually affects high-pitched sounds first, and these carry consonants such as s, f, th and t, so speech can be loud enough to hear but hard to understand; shouting does not fix this, while facing the person, getting their attention and rephrasing help. || accuracy: 'Fifteen' and 'fifty' differ mainly in the ending and stress rather than a single high-frequency consonant; use pairs that differ by s, f, th or sh, and source 'shouting distorts speech' to audiology guidance. || sharpen: Swap in word pairs that clearly turn on high-pitched consonants and show the rephrasing example in the visual.
- [SEL] C14-19 total 47.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Eye drops that slow the heart :: Beta-blocker eye drops such as timolol are absorbed into the bloodstream and can cause a slow heart rate, heart block, fainting and breathlessness, so they belong in every medicines review for bradycardia, syncope and falls. || accuracy: Case reports show that it can happen, not how often; the tissue press and tear-duct occlusion lowered plasma levels, but clinical benefit is less certain; the Müller trial is 2020, and the press duration must come from the BNF or SPC. || sharpen: Add one line on how to find the drops (ask directly, since eye drops are often missing from medicine lists), and name the non-beta-blocker alternatives in general terms.
- [SEL] C14-27 total 47.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | The vision screening that did not improve vision :: In a large UK trial of people aged 75 and over, adding vision screening to health checks did not improve eyesight three to five years later, which shows that finding eye problems helps only when treatment such as glasses or cataract surgery follows. || accuracy: The comparison arm received targeted screening, not no screening; describe it as universal versus targeted screening, and mark the reasons for the null result as the authors' interpretation. || sharpen: Name the one step most likely to fail (taking up the referral) and what a practice can do about it.
- [SEL] C14-06 total 46.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | The view from the patient's chair :: People with hearing loss rely on seeing the speaker's face, so sitting with your back to a window, turning to the screen while talking, or covering your mouth removes information they use to understand speech. || accuracy: Sumby and Pollack 1954 tested young listeners with normal hearing in noise; cite a study of older adults with hearing loss for the benefit of seeing the face, and mark the typing advice as judgement. || sharpen: Keep the two-panel patient's-eye view and add one sentence on masks and hands over the mouth so the post covers the other common way faces are hidden.
- [RES] C14-09 total 46.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | Between the first and second cataract operation :: After cataract surgery on one eye, old glasses may no longer suit that eye, which can leave vision uneven until new glasses or the second operation; an optician can often remove or change the lens on the operated side, and it helps to ask before surgery what to do. || accuracy: Advice on glasses between operations varies by surgeon and prescription, so present removing or changing the lens on the operated side as one option to ask about; the Foss 2006 falls result was not statistically significant. || sharpen: Find an NHS or RCOphth patient leaflet that states the between-eyes glasses advice, and make the pre-operative question the closing line.
- [RES] C14-13 total 46.0 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | You do not have to wait for a cataract to ripen :: NICE says access to cataract surgery should not be restricted by a letter chart threshold and that referral should depend on how the cataract affects the person's life, so people can ask about referral when cataract is interfering with driving, reading, hobbies or safety. || accuracy: NICE NG77 says not to restrict access on visual acuity, but local criteria and waits vary; Harwood 2005 studied women over 70 only; give the complication rate from the NOD audit or leave it out. || sharpen: Fold in the glare and dim-light examples from C14-04 so the reader knows what to describe at the optometrist, and say who the trial studied.
- [RES] C14-17 total 46.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Hearing aids for hearing: what ACHIEVE found about memory :: In the ACHIEVE trial, hearing aids with audiology support did not reduce cognitive decline over three years in the whole group, with a possible difference in a higher-risk subgroup, so hearing aids should be recommended for their clear benefit to hearing and communication, and any effect on dementia described as unproven. || accuracy: The ARIC-cohort finding was a prespecified sensitivity analysis of a secondary kind, not the primary result; do not quote a percentage slowing as an effect. || sharpen: Give clinicians one sentence they can use in place of the dementia argument when recommending hearing aids.
- [   ] C14-21 total 46.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Hearing music that is not playing :: Older people with hearing loss sometimes hear music, often familiar songs, when none is playing; this is linked with hearing loss, and once other causes have been looked for, first-line management is explanation and improving hearing. || accuracy: The evidence is a review of case reports and series, so prevalence and treatment effects are uncertain; the opening puts ears before every other cause, but brain disease and medicines need assessment alongside. || sharpen: Rephrase the opening so hearing assessment and medicine review come before antipsychotics rather than before 'anyone considers' other causes.
- [   ] C14-29 total 45.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | A fading sense of smell is a safety issue :: The sense of smell declines with age, so many older people may not notice gas, smoke or food that has gone off, and working alarms, date labels and timers take over the warning job. || accuracy: Santos 2004 studied smell clinic patients, not the general older population; check the 37% and 19% figures; Doty 1984 is old and measured smell identification. || sharpen: Lead with carbon monoxide, which nobody can smell, so the alarm advice holds whatever the reader's sense of smell.
- [   ] C14-30 total 45.5 | practitioners | Who gets left out | teaching | risk medium | For many Deaf people who sign, English is a second language :: Many Deaf people who use British Sign Language have English as a second language, so written notes and lip-reading are poor substitutes for a qualified BSL interpreter in health and care, and relatives should not be asked to interpret. || accuracy: Emond 2015 was a convenience sample of Deaf BSL users of all ages; the link with poor access is an inference; check whether the NHS England interpreting guidance applies beyond primary care. || sharpen: Strengthen the older-person angle with a sourced point about older Deaf people in care homes, or cut that line.
- [   ] C14-31 total 45.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | A cognitive test is also a hearing and vision test :: Cognitive screening tests are delivered by voice and on paper, so hearing or sight loss can lower scores; check the person can hear and see the items, use aids and simple adaptations, and record them with the score. || accuracy: Check the exact Dupuis 2015 finding on adjusted MoCA scores and that it applies to the MoCA only; the hearing-impaired MoCA source needs verifying. || sharpen: Make 'record the adaptations with the score' the closing instruction, and make the difference from the earlier family-facing hearing and eyes post (E006) explicit.
- [   ] C14-03 total 45.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Sight loss certification can bring help at home :: People with serious sight loss can be certified as sight impaired or severely sight impaired by an eye consultant, which can lead to a council assessment and vision rehabilitation that teaches safer ways to cook, move around and read, with equipment such as liquid level indicators and talking clocks. || accuracy: A council assessment and vision rehabilitation can be requested without certification, so the post should not imply the certificate is the only route; the CVI process and concessions differ between England and Scotland. || sharpen: Say plainly that you can also ask the council for a sensory or vision rehabilitation assessment directly, and write separate England and Scotland lines.
- [   ] C14-18 total 44.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Earwax: a fixable cause of hearing loss that has become harder to get treated :: Impacted earwax is common in older people, especially in care homes and among hearing aid users, and removing it can restore hearing; NICE recommends offering wax removal in primary or community care, but in many areas GP practices no longer provide it. || accuracy: The 'up to 57%' care home figure is an old estimate cited by AAO-HNS; the claim that many GP practices stopped wax removal needs a dated RNID or official source. || sharpen: Add what a reader can do now (ask the practice where NHS wax removal is provided locally) so it gives an action as well as a criticism.
- [   ] C14-24 total 44.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | Changes in sight that need an appointment today :: Sudden loss or blurring of sight, a shadow or curtain across vision, new flashes or a shower of floaters, straight lines that look wavy, or sight problems with a new headache and scalp tenderness need to be seen the same day rather than at the next routine sight test. || accuracy: Wavy lines from suspected wet macular degeneration need urgent referral, but the NICE NG82 timeframe may not be same day; check each symptom's recommended timeframe and pathway. || sharpen: Group the symptoms by where to go (999, eye casualty, optometrist today) instead of one 'today' list.
- [   ] C14-25 total 44.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Suspected giant cell arteritis with visual symptoms: treat today :: In suspected giant cell arteritis, especially with visual symptoms, glucocorticoids should start immediately and the person be assessed urgently by a specialist, because sight loss may be permanent, and ultrasound or biopsy can still be done after treatment starts. || accuracy: Check the BSR 2020 wording on time windows (ultrasound within about one week, biopsy within about two weeks of starting glucocorticoids) and on dosing for visual symptoms. || sharpen: Make the older-person detail the angle: vague presentations (weight loss, tiredness, jaw ache) that delay suspicion, not only the treatment order.
- [   ] C14-14 total 43.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Say who you are before you move someone :: A person with sight loss may not see a name badge, a uniform or where a wheelchair is heading, so staff who move or guide them should say their name and role, say where they are going and warn before moving, and offer an arm rather than steering. || accuracy: Check the sighted guide description against RNIB or Guide Dogs text, and give the #hellomynameis campaign a checkable source. || sharpen: Make porters, radiographers and transport staff the named readers, and cut the sight-loss prevalence claim unless a figure is verified.
- [   ] C14-05 total 43.0 | public | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Mild hearing loss is worth treating :: Trials in adults with mild to moderate hearing loss found that hearing aids likely give a large improvement in everyday listening and hearing-related quality of life, so people do not need to wait until hearing is severe to ask for help. || accuracy: The trials were in adults with mild to moderate loss, not specifically older people; check the age range in the 2026 review before addressing older readers, and explain that 'large' refers to a standardised effect size. || sharpen: Lead with a concrete everyday gain (following talk at a family meal) and leave the memory result to C14-17 if both are used.
- [   ] C14-07 total 43.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Ask them to record how you need to be contacted :: In England, NHS and adult social care services must identify, record, flag, share and meet the communication needs of people with sensory loss or disability, so you can ask your GP practice or hospital to record that you need, for example, large print, texts instead of calls, or a sign language interpreter. || accuracy: Check the current version of the Accessible Information Standard (it was reviewed after 2017) and state that it applies in England only. || sharpen: Add the equivalent route for readers in Scotland and one line on what to do if the flag is recorded but letters still arrive in small print.
- [   ] C14-23 total 41.5 | both | Who gets left out | teaching | risk medium | When hearing and sight both fade :: When a person has both hearing and sight loss, each sense can no longer make up for the other (lip-reading needs sight; getting about with poor sight relies on hearing), so the combination needs an assessment by someone who understands both. || accuracy: The 7% dual sensory figure is from a small sample (Dupuis 2015); use a population estimate or omit it, and confirm the deafblind guidance wording and its status under the Care Act. || sharpen: Open with a concrete situation (a person who can no longer lip-read because of macular degeneration) and give the exact name of the assessment to ask for.
- [   ] C14-01 total 41.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk medium | Count the hearing aids that work :: A care record that says a person has a hearing aid does not show whether it works, and a short daily check of battery, switch and programme, tubing or dome, and wax guard catches the common faults. || accuracy: Cohen-Mansfield and Taylor 2004 is one US nursing home sample over 20 years old; check the 69% and 86% denominators in the full text and present it as dated US data; the five-point daily check needs a sourced audiology leaflet. || sharpen: Anchor it in a UK care home source (for example the RNID care home work) and make the record change concrete: 'aid worked this morning: yes/no' beside 'hearing aid: yes'.
- [   ] C14-28 total 40.5 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Tell the audiologist where you want to hear better :: Some hearing services ask you to name up to five situations where you most want to hear better, such as a grandchild on the phone or a lunch club, and then judge success against those, so thinking about them before the appointment helps. || accuracy: Check that UK NHS audiology services use COSI or the Glasgow profile routinely before saying 'some services'. || sharpen: Combine with C14-02 as one 'before and after your hearing appointment' post, or make the opening a situation rather than an instruction.
- [   ] C14-12 total 39.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | Balance in the dark :: Balance uses vision, the inner ear and feeling from the feet; in the dark vision contributes little and older eyes adjust more slowly, so a light along the route to the toilet helps, especially for people with numb feet. || accuracy: No trial shows that night lights alone reduce falls; the dark-adaptation and numb-feet reasoning is physiology, and 'leaves mainly the inner ear' oversimplifies balance. || sharpen: Say more clearly how this differs from the earlier night-light advice (E072), open with the numb-feet case rather than a textbook line, and keep the claim to physiology.
- [   ] C14-32 total 39.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk high | Cataract surgery when a person has dementia :: Some people with dementia may benefit from cataract surgery through better recognition of faces, activities and safer movement, though evidence in established dementia is limited, and the decision turns on practical questions (lying still, the type of anaesthetic, weeks of eye drops, not rubbing the eye) that need planning with the person and their family. || accuracy: Evidence of cognitive or functional benefit from cataract surgery in established dementia is weak and observational; do not imply cognitive improvement, and the drop regimen and lying-still time need sourced figures. || sharpen: Base the post on the planning questions and the principle of not excluding people because of the diagnosis, and drop the cognitive-benefit claim.
- [   ] C14-08 total 39.0 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Hearing loss in a couple is shared :: Spouses of older people with hearing loss commonly report effects on their own lives, and bringing the partner into hearing care, including learning communication tactics together, helps both people. || accuracy: The 98% figure means at least one item of third-party disability, mostly mild, and must not read as nearly all spouses being badly affected; the ACE trial does not show that involving partners helps both people. || sharpen: Drop the 98% headline figure and build the post on one practical joint tactic, supported by a study that included communication partners.
- [   ] C14-02 total 38.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | The first weeks with new hearing aids :: New hearing aids often sound loud or strange at first and many problems can be fixed at a follow-up appointment, so people should go back and ask for adjustments rather than put the aids in a drawer. || accuracy: The NICE NG98 follow-up timing (6 to 12 weeks) must be checked in the guideline text, and the McCormack and Fortnum review must be confirmed in PubMed before any non-use figure is given. || sharpen: Give one verified figure on how many people stop wearing new aids, so the settling-in advice has a reason attached, and keep the question card as the visual.
- [   ] C14-04 total 38.5 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Cataract surgery and what the letter chart misses :: Cataract surgery is often judged by the letter chart, but people also care about glare, dim light, faces, steps and reading, and a short UK questionnaire asks about eyesight in daily life. || EXCLUDED: duplicate of C14-13 (kept the higher score) || accuracy: Cat-PROM5 is a research and audit tool, not something clinics routinely use to decide on referral; do not imply that it is. || sharpen: Merge its glare, dim-light and contrast detail into C14-13 rather than running a second post on why the letter chart is not enough.

## C15 Hospital care, emergency care and discharge (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: Top 14 split practitioners 5 (C15-16, 01, 11, 22, 18), both 5 (12, 14, 25, 17, 26) and public 4 (23, 07, 04, 15). The public posts cluster in ranks 8 to 14 because 'Ask this' ideas rest on guidance rather than trials, so a smaller allocation would lose public coverage first; keep at least four public posts. Within the top 14, three pairs are close in theme (12 and 23 on treatment outside hospital; 14 and 15 on emergency department waits; 18 and 26 on discharge interventions); they complement each other but should be spaced apart in the calendar. C15-19 or C15-20 can replace C15-18 or C15-26 if the discharge-trial pair feels repetitive.
Judge allocation reason: 25 of 26 candidates score 36 or more and 14 score 44.5 or more with complementary angles, sound sources and no disqualifying flags; the category is broad and central to geriatric medicine, so it can fill the top of the range.
- [SEL] C15-16 total 51.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | A normal temperature can be a fever for this person :: Older people with serious infection often do not reach standard fever thresholds, so a rise from the person's usual temperature (such as an increase of more than 1.1 degrees Celsius) or a lower repeated threshold can indicate infection even when an early warning score records the temperature as normal. || accuracy: Check the IDSA wording on how often fever is absent in serious infection (commonly quoted as 30 to 50% of frail older adults, not 'more than half'); thresholds differ by route (oral 37.8 single, 37.2 repeated; rectal 37.5); the guideline population is long-term care residents, and a baseline temperature is often unknown in the emergency department. || sharpen: Add a practical line on where to find the baseline (care home charts, GP or previous admissions) so the habit is usable at the front door.
- [SEL] C15-01 total 51.0 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | The antibiotic needs a stop date on the discharge letter :: In a large US study of adults in hospital with pneumonia, two thirds received more antibiotic days than needed, almost all the extra days came from the discharge prescription, and each extra day was linked with more side effects reported by patients. || accuracy: Vaughn 2019 cohort was all adults in 43 Michigan hospitals (not an older-only sample) and the 5% rise in odds of adverse events per excess day is observational, so no causal verb; NICE NG138 advises 5 days for low-severity CAP and a review at 5 days for moderate/high severity, so 'most adults' needs the exact wording; the C. difficile age claim is still unverified. || sharpen: State the population (adults of all ages with pneumonia, US) in one clause and show the two discharge lines side by side as the whole visual, since the wording change is the post.
- [SEL] C15-11 total 49.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | A fall from standing height can be major trauma :: In UK trauma registry data, falls from less than two metres became the commonest cause of major trauma, and older people make up a large share of major trauma patients, so a low fall in an older person should be assessed for serious injury rather than triaged by mechanism alone. || accuracy: Bulger 2000 pneumonia estimate conflicts between results (OR 1.16) and conclusion (27%): quote only the mortality OR or the direction; Kehoe 2015 figures are 2013 TARN data and newer TARN reports exist; the undertriage claim from TARN 2017 is still to check. || sharpen: Use the 1990 versus 2013 mechanism shift as the visual and close on one triage instruction (anticoagulant plus low fall means look for head and chest injury).
- [SEL] C15-12 total 47.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Hospital at home: what the UK trial found :: In a UK trial of 1,055 older people referred for emergency admission, treatment by a hospital-at-home team with comprehensive geriatric assessment gave similar rates of living at home and of death at six months as hospital admission, and fewer people were in long-term residential care. || accuracy: The residential care relative risk 0.58 is adjusted and differs from the crude 5.7% versus 8.7% (about 0.66): give the absolute figures only; randomisation was 2:1 and participants were selected, stable, mean age 83. || sharpen: Give the per 100 icon array only and add one verified sentence on carer outcomes from the trial report, so the 'what it asks of families' promise is kept.
- [SEL] C15-22 total 47.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | How many readmissions are avoidable? Studies disagree widely :: A systematic review of 34 studies found that the proportion of readmissions judged avoidable ranged from 5% to 79%, with a median of about 27%, mostly using subjective judgements at single hospitals, so a readmission rate on its own says little about the quality of a discharge. || accuracy: The 5% to 79% range and 27.1% median are from 34 mostly single-centre studies using subjective criteria; Auerbach 2016 (about 27% potentially preventable) should be checked before it is used as confirmation. || sharpen: Fill the placeholders with the verified range and median and add one example of a preventable cause a case review finds that a rate can't show.
- [SEL] C15-14 total 46.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Longer waits for a bed, more deaths within 30 days :: In English hospital data covering over five million admitted patients, longer time in the emergency department before admission was linked with higher 30-day mortality after adjustment for age, illness and crowding, rising steadily from about five hours after arrival. || accuracy: The model adjusted for comorbidity, age, deprivation and crowding, not acute illness severity, so 'adjusted for illness' overstates and residual confounding remains; the 1 in 82 figure applies to delays beyond 6 to 8 hours from arrival and the comparison group needs the full text; all adult admissions, not older people only. || sharpen: Drop 'illness' from the adjustment list, give the 1 in 82 figure with its comparison in plain words, and add the verified share of long waits experienced by older people.
- [SEL] C15-18 total 46.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | What discharge planning achieves in trials :: In a Cochrane review of 33 trials, an individual discharge plan for older people admitted with a medical condition probably shortened the initial stay by less than a day and modestly reduced readmissions over about three months, with little or no difference in mortality or function. || accuracy: Confirm that each effect estimate (length of stay -0.73 days, readmission RR 0.89, mortality RR 1.05) comes from the subgroup of older people admitted with a medical condition, not the whole review; readmission follow-up averaged three months. || sharpen: Give readmission per 100 with the control rate, and name one thing the trials show discharge also depends on (home care availability) so modest effects lead to an action.
- [SEL] C15-23 total 45.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Pneumonia in a care home: hospital, or treatment where they live? :: In a Canadian trial in 22 nursing homes, residents with pneumonia treated in the home on a clinical pathway (oral antibiotics, oxygen monitoring, fluids and close nursing review) were admitted to hospital less than half as often as those given usual care, without a clear difference in deaths. || accuracy: Loeb 2006 was Canadian, 2006, and the pathway included a research nurse and portable chest X-ray, which most UK care homes lack; hospitalisation figures are crude and the trial was not powered for mortality. || sharpen: Put 'what the home needed to make this work' on the visual as prominently as the result, so families can ask whether their home has it.
- [SEL] C15-25 total 45.5 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Older people with a learning disability in hospital :: People with a learning disability die on average many years younger than the rest of the population, and more of their deaths are judged avoidable with good health care, so a hospital stay should include the adjustments the law requires, such as a hospital passport, the learning disability liaison team and the people who know them best. || accuracy: Heslop 2014 covers south-west England deaths 2010 to 2012 and uses 'amenable to good-quality healthcare', which differs from LeDeR's 'avoidable'; median age at death was 64, so the geriatric framing must not imply these are mostly old-age deaths; the Equality Act does not extend to Northern Ireland. || sharpen: Use the latest LeDeR figures for currency, keep Heslop for the sex gap, and focus the hospital checklist on older people with a learning disability admitted with frailty.
- [SEL] C15-04 total 44.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Which of today's doses have you already had? :: On the day you leave hospital, ask which of that day's doses the ward has already given and when each medicine is next due, including any weekly tablet or patch, so that nothing is doubled or missed on the first evening at home. || accuracy: The central practical claim (the ward may already have given that day's doses; weekly tablets' day may shift) is general practice without a named source; Alqenae 2020 prevalence estimates vary widely between studies and should not be given as one number. || sharpen: Lead with the methotrexate or alendronate weekly-day example as the concrete hazard, and find one pharmacy or NICE NG5 source for recording last-dose times at transfer.
- [RES] C15-07 total 44.5 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Getting home to die: start every step on the same day :: When a person who is dying wants to go home and time is short, the discharge depends on several separate arrangements (equipment, medicines for symptoms, community nurses, the GP, care overnight and transport), and asking for them all to be started the same day gives the best chance of getting home in time. || accuracy: The NHS Continuing Healthcare Fast Track is England only (Scotland ended NHS continuing healthcare in 2015 and uses different arrangements); the claim that some people die before rapid discharge happens has no source yet; BMJ Open Qual 2024 authors and findings need the full text. || sharpen: Name the slowest usual step (equipment or community medicines) from the QI paper and give a single question per lane, with a Scotland-appropriate funding line or none.
- [RES] C15-15 total 44.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | decision_support | risk medium | On a trolley for hours: the basics still apply :: During a long wait on a trolley or chair in the emergency department, an older person still needs a drink if allowed, help to the toilet, their usual medicines on time, and help to change position, and it is reasonable to ask for each. || accuracy: Dugaret 2012 is a single French emergency department and its figures need checking; NPSA RRR009 (2010) is old though still standard; the older-people waits figure is not yet sourced. || sharpen: Keep the four labelled requests and add one sourced figure (ED pressure ulcer incidence by length of stay) so the post is more than common sense.
- [RES] C15-17 total 44.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | What comprehensive geriatric assessment does in hospital :: Across 29 trials, older people who received comprehensive geriatric assessment (a full assessment of health, function, medicines, mood and support by a specialist team) after admission were more likely to be alive and living in their own homes 3 to 12 months later, with little or no difference in deaths and fewer moves into care homes. || accuracy: RR 1.06 needs the control-group rate to give an absolute figure (roughly 3 more per 100 alive at home); most benefit came from ward-based trials, so the 'ward or team: unclear' point must follow the review wording; 2017 review, check for any update. || sharpen: Lead with the absolute number of people per 100 more likely to be at home and use that as the visual, which will also lift the opening.
- [   ] C15-26 total 44.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | A coach for the first month after discharge :: In a US trial of 750 older people, a four-week care transitions programme that prepared patients and carers to manage their medicines, keep their own health record, arrange follow-up and recognise warning signs reduced readmissions at 30 and 90 days. || accuracy: Coleman 2006 was one US health system in 2002 to 2003; the four elements come from programme materials, not the trial abstract; replications in other settings have been mixed. || sharpen: Show the 30-day and 90-day figures per 100 and name one UK or European test of the model, or say that none has been found.
- [   ] C15-09 total 43.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | What a readmission risk score can't see :: A systematic review found that most readmission risk prediction models discriminate poorly, and few include function or social circumstances, which the person and their family can often describe at the bedside. || accuracy: The 'only modestly better than chance' line fits the administrative-data models (c 0.55 to 0.65) but models used at discharge reached 0.68 to 0.83; the 2011 review predates newer scores such as HOSPITAL and machine learning models, so a later review is needed. || sharpen: Replace the c statistic with a plain ranking reading ('picks the right one of two patients about 6 times in 10') and name which kinds of score it applies to.
- [   ] C15-06 total 42.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk low | Results still awaited: name who will read them :: Many patients leave hospital with test results still to come back, and in a US study the responsible doctors were unaware of most of the potentially actionable ones, so every discharge letter should list the awaited results and name who will act on them. || accuracy: Roy 2005 is 2004 US academic-hospital data across all ages; physician unawareness (61.6%) was measured in a surveyed subset of potentially actionable results; the study did not show harm, which must be stated. || sharpen: Tie it to older people by naming the results most often pending for them (cultures, B12, thyroid, histology) and give the named-owner line as the visual.
- [   ] C15-08 total 42.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | What would let him be treated at home tonight? :: In the emergency department, the decision to admit an older person partly depends on what support is available at home, so it is reasonable to ask what would need to be in place for treatment at home, and to say clearly what you can and can't provide. || accuracy: The urgent community response two-hour standard is England only; hospital at home availability varies; Shepperd 2021 found similar living-at-home and death outcomes in selected, stable patients, so 'similar outcomes' should be limited to those. || sharpen: Keep the 'what we can and can't do tonight' two-column card, and add one sentence saying these services are not available everywhere so a 'no' is not the family's failure.
- [   ] C15-13 total 42.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | Count the bed moves :: Hospitals report admissions and length of stay but rarely the number of times one person is moved between beds and wards, although an Australian hospital study found each move was linked with higher odds of an injurious fall. || accuracy: Blay 2017 was one Australian hospital, all ages, 2008 to 2009 administrative data, with likely confounding by indication (sicker patients are moved more); 'routine NHS statistics do not report bed moves' is unverified; the night-move FOI claim has no source. || sharpen: Say in one clause that sicker people are moved more so the link is not proof, and keep the countable measure (moves per stay, moves at night) as the call to action.
- [   ] C15-19 total 42.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | 'Medically fit' needs a second sentence :: 'Medically fit for discharge' records that acute medical treatment is finished, and the note should also say what the person still needs, such as rehabilitation, a care package, equipment or a result, because many older people are less able at that point than before they became ill. || accuracy: The Loyd 2020 figure (about 30%) is a pooled prevalence of hospital-associated disability with varied definitions; 'criteria to reside' is England-specific; no source yet criticises the phrase 'medically fit', so the claim that others read it as 'well' is judgement. || sharpen: Show the two note lines as the whole visual and mark the reading by families as the author's judgement, not a finding.
- [   ] C15-20 total 42.5 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | Delayed discharge is counted in bed days :: Delayed discharge is reported mainly as bed days, a measure of the hospital's capacity, while a mixed-studies review found it is linked with infections, depression, loss of mobility and daily activities, and death for the person who is waiting. || accuracy: Rojas-Garcia 2018 associations come mostly from moderate or low quality studies, and people who are delayed tend to be frailer, so confounding is likely; the professional-body criticism of 'bed blocking' is not yet sourced. || sharpen: Put one plain sentence on confounding next to the list of harms and find the BGS or RCP statement before naming the term.
- [   ] C15-03 total 42.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Ten days in bed, ten years of ageing: what was measured :: The slogan that ten days in a hospital bed ages an older person's muscles by ten years points in the right direction, but the best-known bed-rest study measured something narrower: in 11 healthy volunteers with an average age of 67, ten days of continuous bed rest reduced leg strength and aerobic capacity substantially, while simple physical performance tests did not change. || accuracy: Kortebein studied 11 healthy volunteers, and acutely ill patients may lose more, so the slogan may understate as well as overstate; the ~1 kg lean leg mass loss (about 6%) can be converted into years of age-related loss, so 'an equivalence nobody measured' may be wrong; attribution of the slogan to Gill 2004 is unverified; it also sits awkwardly against the earlier E131 equivalence claim. || sharpen: Frame it as 'what the ten days in bed study actually measured, in healthy volunteers' and say plainly that ill patients are likely to fare worse, so the correction strengthens rather than weakens the mobility message.
- [   ] C15-10 total 40.5 | both | What gets counted | public_explanation | risk high | When does the waiting clock start? :: Different official emergency waiting measures start their clocks at different points, so a person's experienced wait, from the call or arrival to a ward bed, can be much longer than a headline figure suggests; knowing which clock a figure uses helps readers interpret it. || accuracy: NHS England has published 12 hours from arrival monthly since 2023, so 'has been counted' must be dated; official definitions pages could not be opened from this environment; ambulance handover time and Scottish definitions need official documents; not specific to older people. || sharpen: Build it on one opened official document per clock (NHS England and PHS) and add one line on why older people are more exposed to the longest waits, or drop it.
- [   ] C15-05 total 38.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk high | Is the home care being kept open? :: If someone had home carers before going into hospital, the family can ask early whether that care is being held open and for how long, because care that is released during the admission may have to be arranged again before discharge. || accuracy: Hold periods and restart processes vary by council, provider and nation (England and Scotland differ), and no published source yet supports the general claim; in some areas packages are held or restarted automatically, so the post could mislead. || sharpen: Find one official or Healthwatch source that describes packages being released during admission, and phrase the rule as 'varies by area: ask your council' rather than implying a standard period.
- [   ] C15-02 total 37.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | The four answers a care home needs before the first night :: When a resident returns from hospital, care home staff need to know how the person now moves, what they can eat and drink, which medicines are next due and at what time, and what to do if they get worse overnight, so the discharge information should answer those four questions directly. || accuracy: The four headings are editorial judgement, not a published standard; NICE NG27 wording on sharing information with the care home at the time of discharge needs checking; the CQC and qualitative sources on incomplete information are not yet found. || sharpen: Anchor it to one sourced finding about what care home staff report missing at transfer, and cut the opening to one short sentence before the four questions.
- [   ] C15-21 total 36.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Sent home from A&E: ask about the next step :: Older people who go home from the emergency department have higher rates of return visits, admission and other adverse outcomes (including loss of function in some studies) in the following months than younger adults, so before leaving it helps to ask what was found, what should prompt a return, and whether any follow-up has been arranged. || accuracy: Aminzadeh and Dalziel 2002 is an old review and emergency department interventions were inconclusive; PROFET (1999) findings were not consistently replicated in later trials, so it should not be presented as settled. || sharpen: Replace the 2002 review with a recent systematic review or cohort of adverse outcomes after ED discharge in older people, and keep only the questions that evidence supports.
- [   ] C15-24 total 35.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Ask for the planned discharge date early :: Many hospitals set an expected date of discharge early in a stay, and asking for it, and for what has to happen before it, lets families plan transport, time off work, the home and support, rather than hearing about discharge on the day. || accuracy: SAFER bundle wording on expected discharge date needs checking; Healthwatch 2015 is dated; Carers (Scotland) Act section 28 duty wording is unverified and overlaps C18-16. || sharpen: Merge with C15-05 into a single 'questions to ask in the first two days' post, or give it a sourced figure on how little notice families report.

## C16 Primary care, home care and care homes (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: Among the top 14, six are for practitioners (C16-01, 10, 11, 15, 17, 27), six for both (04, 16, 20, 21, 23, 28) and only two for the public (07, 26). To lift the public share, swap C16-06 (public, 43.5) and possibly C16-13 (public, 42.5, after evidence checks) in for C16-16 or C16-27. Four of the top 14 are about medicines (11, 17, 20, 21) and should be checked against C06. Only C16-15 is from the Care that works series, so consider C16-14 or C16-29. C16-23 (CPR decisions), C16-14 (long lies), C16-10 (discharge) and C16-08/C16-26 (access and digital exclusion) overlap with C19, C01, C15 and C17 and should be coordinated across categories.
Judge allocation reason: Fourteen candidates score 44.5 or more with sound evidence routes and no disqualifying flag, and 27 of 29 clear the strength bar (36 of 50, or about 40 on the formula's true maximum of 55), so the set supports the full 14.
- [SEL] C16-17 total 51.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Before a tablet goes into the yoghurt :: Giving medicines hidden in food or drink to a person who lacks capacity to decide about them needs a capacity assessment, a best-interests decision with the family and prescriber, pharmacist advice for each medicine, a written plan and regular review; the first question is whether each medicine is still needed. || accuracy: In Scotland covert medication sits under the Adults with Incapacity Act with a section 47 certificate and the Mental Welfare Commission guide, which may have been updated since 2017; the Kirkevold 2005 prevalence figures are old Norwegian data. || sharpen: State the Scottish legal route explicitly alongside NICE SC1 and keep the crushing warning to one named example (modified-release tablets).
- [SEL] C16-11 total 48.0 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | The tablet stopped in hospital that came back next month :: After a hospital stay, a medicine the hospital stopped can reappear weeks later in a care home or at home if the GP repeat list and the next pharmacy order are not updated. || accuracy: The repeat-list mechanism is plausible and should be labelled as an illustrative sequence, not a measured frequency; the Discharge Medicines Service readmission findings come from service evaluations, not trials. || sharpen: Add one named fix in a single line (who updates the repeat list, and by when, after the discharge letter arrives) and cite a study showing discharge medicine changes not carried into primary care records.
- [SEL] C16-07 total 46.5 | public | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk medium | Who comes to the door when the alarm is pressed? :: Many monitored personal alarm services rely on named responders who live nearby, so people with nobody close should ask before signing up whether the service has its own responders. || accuracy: Responder rules vary by council and provider and there may be no national source, so the key premise rests on service pages; Fleming and Brayne studied people aged 90 and over in Cambridge, so do not generalise their figures to all older people. || sharpen: Find one checkable national source (Age UK or the Telecare Services Association) that describes responder requirements, and add what someone with nobody nearby can ask for (a mobile response service, and whether it costs extra).
- [SEL] C16-21 total 46.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | When a blister pack helps, and when it gets in the way :: Pharmacy blister packs and dosette boxes help some people take their medicines, but evidence of benefit is limited, some medicines are unsuitable for them and changes mid-month are harder; guidance advises using them to solve a specific problem after assessing why doses are missed. || accuracy: NICE CG76 and NG67 wording on compliance aids must be quoted exactly; the RPS 2013 statement is older and should be checked for any update. || sharpen: Lead with the 'why are doses being missed?' question so the post reads as a refinement of E169 rather than a contradiction of it.
- [SEL] C16-28 total 46.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Reablement: free for six weeks, and under-studied :: Reablement is short-term help at home to relearn everyday tasks after illness or injury; in England it is free for up to six weeks, and although trial evidence is limited, people offered it can ask what goals it will work on and what happens at the end. || accuracy: Check whether the 2016 Cochrane review has been updated; the English rule is that councils must not charge for the first six weeks, and Scotland's arrangements differ; Tuntland 2015 effects were strongest at early follow-up. || sharpen: Phrase the charging rule exactly with the Scottish position noted, and make 'ask for your goals in writing and a review at the end' the closing instruction.
- [SEL] C16-01 total 46.0 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Write the care plan for the 3am reader :: An anticipatory care plan for a care home resident or housebound person is most useful when an out-of-hours clinician who has never met them can find it and act on it within minutes. || accuracy: The 'more than two thirds' figure is correct arithmetic for hours (115.5 of 168 outside 08:00 to 18:30 weekdays) but hours are not demand, and England's enhanced access adds some evening and Saturday GP cover; the Scottish core-hours definition and any claim about when care home admissions start need their own source. || sharpen: Build the checklist around the three things an out-of-hours reader needs in the first minute (what to do if unwell, what the person would not want, whom to phone) and drop the admissions-timing claim unless a source gives a figure.
- [SEL] C16-10 total 46.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Don't decide on a care home at the lowest point :: Judging whether someone needs a permanent care home while they are still in an acute hospital bed measures them near their weakest point, and guidance favours assessing long-term needs after a period of recovery outside hospital. || accuracy: Boyd et al. 2008 found that many people did not recover to their pre-admission function within a year, so the recovery claim must be given with the non-recovery figure; the English discharge guidance was revised in 2022 and 2024 and must be quoted exactly; the interim-placement drift claim is unverified. || sharpen: State Boyd 2008's recovery and non-recovery proportions side by side so the post neither promises recovery nor dismisses it, and quote the discharge guidance wording.
- [SEL] C16-20 total 46.0 | both | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Parkinson's doses and the care rota :: Many Parkinson's medicines need to be taken at set times, and a home care visit schedule or care home medicines round that does not match those times can leave people stiff, slow and at higher risk of falls and swallowing difficulty. || accuracy: Parkinson's UK's 'within 30 minutes' advice and the NICE NG71 wording on care homes must be verified; the claim that home care visit times often do not match dose times needs a source or should be framed as a check to make. || sharpen: Verify the timing advice and add one concrete home care fix, such as booking the morning visit to the first dose time.
- [SEL] C16-23 total 46.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk low | A DNACPR decision is about one treatment :: A DNACPR decision covers only whether to attempt CPR if the heart stops; it does not mean a care home resident should not be treated for an infection, seen by a GP or taken to hospital when that would help, and blanket decisions for groups of residents are wrong. || accuracy: Quote the current edition of the BMA, Resuscitation Council UK and RCN guidance; keep to what CQC 2021 found (concerns about blanket decisions and poor involvement) without implying a prevalence. || sharpen: Coordinate with C19; if it stays here, focus on care home residents specifically and add the question a family can ask when a form appears.
- [SEL] C16-15 total 45.0 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | A care worker's observation is clinical information :: Care workers who see a person every day often notice early signs of illness, such as eating less, sleeping more or being unlike their usual self, and structured tools help them hand these observations over so that a nurse or GP acts. || accuracy: RESTORE2 evaluations are largely uncontrolled; Kane 2017 did not show a significant overall reduction in admissions, and the larger effect in high-engagement homes is an exploratory subgroup. || sharpen: Put the feedback step (the care worker hears what was decided) at the centre of the post and state the INTERACT trial result in plain words.
- [RES] C16-04 total 44.5 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | How many different people came through the door this fortnight? :: The number of different care workers a person sees over two weeks is a simple, countable measure of continuity; Sweden reports one nationally (to verify), and UK services rarely publish anything like it. || accuracy: The Socialstyrelsen indicator and the national figure (thought to be around 15 to 16 staff in 14 days) must be verified from a citable document, which may be hard because official web pages could not be opened; 'the UK publishes nothing like it' is an absence claim that needs to be phrased as 'we found no national measure'. || sharpen: Verify the current Swedish indicator and figure from a report or peer-reviewed paper before drafting, and keep the calendar grids clearly labelled as illustrative.
- [RES] C16-16 total 44.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Seeing the same GP :: Seeing the same GP over time is linked with fewer avoidable hospital admissions and lower mortality in large observational studies, so it is reasonable for an older person with ongoing problems to ask to see the same doctor. || accuracy: Barker 2017 is cross-sectional and the mortality review and Norwegian study are observational, so no causal verbs; give absolute figures where the papers allow. || sharpen: Open with the request the reader can make ('You can ask to see the same GP each time') and give one plain-number finding from Barker 2017.
