- [RES] C16-26 total 44.5 | public | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk medium | Housebound and missing from the list :: People who can no longer get to the surgery can miss routine care such as vaccines, medicine reviews, blood tests and sight tests unless the practice knows they are housebound and arranges care at home. || accuracy: Home sight test eligibility as described is England-specific (Scotland has free eye examinations for all and its own domiciliary rules); Ornstein 2015 is US data; the UK preventive-care gap for housebound people is unverified and official NHS pages could not be opened. || sharpen: Add the Scottish position on home eye examinations and either find a UK source on housebound patients' routine care or cut that claim.
- [   ] C16-27 total 44.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Proactive care and the admissions promise :: Trials of case management for older people in primary care have not reliably reduced hospital admissions, while some community interventions may help people stay independent, so these services are better judged by function, independence and people's experience. || accuracy: Crocker 2024 graded most findings as low or very low certainty and its benefit was on living at home for some combinations only; Stokes 2015 reported secondary care use and some other outcomes, which should be stated accurately. || sharpen: State the certainty of the Crocker 2024 findings in plain words and name one outcome a practice could measure instead of admissions avoided.
- [   ] C16-06 total 43.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | The first question to ask a care home :: When choosing a care home, ask how residents see a GP, a dentist and therapists such as physiotherapy, speech and language therapy and dietetics, and how long that usually takes. || accuracy: The Enhanced Health in Care Homes weekly 'home round' can be a remote check-in, and alignment does not mean every home meets the framework; the framework is England-only and Scotland's differs; the NHS England documents may not be openable. || sharpen: Keep this as a family question card, separate from C16-29, and add what a good answer sounds like (a named practice, a regular clinical check, a stated route to therapists and a dentist).
- [   ] C16-18 total 43.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | What 'potentially avoidable' admissions from care homes means :: Figures for 'potentially avoidable' emergency admissions from care homes are based on diagnosis groups; they show where services could improve, and they give no judgement on whether a particular resident should have gone to hospital. || accuracy: The proportion (thought to be about 41 in 100) must be taken from Wolters et al. 2019 with its data year; the classification combines ambulatory care sensitive and other diagnosis groups and involved no case review. || sharpen: Give the verified proportion, then one clearly labelled illustrative case of a resident whose admission fell in a 'potentially avoidable' group and was still right for them.
- [   ] C16-02 total 42.5 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | The visit was on time; the bedtime was not their choice :: Home care monitoring usually records whether a visit happened and how long it lasted, while people receiving care also value when they get up and go to bed and who comes, and those are seldom reported. || accuracy: The EHRC 2011 and CQC 2013 reports are old and must be checked for specific findings on early bedtime visits; the ASCOF 'control over daily life' measure covers all social care users, not home care alone. || sharpen: Anchor the post in one verified source that documents early bedtime visits and end on the exact question to raise at a care review about the time of the evening visit.
- [   ] C16-13 total 42.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk high | Settling in: does the family need to stay away? :: Families are sometimes advised to stay away for the first days or weeks after a relative moves into a care home; the evidence for this is weak, and families can agree visiting with the person and the home. || accuracy: 'No good-quality evidence' needs a search of relocation-adjustment reviews to support it as an evidence gap; the premise that homes advise staying away needs a source; Regulation 9A allows restriction in some circumstances; the status and wording of Anne's Law in Scotland (Standards amended 2022, later legislation) must be checked. || sharpen: Confirm first that reviews show no evidence for staying away and that the advice is actually given; otherwise reframe as 'agree visiting around what the person wants, before the move'.
- [   ] C16-19 total 42.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | The eFI finds people to review :: The electronic Frailty Index counts coded problems in a GP record to sort a practice population; because it depends on what has been coded, it finds people to review but should not be recorded or quoted as a diagnosis of frailty without a clinical assessment. || accuracy: 'Moderate agreement' needs a figure from a named validation study (Brundle 2019 or similar); the eFI was developed to predict outcomes in populations; the contract wording on frailty identification must be checked. || sharpen: Give the agreement result in plain words from a named study and keep clear of C05's Clinical Frailty Scale posts.
- [   ] C16-14 total 42.0 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | After a fall in a care home: check, then lift :: When a care home resident falls without signs of serious injury, trained staff using a structured check and lifting equipment can help them up safely instead of leaving them on the floor waiting for an ambulance; when the check finds warning signs, they call for help and do not lift. || accuracy: I STUMBLE items must be copied verbatim from the ambulance service source; evaluations of lifting equipment and post-fall tools are mostly local before-and-after data; long-lie harms are associations; residents with dementia may not report pain, which makes 'no injury' harder to judge. || sharpen: Coordinate with the C01 long-lie posts and keep this one strictly about care home equipment, training and the 'do not lift' rules, with the tool quoted exactly.
- [   ] C16-24 total 42.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | What NICE says home care should look like :: NICE guidance for England sets out what people receiving home care can expect, including visits long enough to do the work without rushing, visits under half an hour only in specific circumstances, and continuity of care workers. || accuracy: NICE NG21 is England-only guidance and not a legal entitlement; the short-visit conditions must be quoted exactly. || sharpen: Merge the short-visit rule into C16-02, or give this post a position of its own; as a standalone summary of guidance it lacks an argument.
- [   ] C16-12 total 41.5 | public | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Reading survival figures after a move into a nursing home :: Survival figures for people who move into nursing homes are averages across a group with a very wide spread; they do not tell a family how long their relative will live, and figures differ between nursing and residential homes. || accuracy: Forder and Fernandez 2011 used older cohorts and stays may have shortened since; the higher mortality of care home residents reflects who moves in and must not be presented as an effect of the home. || sharpen: Use a verified distribution from a recent UK linked-data study if one exists, and end on the question to ask the clinician about this person.
- [   ] C16-29 total 41.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | What good health care in a care home includes :: Care home residents benefit from a named GP practice with regular visits, a wider team (nurses, pharmacists, therapists), medicine reviews, planning ahead with residents and support for staff; evaluations of this model found fewer emergency admissions in some areas and not in others. || accuracy: Rushcliffe effects must be quoted from Lloyd et al. 2017 and attributed to the package, not to each component; other vanguard sites showed smaller or no differences. || sharpen: Differentiate from C16-06 by focusing on why results varied between vanguard sites, with verified figures from the Improvement Analytics Unit reports.
- [   ] C16-03 total 41.0 | practitioners | Who gets left out | teaching | risk medium | Tell the home care workers the plan :: When someone is dying at home with home care, the care workers who visit several times a day need to know what changes to expect, whom to call day and night, and what is already in place, such as a ReSPECT or DNACPR form or just-in-case medicines. || accuracy: The evidence leads (2010 to 2013) are marked unsure; the link between uninformed care workers and unwanted 999 calls needs a source or must be phrased as a risk, and ReSPECT is not used everywhere. || sharpen: Replace the old, unsure leads with a recent review or UK study of home care workers in end-of-life care, and make the four-item 'share this with the agency' list the visual centrepiece.
- [   ] C16-08 total 41.0 | practitioners | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Test every way into the practice on the person with the least help :: A GP practice can check its access by trying each route as an older person with no internet, poor hearing and nobody to phone for them would; routes that work for that person work for most people. || accuracy: The share of people aged 75 and over who do not use the internet must come from a current Ofcom or ONS release; GP contract wording on access routes changed in 2025 and must be quoted exactly; overlaps with C17 digital exclusion. || sharpen: Replace the composite 85-year-old with a named, repeatable test (ring at 08:00 with a hearing aid out, no online form) and verify the current non-user figure.
- [   ] C16-09 total 41.0 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Home with visits, or a care home: write both lists :: Staying at home with care visits and moving into a care home each give something and take something away, and the older person's own priorities should decide how they are weighed. || accuracy: The decision belongs to the person only when they have capacity for it, otherwise a best-interests process applies; funding rules can constrain options; outcome comparisons between settings are confounded. || sharpen: Give each list three verifiable items and add the capacity point in one line so readers know who decides when the person cannot.
- [   ] C16-25 total 40.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Ask for a longer appointment :: People with several long-term conditions can ask for a longer GP appointment when they book, and can bring a written list with the most important problem first and an up-to-date list of medicines. || accuracy: The Irving 2017 UK consultation length is based on older data; NICE NG56 wording on longer appointments must be checked; the 3D trial population included adults of all ages with multimorbidity. || sharpen: Lead with the 3D trial finding (better experience of care, no difference in quality of life), which is a more interesting and defensible hook than generic appointment advice.
- [   ] C16-22 total 38.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | The bag that goes to hospital and comes back :: A standard transfer bag that goes with a care home resident to hospital, carrying their medicines, their details and personal items such as glasses, hearing aids and dentures, and returns with the discharge information, keeps information and belongings together. || accuracy: Reported shorter stays come from uncontrolled Sutton data; current use of the Red Bag varies and some areas have stopped, so it must not be presented as a current national standard. || sharpen: Check whether the scheme is still in use before presenting it as a model, and consider folding it into a care home to hospital transfer post in C15.
- [   ] C16-05 total 38.0 | both | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Which tasks does the person keep? :: In care homes and home care, taking over tasks a person can still do with time or a little help may reduce their remaining abilities, so a care plan should name the tasks the person keeps doing. || accuracy: The dependence-support script comes from observational studies from the 1980s and 1990s, and 'doing for' as a cause of lost ability is not established by trials; function-focused care trials show modest and mixed effects. || sharpen: Drop the causal implication, lead with the care plan question, and cite one trial or review of function-focused care with its modest results stated.

## C17 Loneliness, relationships, housing and poverty (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 by score are 8 practitioner, 2 both and 4 public posts, and pensioner poverty is thin (only C17-17 and the money questions in C17-07); swap one or two of the cross-category practitioner items (C17-10, C17-27 or C17-21) for C17-05 (both, transport) and a verified public money post (C17-18), and run only one of the cold-weather pair (C17-03 or C17-20) alongside the heat post C17-24.
Judge allocation reason: 26 of 28 candidates reach 36 or more with no disqualifying flag and 15 reach 42 or more, most with low evidence risk, so the category supports the top of the range even if C17-21, C17-27 and C17-10 are ceded to C15, C01 and C02.
- [SEL] C17-15 total 48.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Where the '15 cigarettes a day' line comes from :: The claim that loneliness is as harmful as smoking 15 cigarettes a day comes from comparing effect sizes across observational studies of social relationships; it overstates certainty, and loneliness deserves attention without it. || accuracy: The 2010 comparison was with smoking fewer than 15 cigarettes a day and used a composite of social relationship measures, not loneliness; the 2015 meta-analysis did find loneliness linked with mortality in adjusted studies (OR 1.26), so the ELSA result must sit beside it rather than stand as the answer. || sharpen: Show the three findings side by side in plain words (2010 composite of social relationships, 2015 loneliness after adjustment, ELSA loneliness not independent) and close with the reason to act that needs no slogan: loneliness is distressing.
- [SEL] C17-22 total 48.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | criticism | risk low | 'Did not attend' is a finding :: Repeatedly missing appointments is more common in the oldest and the poorest and is linked with higher mortality, so it should prompt a question about why rather than a discharge letter. || accuracy: RRR values come from a multinomial model against reference groups that must be confirmed, and adults aged 16 to 30 were also at high risk, so older age should not be presented as unique; the mortality link is a risk marker, not a cause. || sharpen: Mention young adults as the other high-risk group, make the close 'phone before you discharge', and add one line respecting a person's choice not to attend.
- [SEL] C17-16 total 47.0 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Phone calls that listen :: In a randomised trial, regular phone calls from briefly trained young callers who focused on listening reduced loneliness, depression and anxiety within four weeks in homebound adults. || accuracy: The change was modest (1.1 points on a 3 to 9 scale), one of two loneliness scales did not show a clear difference, follow-up was 4 weeks during the pandemic and 37% of participants were under 65; the 'same caller' and 'person leads' features must be confirmed in the full text before they appear on the visual. || sharpen: Lead with the young callers and the call schedule, state the 4-week limit and the modest size of the change in plain words, and name what a befriending service could copy.
- [SEL] C17-07 total 46.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | The partner left at home :: When one partner moves into a care home, the partner at home often stays as distressed as before and has money questions of their own, so they need their own conversation. || accuracy: Schulz 2004 is a US study of dementia carers; the England home and pension disregard rules (half of an occupational or personal pension passed to a spouse at home) must be verified from the statutory guidance, and Scottish rules stated separately or omitted. || sharpen: Keep the money points as questions to ask the council's financial assessment team unless the statutory guidance is verified, and name that team as the place to ask.
- [SEL] C17-19 total 45.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Sexual health belongs in the history and the medicines review :: Many people in their 70s and 80s are sexually active, sexual problems are common, and most older people have not discussed sex with a doctor, so clinicians need to ask. || accuracy: Lindau data are from 2005 to 2006 in the US; 26% of people aged 75 to 85 were sexually active, so 'many' should be 'about 1 in 4'; the medicine side-effect claim needs a named source such as the BNF. || sharpen: Use natural frequencies ('about 1 in 4 people aged 75 to 85'), cite a named source for the medicines, and give the exact wording of one opening question for clinic.
- [SEL] C17-21 total 45.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | criticism | risk low | 'Social admission' is where assessment starts :: Older people labelled 'social admission' or 'failure to cope' often have an acute medical problem, and the label is linked with delays and missed diagnoses. || accuracy: One Canadian centre with 60 labelled patients; the 88% and delay figures must be matched to the full text; HR 3.06 for missed diagnosis and 180-day death is an association in a trauma cohort and must not become 'three times more likely to die'. || sharpen: Show the 60-person icon array, add one line naming the commonest missed diagnoses from the full text without singling out delirium, and check fit against C15.
- [SEL] C17-27 total 45.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Handrails on the front step: reading a home repair trial :: Low-cost home repairs (handrails, grab rails, outdoor lighting, non-slip surfaces) reduced injuries from falls at home in a New Zealand trial, but the headline figure depends on the analysis and the trial included people of all ages. || accuracy: Participants were of all ages in low-income households, so any older-person effect needs the full-text age breakdown; the crude comparison (relative rate 0.86, CI 0.66 to 1.12) did not show a clear difference and must be stated as plainly as the adjusted 26%. || sharpen: Give both results side by side as injuries per 100 people per year, state the trial population in the first sentence, and check fit against C01 and E068.
- [SEL] C17-02 total 44.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | What would have to change for home to work? :: When a family is told a parent is not safe to go home from hospital, the useful response is to ask which specific problem makes it unsafe and what equipment, adaptation or support would change that. || accuracy: The free minor adaptations rule (up to 1,000 pounds, England) is in the Care and Support (Preventing Needs for Care and Support) Regulations 2014, not the charging regulations cited; the NHS discharge guidance wording must be verified from a copy; the 46% and 57% Scottish figures come from different periods and definitions, so use one. || sharpen: Cut to one Scottish figure, add the question 'Can we try home with this support and review it in a few weeks?', and say plainly that a care home can be the right answer when the named problems cannot be solved.
- [SEL] C17-04 total 44.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | The first six months after a partner dies :: The rise in risk of death after losing a spouse is largest in the first six months and clearer in men, so the surviving partner needs attention early, including the tasks the person who died used to do. || accuracy: Verify the sex-specific estimates (a women's RR of 1.04 with CI 1.00 to 1.08 is borderline and should be called small rather than absent); relative risks need a plain framing in words, and loss of practical care as the mechanism is a hypothesis, not a finding. || sharpen: Describe the risk in words rather than as RR labels on the visual, and make the checklist (who now does the meals, medicines, money, driving and appointments) the centrepiece.
- [SEL] C17-12 total 44.5 | public | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk high | Who is in your next of kin box? :: The next of kin entry on a health record can be anyone you choose, including a friend or neighbour, but it gives them no legal power to make decisions for you; that needs a power of attorney. || accuracy: Next of kin gives no decision-making authority for adults in UK healthcare, but Scotland's Adults with Incapacity Act 'nearest relative' and mental health 'named person' roles exist, so 'no legal role' is too broad; the Stonewall 2011 figures are unverified. || sharpen: Add one line on Scotland's 'nearest relative' role and end on two concrete actions: tell the ward who you want contacted, and arrange a power of attorney while you are able to.
- [RES] C17-24 total 44.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | In a heatwave, check on the person who lives alone :: In past heatwaves, the older people most likely to die were those who lived alone, did not leave home each day, were confined to bed, or slept on a top floor, so a daily check and a cooler room are practical protection. || accuracy: In Semenza 1996 the highest odds were for being confined to bed and not leaving home each day; living alone had a smaller odds ratio, so 'among the strongest' overstates it; both studies date from 1995 and 2003, and air conditioning findings do not transfer directly to UK homes. || sharpen: Order the risk factors by the study's own odds ratios, add one UK-relevant practical step (curtains closed on the sunny side, water within reach, a cooler room downstairs), and schedule for early summer.
- [RES] C17-01 total 44.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk low | Living alone is recorded; loneliness is not asked :: Living alone, the number of visitors and feeling lonely are different things, and only asking the person finds the loneliness. || accuracy: Confirm from the full text whether 18% is the share of the whole sample living alone or the share of lonely people who lived alone before pairing it with 43%; do not use mortality prediction to rank loneliness above living alone, since in ELSA (Steptoe 2013) isolation, not loneliness, stayed linked with death after adjustment. || sharpen: Lead with the paper's own cross-tabulation of lonely people by living arrangement and print the three short-scale questions verbatim on the card.
- [RES] C17-10 total 43.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Ask where they have been in the last four weeks :: Life-space assessment records how far, how often and with what help a person has moved beyond their bedroom, home, neighbourhood and town in the past four weeks, and lower scores are linked with higher mortality. || accuracy: In Peel 2005 physical function and performance explained most of the variance in life-space, with sociodemographic factors adding 13%, so the claim that life-space picks up transport, money and confidence is plausible rather than shown; the mortality link is observational. || sharpen: Give one life-space question a clinician can ask in clinic, and present 'a shrinking life-space should prompt questions about pain, confidence, transport and money' as a clinical judgement; check fit against C02.
- [   ] C17-17 total 43.0 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Welfare advice in the GP surgery :: Welfare rights advice offered in health settings consistently increases people's income, while evidence on health outcomes is weaker because the studies are small and of low quality. || accuracy: The 14 studies covered adults of all ages, not older people specifically, and the 27 pounds per 1 pound social return is a modelled estimate built on low-quality studies; omit it or label it as modelled. || sharpen: Say that the studies included all ages, and give one concrete referral route (council welfare rights or a Citizens Advice service in the practice) instead of the modelled return.
- [   ] C17-05 total 42.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Stopping driving: plan what replaces the car :: Stopping driving is linked with depressive symptoms, less social activity and poorer health, so the decision should come with a plan for how the person will get to shops, friends and appointments. || accuracy: Do not turn OR 1.91 into 'twice as likely'; the pooled studies are observational and people often stop driving because their health is already declining, so the links with depression and care home admission may reflect the reason for stopping. || sharpen: Open with the practical question 'How will you get to the shop, your friends and the GP?' and add one verified UK transport option such as concessionary travel or community transport.
- [   ] C17-26 total 41.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Social prescribing for loneliness: an evidence gap :: Social prescribing is widely used for loneliness, but UK evaluations have mostly been small, uncontrolled and at high risk of bias, which is an evidence gap rather than proof that it does not work. || accuracy: The 2017 review is nine years old and needs a recent systematic review beside it; Masi 2011's finding on social cognition rests on few trials and later reviews are less clear; neither review is specific to older people. || sharpen: Add a post-2020 systematic review and give link workers one concrete outcome to record, such as a short loneliness scale before and after referral.
- [   ] C17-28 total 41.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | New social withdrawal: risk factor or early sign? :: Less social contact is linked with later dementia, but withdrawal from friends can also reflect early cognitive change, low mood or hearing loss, so new withdrawal in later life needs assessment as well as encouragement. || accuracy: The 2024 Lancet Commission report has not yet been read; the Whitehall II association (HR 0.88 per SD) was for contact with friends and is observational; the reverse-causation wording must be taken from the paper itself. || sharpen: Name exactly what to check (mood, a hearing test, a memory history from someone who knows the person) and read the Lancet Commission report before quoting it; keep clear of earlier dementia and hearing posts (E005, E006, E019).
- [   ] C17-25 total 41.0 | practitioners | Who gets left out | teaching | risk medium | Ask who is important before you ask about marriage :: Assessment questions that assume a spouse or children miss the people who are closest to many older adults, including partners, friends and chosen family. || accuracy: The Stonewall 2011 findings and the claim that LGB older people rely more on services are unverified; the CQC 2019 guidance title and content need checking. || sharpen: Anchor the post in one verified source on older people without a partner or children, and show where the two questions belong on a real admission or care home form; pair or merge with C17-12 rather than running both close together.
- [   ] C17-08 total 40.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk high | The terminal illness benefits form is a clinical task :: Fast-track disability benefit claims for terminally ill people depend on a short form from a doctor or nurse, and the criteria differ between Scotland and the rest of the UK. || accuracy: Form names, the 12-month threshold and its dates (April 2022 for Universal Credit and ESA, April 2023 for PIP, DLA and Attendance Allowance) and the Scottish BASRiS criteria all rest on official pages that could not be opened; the Marie Curie poverty figure is unsourced and should be cut. || sharpen: Hold until the DWP and Social Security Scotland guidance can be verified from an opened copy, then lead with the outdated six-month belief and keep the two-column card.
- [   ] C17-20 total 40.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Winter deaths follow the house as well as the weather :: Countries with milder winters and poorly insulated housing had higher excess winter mortality, and among people aged 75 and over in Britain, winter risk was not confined to the poorer, so protection from indoor cold is needed for all older people. || accuracy: Healy 2003 is an ecological comparison of countries in the 1990s, so 'housing counts more than climate' is an association between countries, not a measure of individual risk; Wilkinson found little variation by deprivation in people aged 75 and over, which is not evidence that fuel poverty has no effect. || sharpen: Describe Healy as a comparison of countries in the 1990s and turn the insight into actions (heat the room you sit in; ask about insulation and draught-proofing help); choose this or C17-03, not both.
- [   ] C17-11 total 40.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk high | Proxy access instead of a shared password :: A family member can be given their own proxy access to a person's online GP services, at a level the person chooses, instead of using the person's login. || accuracy: All key claims rest on NHS England proxy-access guidance that could not be opened; arrangements in Scotland and Wales differ; proxy access for a person who lacks capacity has specific conditions that must not be simplified. || sharpen: Verify from an opened copy of the guidance, add a one-line Scotland and Wales caveat, and open with the security problem of logging in with a parent's password.
- [   ] C17-09 total 38.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk high | Medical equipment runs on the household's electricity :: People who rely on powered equipment at home can join a free priority register with their energy supplier, and should ask whether any running costs are reimbursed. || accuracy: Home oxygen electricity reimbursement applies to concentrators through the home oxygen supplier and may differ in Scotland; dialysis reimbursement varies by unit; a stairlift is not medical equipment in the same sense and air mattress running costs are unverified, so the opening line groups unlike items. || sharpen: Narrow to home oxygen and the Priority Services Register, the two points most likely to verify, and drop the stairlift and mattress examples.
- [   ] C17-13 total 38.5 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | Relationships are in the law; the visit has no time for them :: England's social care law names relationships and social wellbeing as part of what care should promote, while home care visits are often too short to include company. || accuracy: The claim that visits are often too short for company needs a published source on time-and-task commissioning; the NICE NG21 wording on visits under 30 minutes must be quoted exactly and applies to England only. || sharpen: Find a published source on time-and-task commissioning, quote the Care Act wording beside the NICE 30-minute standard, and keep care workers explicitly outside the criticism; check fit against C16.
- [   ] C17-18 total 38.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk high | Attendance Allowance: four facts families get wrong :: Attendance Allowance is for people over State Pension age who need help or supervision because of illness or disability; income and savings do not count, you do not need a carer, and you choose how to spend it. || accuracy: Rates, the 6-month qualifying rule and the Pension Age Disability Payment rollout dates in Scotland must be verified from official sources that could not be opened; receiving Attendance Allowance raises Pension Credit entitlement only for some people. || sharpen: Verify the current rates and the Scottish rollout from an opened source, and end with how to start a claim so the post closes on an action.
- [   ] C17-03 total 36.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | A cold spell's health effects last for weeks :: Deaths linked with cold weather stay raised for up to about three weeks after a cold day, more so in older people, so keeping warm is needed through the whole spell and the weeks after it. || accuracy: The lag of up to 23 days describes delayed deaths after exposure on the cold day, not continuing risk from indoor temperature in the following weeks, so 'keep warm for the weeks after' does not follow from the study; the Keatinge cooling study used young volunteers and cannot sit on an older-person timeline as if it were about them. || sharpen: Rewrite the position around the delay (deaths linked with one cold day continue for up to three weeks, so watch for chest and heart symptoms afterwards) and drop the young-volunteer laboratory study.
- [   ] C17-06 total 36.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Age is a poor guide to who is lonely :: In large surveys loneliness is reported less often with increasing age, and income, household, marital status, health and how often people socialise predict it at every age. || accuracy: The BBC Loneliness Experiment is a self-selected online sample, so its older respondents are unlikely to represent housebound or offline older people; UK studies suggest loneliness rises again in the oldest age groups, which the post must state rather than leave unresolved. || sharpen: Replace the online survey with a representative UK source, state that loneliness rises again in the oldest groups, and make the ask 'find people by circumstances such as bereavement, poor health and living alone'.
- [   ] C17-14 total 35.5 | public | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk high | Pension Credit: why a small award is worth claiming :: Qualifying for Pension Credit, even for a small weekly amount, also makes a person eligible for other help, and many eligible households do not claim it. || accuracy: Linked entitlements changed in 2024 and 2025 (Winter Fuel Payment rules; Scotland's Pension Age Winter Heating Payment), the take-up figure is unverified, and every claim relies on DWP pages that could not be opened. || sharpen: Verify the linked entitlements for the posting year and the DWP take-up estimate, and lead with the one linked form of help most likely to be worth more than a small award.
- [   ] C17-23 total 35.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk high | Offline in a digital-first health service :: People aged 75 and over are the least likely to use the internet, so every online route to care needs an offline route that works as well. || accuracy: The central statistic (people aged 75 and over least likely to use the internet) and the NHS digital inclusion framework rely on Ofcom, ONS and NHS pages that were not opened; a peer-reviewed source is needed before use. || sharpen: Find a peer-reviewed or opened official source for the internet-use figure and propose one measurable standard, such as time to book by phone compared with online.

## C18 Unpaid carers and the care workforce (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: Top 14: 6 practitioners (14, 11, 01, 09, 08, 12), 5 public (15, 22, 17, 02, 07), 3 both (13, 04, 03); series spread across Teaching round, Ask this, Reading the evidence, Care that works, What gets counted, Who gets left out, Weighing it up and Small detail. Most explain or teach rather than criticise. The paid care workforce is thin (only 03 and 08); C18-05 (count the different care workers) is the best workforce reserve and should replace 12 or 08 if workforce coverage is wanted. About half of the top 14 (14, 15, 22, 02, 07, 09, 08) rest on legislation, GMC or NICE text not yet opened, so verify early. Schedule pairs apart: 01 and 16, 09 and 22, 06 and 17 (same Schulz 2003 source), 02, 15 and 27 (carer's assessment).
Judge allocation reason: 26 of 27 candidates score 36 or more with no disqualifying flag, the earlier collection had almost nothing on carers or the care workforce, and the top 14 are distinct across audiences and series, with strong reserves (C18-05, C18-06, C18-10) if any legislation-based post fails verification.
- [SEL] C18-14 total 50.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Listening to a carer breaches no confidence :: GMC guidance says that listening to the concerns of a patient's relatives or carers is not a breach of confidentiality, so refusing to hear a carer on those grounds is a mistake; the patient's consent is needed before sharing their personal information, but not before listening. || accuracy: Quote the GMC paragraph exactly, including that the doctor should explain they may not be able to keep the conversation from the patient; nurses and allied professionals follow their own codes; sharing personal information still needs consent or another lawful basis. || sharpen: Add the patient-side action: ask and record who the patient is happy for information to go to, so the next clinician does not repeat the refusal.
- [SEL] C18-11 total 50.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Caring and early death: it was the strained carers :: The often-quoted finding that caring shortens life came from spouse carers who reported strain; later population studies found carers as a whole live as long as, or longer than, non-carers, so support should be targeted by asking about strain. || accuracy: The strain estimate's lower bound is 1.00, so it is borderline; lower mortality in population studies probably reflects healthier people taking on care and does not show that caring protects health; a cohort relative risk must not become 'X times more likely' language. || sharpen: End on the practical instruction (ask each carer directly about strain) rather than on the population finding.
- [SEL] C18-13 total 50.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Does respite help? What the trials can and can't tell you :: Trials of respite for carers of people with dementia are few and of very low quality, so they have not shown a clear benefit; that is an evidence gap, not proof that breaks don't help, and qualitative research suggests carers rest best when they believe the person is also having a good time. || accuracy: Check whether the 2014 Cochrane review has been updated; the 2026 review's possible benefits come from studies at high risk of bias or with some concerns, so do not present them as confirmation. || sharpen: Put 'few, poor trials is an evidence gap, not proof that breaks fail' in plain words on the visual and keep the two judging questions as the close.
- [SEL] C18-15 total 50.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Your carer's assessment must ask whether you are willing :: In England, a carer's assessment must consider whether you are willing to go on caring, as well as whether you are able, and Scotland's adult carer support plan asks the same, so you can say what you will and won't do. || accuracy: Care Act section 10(5) wording is 'able, and is likely to continue to be able' and 'willing, and is likely to continue to be willing'; the Scottish section needs confirming; the council must consider willingness, which is not a promise to provide whatever is asked. || sharpen: Add one sentence on what follows (the support plan, and a review of the cared-for person's own care) so carers see that stating a limit leads somewhere.
- [SEL] C18-01 total 47.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | The discharge handover to the family carer :: Discharge produces a letter for the GP and plans for pharmacy and home care, but the family carer who will give most of the care also needs a deliberate handover, and trials that built carers into discharge planning were linked with fewer readmissions. || accuracy: The 25% and 24% are relative reductions pooled across varied multicomponent discharge programmes; the meta-analysis cannot isolate the carer component, absolute readmission rates are needed for an N in 100 statement, and any shorter-term (30-day) result should be checked and stated if it was not clear. || sharpen: Lead with absolute readmission figures from the full text and make 'What are you willing and able to do?' the first item on the handover checklist.
- [SEL] C18-22 total 47.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | If you were taken ill tonight :: A carer can write a one-page emergency plan in advance, and share it, so that someone knows what the person they care for needs if the carer is suddenly ill; in Scotland, an adult carer support plan should cover emergency planning. || accuracy: The Carers (Scotland) Act section on emergency planning and the NICE NG150 wording need checking; name an emergency card scheme only if one is verified. || sharpen: Give the England route as well (ask for a contingency plan in the carer's assessment) so the post works for readers across the UK; schedule apart from C18-09.
- [SEL] C18-04 total 47.0 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | A carer programme that worked without delaying the care home :: Carer support is often judged by whether it delays care home admission, but the UK START programme improved carers' anxiety and depression for six years without changing time to care home, and that benefit to the carer is worth having in itself. || accuracy: The two trials differ in population (UK mixed carers versus US spouse carers), intervention and era, so the side-by-side card must not imply one programme is better; care home admission was a secondary outcome in START; a 2-point HADS difference needs a plain reading. || sharpen: Open with the question of what should count as success, and state the size of START's effect on carers in plain words rather than as a HADS score.
- [SEL] C18-17 total 47.0 | public | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Relief after the death of the person you cared for :: In a US study of 217 family carers of people with dementia, 72 in 100 said the death was a relief to them, and their depressive symptoms fell within three months; relief after long, demanding care is a common response, and the study's authors concluded that support was needed most before the death. || accuracy: Single US sample of carers in a trial, in the final year of life; relief was self-reported; mention the minority with prolonged grief only if a verified figure is found. || sharpen: Add one sentence that relief and grief often come together, and name where carers can get support before the death.
- [SEL] C18-02 total 46.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | When one parent is caring for the other :: When one of your parents looks after the other, the parent doing the caring has needs of their own, and asking them directly, on their own, is the first step to getting them support, including a carer's assessment that does not depend on their income or on how much care they give. || accuracy: The Care Act section 10 duty rests on the appearance of need in England; Scotland's adult carer support plan duty is worded differently and Wales and Northern Ireland differ; the census statistic on older carers' hours is unverified and should be dropped unless checked. || sharpen: Drop the census figure unless verified, and state the English and Scottish entitlements in one plain sentence each on the card.
- [SEL] C18-03 total 45.5 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk low | When a home care client dies :: Home care workers can grieve a client who dies, and in qualitative studies from New York and England they often lost paid work at the same time and received little acknowledgement; being told personally, support from peers and managers, and time to attend the funeral were what helped. || accuracy: Losing paid work on a client's death comes from the New York study; say it about England only if Skills for Care data on zero-hours contracts is verified, and draw the list of supports only from the English study. || sharpen: Add one line for families (tell the care worker yourself, and invite them to the funeral if you wish) so the post gives relatives an action as well as agencies.
- [RES] C18-07 total 45.0 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Before you give up work to care :: Before leaving a job to care for a parent or partner, check the legal rights that might let you keep working, and how to protect your own State Pension record if you do stop. || accuracy: Carer's leave and day-one flexible working requests apply to employees in England, Scotland and Wales, not Northern Ireland or the self-employed; flexible working is a right to request, which an employer can refuse on set grounds; the Carer's Allowance earnings limit changes each April. || sharpen: Open at the decision point ('Before you give up work to care, check four things') and say plainly that a flexible working request can be refused.
- [RES] C18-08 total 44.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | 'As directed' gives a care worker nothing to follow :: Home care workers give medicines according to the written instruction, the medicines chart and their employer's policy, so wording such as 'as directed', or 'when required' without criteria, can lead to doses being missed or given inconsistently. || accuracy: Exact NICE NG67 wording on when-required directions needs checking; the CHUMS study was in care homes, not home care, so it should not supply home care error figures; care workers work from the medicines administration record and care plan as well as the label. || sharpen: Quote the NICE NG67 recommendation directly, and check it against any C06 or C16 candidate on medicines in social care before scheduling.
- [RES] C18-09 total 44.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Ask who depends on the patient :: Some older people admitted to hospital are the main carer for someone at home, and asking 'Does anyone depend on you for care?' at admission finds a person who may now be alone and unsafe. || accuracy: The proportion of older people who provide unpaid care needs a verified census figure; NICE NG150 wording on identifying carers should be checked; avoid implying the dependent person is always unsafe. || sharpen: Add one verified figure for the share of people over 65 who provide unpaid care, and schedule apart from its public counterpart C18-22.
- [   ] C18-12 total 44.5 | practitioners | Who gets left out | teaching | risk medium | The grandchild who helps :: Some young carers look after a grandparent, and adult services can miss them when the social history asks only about partners and adult children; in the UK, young carers have a legal right to an assessment of their own needs. || accuracy: The survey was an online convenience sample of 15 to 17 year olds in six countries, so the positive-outcomes finding does not describe all young carers; the Canadian 40% figure should not stand in for the UK; the census count of young carers needs verifying. || sharpen: Drop the European outcome comparison and use one verified UK figure on young carers, keeping the focus on asking and on the right to a young carer's assessment.
- [   ] C18-05 total 43.5 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | Count the faces at the door :: Home care is usually monitored by visits delivered, late or missed; the number of different care workers a person sees each month is simple to count from the rota and tells you whether the person is being washed and dressed by people who know them. || accuracy: The claim that providers and commissioners do not usually report the number of distinct care workers is unsourced; the NICE NG21 continuity recommendation has to be quoted from the document; the illustrative calendar must stay clearly labelled as not real data. || sharpen: Soften or drop the 'not usually reported' claim and anchor the post on the NICE NG21 recommendation plus one study of what home care users say about seeing the same workers.
- [   ] C18-06 total 43.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk low | Hours of help, or hours on duty? :: Assessments and benefit rules count hours of hands-on care, but many carers of people with dementia describe being on duty around the clock, so a carer assessment should also ask how long the carer can leave the person alone. || accuracy: The Schulz 2003 carers were in the final year of the person's life with advanced dementia, so the figures do not describe dementia carers in general; 46 hours was the median for help with daily activities, not total time. || sharpen: Open with the assessment question ('How long can you leave him on his own?') and say the study was of carers in the last year of the person's life.
- [   ] C18-10 total 43.0 | public | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk medium | Carer's Allowance when you get a State Pension :: Carers who get a State Pension usually can't be paid Carer's Allowance as well, but claiming it can still give an 'underlying entitlement' that adds a carer amount to Pension Credit, Housing Benefit or Council Tax Reduction. || accuracy: Benefit rules and rates change each April and Scotland now uses Carer Support Payment; the underlying entitlement only adds money for people who get or could get means-tested benefits; the sources are gov.uk and charity factsheets not opened in this project, which the evidence rules treat as insufficient on their own. || sharpen: Say in the first two lines who this helps (older carers on low incomes who get or could get Pension Credit), give no figures that will date, and send readers to a benefits check.
- [   ] C18-26 total 42.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk high | Being there: the rule on visiting and accompanying :: In England, since April 2024, a regulation requires hospitals, care homes and hospices to support visiting, and says people attending hospital outpatient and emergency departments should normally be able to have someone with them, so a carer can ask to be present. || accuracy: Regulation 9A applies in England only, to CQC-registered providers, and says people should not be discouraged from being accompanied, with exceptions; the Scottish position (Anne's Law) is uncertain and should be left out unless verified; the regulation text was not opened. || sharpen: Quote the regulation's own wording from the CQC guidance and say it applies in England, since many of the account's readers are in Scotland.
- [   ] C18-24 total 42.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk low | 'They look after their own': what carers report :: When carers from minority ethnic groups use fewer services, a systematic review found most of the barriers they described were the same as for all carers, such as low awareness of services and not wanting outsiders involved, with language barriers and poor cultural fit added. || accuracy: Review of 13 studies with few direct comparisons between groups; do not claim how often services use the phrase; present it as a quoted belief. || sharpen: Give practitioners one concrete check to run in their own service, such as whether carer assessments are offered with a professional interpreter.
- [   ] C18-19 total 41.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk low | Ask what keeps the carer going :: Most carer questionnaires measure strain, but in dementia carers the positive aspects of caring, such as satisfaction, meaning and competence, are linked with lower depression and strain, so a carer assessment should also ask what sustains the carer and protect it. || accuracy: Mostly cross-sectional associations; positive aspects may reflect lower strain rather than protect against it; check the wording on self-rated health and personal strain against the review. || sharpen: Consider folding the 'what keeps you going' question into C18-06 or C18-15 rather than running a third carer-assessment question post.
- [   ] C18-27 total 41.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | When staying at home costs the carer too much :: When a person wants to stay at home and their carer can no longer sustain the care, both interests are real; the law in England and Scotland asks for the person's wishes and the carer's views and willingness to be considered, so the decision should be discussed openly with both. || accuracy: Best-interests law applies only when the person lacks capacity for the decision about where to live; if they have capacity the choice is theirs, but it cannot oblige a carer to provide care; the post should state this distinction. || sharpen: Add the capacity distinction in one sentence and replace 'Both of those facts deserve a hearing' with a concrete opening situation.
- [   ] C18-18 total 41.0 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Training care home staff: what the WHELD trial changed :: In a UK trial in 69 nursing homes, training care staff in person-centred care and social interaction improved residents' quality of life and reduced agitation at lower cost than usual care; the effects were small, and antipsychotic use, already low, did not change. || accuracy: Effect sizes were small (d 0.23 to 0.30); the DEMQOL difference needs a plain reading; check the 19.7% positive-interactions figure against the paper; antipsychotic use did not fall. || sharpen: Frame around what care staff were given (training, protected time, champions) so it belongs to the workforce category, and check overlap with C08 and C16 care home candidates.
- [   ] C18-21 total 41.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | Tell the GP practice you are a carer :: Telling your GP practice that you are an unpaid carer puts it on your record, can make you eligible for a free NHS flu vaccine, and lets the practice point you to carer support. || accuracy: Flu vaccine eligibility wording differs between England and Scotland and is set each season; in England carer eligibility depends on Carer's Allowance or main-carer status; the national programme letters were not opened. || sharpen: Schedule for early autumn only, and add one line on what else being recorded as a carer can lead to, such as a carer's assessment.
- [   ] C18-20 total 37.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | The carer's stress predicts the care home move :: In a systematic review of 80 studies, consistent predictors of care home admission for people with dementia included the carer's emotional stress, wish to place the person and feeling trapped, alongside the person's cognition, dependency and behaviour, while incontinence did not consistently predict admission. || accuracy: The review searched to 2006; 'wish to place' predicting placement is close to circular; the incontinence finding needs checking against a newer meta-analysis (such as Toot 2017) before it is highlighted; predictors are observational. || sharpen: Check against a newer review and lead with the action (assess the carer's stress directly as part of the plan) rather than the incontinence point.
- [   ] C18-16 total 37.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | You have a right to be involved in the discharge :: In Scotland, and in England, the law requires hospitals to involve unpaid carers in planning the discharge of the person they care for, so you can ask to be part of the planning early and to have your views taken into account. || accuracy: The English duty's section number is uncertain (the Health and Care Act 2022 inserted a discharge duty into the NHS Act 2006); state it only after checking the text; the Scottish duty sits with health boards under the Carers (Scotland) Act 2016. || sharpen: Same core idea as C18-01 for a public audience; run it only if C18-01 is not chosen, or fold its four questions into C18-01's family-facing variant.
- [   ] C18-25 total 37.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Ask who gives the tablets :: For many older people living at home, a family carer manages the medicines, so a medication review that does not include the carer misses the person who knows what is actually taken, refused, missed or hard to give. || accuracy: The key 2026 review abstract was only partly read; the claim that carers report problems patients do not needs a source; the patient's agreement to involve the carer should be mentioned. || sharpen: Read the review results and merge with C18-08 or a C06 medicines post if one already covers the carer's role in medication review.
- [   ] C18-23 total 33.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Keeping care staff is part of care quality :: In US nursing homes, quality was better when local unemployment was higher, alongside higher staffing and lower staff turnover, and an English hospital study linked staff work experience with older patients' experience of care; this suggests keeping care staff is part of care quality. || accuracy: Ecological US panel data; Maben 2012 concerned hospital nursing staff, not care workers; the turnover figure is unverified; the chain invites a causal reading that reducing turnover alone improves quality. || sharpen: Replace the unemployment lead with UK evidence on care worker retention and care home quality (for example Allan and Vadean 2021, if verified), or merge the point into C18-05.

## C19 Palliative care and care near the end of life (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: Top 14: public 5 (C19-24, 05, 19, 29, 03), both 6 (C19-21, 04, 10, 06, 08, 16), practitioners 3 (C19-18, 22, 17). Practitioner coverage is light; C19-01 and C19-12 are the best practitioner reserves. Two clusters need managing: prognosis and identification (C19-16, 17, 18, with C19-14 and 35 behind) and dying at home (C19-03, 04, 08, 10, with C19-30); take at most two or three from each so the set also covers symptom care. C19-04 and C19-28 must be worded consistently on whether hospital death counts as failure.
Judge allocation reason: All 36 candidates reach 36 on the stated formula (its true maximum is 55, not 50) and 22 score 44 or more with no disqualifying flag, so the category can fill 14 posts with well-evidenced, distinct ideas for both practitioners and the public.
- [SEL] C19-18 total 50.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Survival estimates run optimistic, most of all for patients you know well :: In a Chicago study of hospice referrals, doctors' survival predictions were accurate only 1 time in 5 and were usually too optimistic, and the longer the doctor had known the patient, the less accurate the prediction. || accuracy: Single US hospice-referral cohort from the late 1990s; 'accurate' means within 33% of actual survival; the relationship-length finding is an association within one study, and the suggested corrections (ranges, a colleague's view) are judgement, not tested remedies. || sharpen: Present the counter-intuitive finding as one study's association and mark the three corrections as recommendations, citing a newer review for the general point that estimates run optimistic.
- [SEL] C19-21 total 49.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Breathlessness: a fan to the face, and oxygen for low oxygen levels :: For breathlessness in advanced illness, a hand-held fan blowing air across the face reduced breathlessness in a crossover trial, while oxygen gave no more relief than room air in people whose oxygen levels were not low. || accuracy: Abernethy excluded people with PaO2 55 mmHg or lower, mostly had COPD, and both groups improved; Galbraith measured a short-term effect in 50 patients; Campbell's near-death study had 32 patients. || sharpen: Say that both oxygen and air groups felt better in the Lancet trial, so moving air may be doing part of the work, which strengthens the fan message.
- [SEL] C19-24 total 49.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk low | Ask what the later stages of dementia usually bring :: Eating problems, fevers and chest infections are common in advanced dementia, and in a US study, residents whose families understood this and the likely prognosis were much less likely to have burdensome interventions, such as hospital transfer or tube feeding, in their last three months. || accuracy: US nursing home cohort (2003 to 2009), advanced dementia defined at a late stage; the proxy-understanding finding is an adjusted odds ratio of 0.12 from an observational analysis and must be described in words, not converted. || sharpen: Open with the finding (most people with advanced dementia developed eating problems within 18 months) rather than 'Ask the doctor', then give the questions.
- [SEL] C19-04 total 48.5 | both | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Do most people want to die at home? :: The common statement that most people want to die at home rests on studies that often leave out people who were never asked or had no preference, so each person's own preference, which may be a hospice, hospital or care home, needs asking and may change. || accuracy: Gomes 2013 found home was the most preferred place in most studies that recorded preferences, so the post must not imply most people do not want to die at home; Hoare's finding is that the proportion is unknown once missing preferences are counted. Steinhauser's 'dying at home ranked lowest' is a US 2000 ranking exercise. || sharpen: State plainly that where preferences were recorded home usually came first, so the argument is for asking, recording and reviewing, not for doubting home.
- [SEL] C19-05 total 48.5 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk medium | The noisy breathing near death :: Noisy, rattling breathing in the last hours or days happens when a dying person can no longer clear saliva and secretions from the throat; the person is usually deeply unconscious, and a change of position, a clear explanation and sometimes a medicine can help those listening. || accuracy: That the dying person is not distressed by the sound is inference, not measured. Evidence for repositioning is consensus only; Cochrane found no clear evidence that drugs help established noisy breathing, and the SILENCE result (27% to 13%) was for prophylaxis in hospice patients, not treatment of an established rattle. || sharpen: Separate what was measured (relatives' distress in Wee; prophylaxis in SILENCE) from what is inferred (the person's own comfort), and say medicines have weak evidence once the sound has started.
- [SEL] C19-10 total 48.5 | both | Who gets left out | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Count the carer's hours :: In a national survey of bereaved carers of people who died of cancer in England, carers reported a median of about 69 and a half hours a week of caregiving in the last 3 months of life, time that evaluations of end-of-life care seldom count. || accuracy: Check whether the 69.5-hour median includes time 'on call' or only active care, and whether it is the median among those who reported caregiving; response rate 28.5%, self-report, cancer deaths only, England 2015. || sharpen: Label the 168-square grid with exactly what the hours include, and end on a specific planning question a home-death plan should answer about the carer.
- [SEL] C19-22 total 48.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Parkinson's medicines when swallowing stops :: When a person with Parkinson's can no longer swallow in the last days of life, stopping all their dopaminergic medicine can cause rigidity and pain and, rarely, a dangerous withdrawal syndrome, so a non-oral route such as a rotigotine patch should be considered and dopamine-blocking drugs avoided. || accuracy: Parkinsonism-hyperpyrexia syndrome is rare and should be stated as such; rotigotine conversions are approximate and patches can cause hallucinations or confusion; some common palliative anti-sickness and sedative drugs (for example levomepromazine, haloperidol, metoclopramide) block dopamine, so any named alternatives must be checked against the guideline. || sharpen: Name the drug classes to avoid only once verified, and add that conversion should be agreed with pharmacy or the Parkinson's team because doses are approximate.
- [SEL] C19-06 total 48.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Is hearing the last sense to go? :: A small EEG study found that the brains of some unresponsive dying hospice patients still responded to sounds in a way similar to healthy young people, which supports talking to a dying person who is unable to respond, though it does not show that they understand the words. || accuracy: Very small sample of unresponsive patients (verify N from PMC full text); the study measured brain responses to tone changes, not hearing of speech, comprehension or awareness, so 'hearing is preserved' must not become 'they understand you'. || sharpen: Put the verified number of unresponsive patients on the evidence slide so the size of the study is visible at a glance.
- [SEL] C19-08 total 47.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | Family members giving the injections: the trade-off :: In a UK feasibility trial, trained family carers gave as-needed injections for symptoms at home far faster than a nurse could arrive, but the approach shifts responsibility onto families and should be a free, supported choice, never a way to cover gaps in nursing. || accuracy: CARiAD was a feasibility trial of 40 dyads with unbalanced retention; the 5 versus 105 minute medians are feasibility data, not a definitive effect estimate, and 'far faster' should be worded as what was observed in a small trial. || sharpen: Open with the question a family would face ('Would you want to be trained to give this?') and label the times as feasibility data from 40 families.
- [SEL] C19-17 total 47.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | The surprise question is a prompt, not a prediction :: The question 'Would I be surprised if this patient died in the next 12 months?' picks up only about two thirds of people who go on to die, and most people it flags are still alive a year later, so it works as a prompt to start planning, not as a forecast. || accuracy: Positive predictive value depends on the proportion of the group who die, so '37%' varies by setting; the outcome window was 6 to 18 months, not strictly one year; most studies had moderate to high risk of bias. || sharpen: Say that the flag's meaning changes with the setting because it depends on how many in the group die, and keep the window as 6 to 18 months.
- [RES] C19-19 total 47.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | Morphine and the fear that it means the end :: Being offered morphine or another strong opioid is a decision about pain or breathlessness; NICE tells doctors to ask about common fears, including the fear that it means the final stage of life, and to prescribe a laxative from the start. || accuracy: Confirm NICE CG140 wording for asking about fears and for laxatives in all patients starting strong opioids; keep out the claim that opioids do not shorten life unless a review supports its exact wording. || sharpen: Add who to contact if drowsiness or constipation develops in the first days, so the card ends on an action.
- [RES] C19-29 total 47.0 | public | Care that works | public_explanation | risk low | When grief does not ease :: Most grief eases with time without treatment, but about 1 in 10 bereaved adults develop prolonged grief disorder, which is more common at older ages and responds to a specific talking treatment tested in older adults. || accuracy: The 9.8% pooled prevalence comes from heterogeneous studies using criteria that predate ICD-11, after non-violent loss; the age association is from meta-regression across studies; Shear's trial compared two therapies in the US and grief-specific therapy may not be available in the UK. || sharpen: Say where to ask for help and, once verified, that grief-specific therapy is not available everywhere, so the post does not promise what readers cannot get.
- [RES] C19-16 total 46.5 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | The last year of life is on every ward :: On a single census day in 25 Scottish hospitals, 28.8% of inpatients died within 12 months, and 45.6% of those aged 85 and over, so recognising possible last year of life and planning future care belong on ordinary wards. || accuracy: The angle's claim that most such inpatients 'will not have been identified' is not a finding of Clark 2014 and needs its own source or removal; data are from one day in 2010. || sharpen: Remove or source the 'not identified' claim and pair the 85-and-over icon array with one ward-level action.
- [   ] C19-03 total 46.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Who to phone when an expected death happens at home :: When someone dies at home and the death was expected, the family does not need to call an ambulance or hurry; they can phone the GP practice, district nursing team or out-of-hours service to have the death verified, and take time with the person first. || accuracy: Phone routes and verification rules (who may verify, expected timeframe) differ between Scotland, England and Wales and between local services; the key sources are NHS and Hospice UK web pages that could not be opened from this environment. The CPR-by-ambulance-crew claim needs a JRCALC or Resuscitation Council source before use. || sharpen: Make the phone card nation-specific (NHS 24 on 111 in Scotland; NHS 111 in England and Wales) and source the verification and 'no need to hurry' claims from an opened Hospice UK document.
- [   ] C19-02 total 45.5 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | How alert do you want to be? :: Many seriously ill people rate staying mentally aware as important, so the balance between symptom relief and drowsiness near the end of life should be set by asking the person, and the answer recorded for when they can no longer say. || accuracy: Steinhauser 2000 measured importance ratings of attributes, not preferences about sedation trade-offs; drowsiness in the last days is often from the dying process rather than the medicine, so the post must not imply alertness can always be preserved by dose choice. || sharpen: Add one line that drowsiness near death is often the illness itself, so the question sets priorities for dosing rather than promising alertness.
- [   ] C19-28 total 45.5 | both | Who gets left out | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Poorer neighbourhoods, more hospital deaths :: In high-income countries, people from more deprived areas are more likely to die in hospital rather than at home, more likely to receive hospital care in the last 3 months of life, and less likely to receive specialist palliative care. || accuracy: Pooled odds ratios from heterogeneous observational studies with modest effects (1.30, 1.16, 1.13); causes not established; describe in words only. || sharpen: Frame the hospital-death result as a gap in choice and support, and name one thing a service could report by deprivation.
- [   ] C19-01 total 45.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | When the regular opioid dose goes up, recalculate the as-needed dose :: When the regular 24-hour opioid dose is increased, for example in a syringe pump, the as-needed (rescue) dose on the chart usually needs recalculating at the same time, or the night team inherits a dose too small to help. || accuracy: The one-sixth fraction is a convention, not trial-derived; sources give one tenth to one sixth, and frail older people or those with poor kidney function may need a lower fraction or a different opioid. Scottish Palliative Care Guidelines and the Palliative Care Formulary are web or paywalled sources that may not be openable here. || sharpen: Lead with a labelled worked example (30 mg over 24 hours gives 5 mg as needed at one sixth) and state that the fraction is a range to adjust for frailty and kidney function.
- [   ] C19-12 total 44.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | 'Relatives aware': write what they understood :: A note saying 'family aware' records that a conversation happened, so staff should use the words 'dying' and 'death' when they mean them, ask the family what they have understood, and write that down. || accuracy: Quote the Neuberger review's own wording on communication rather than paraphrasing 'often not told'; Anderson 2019 is marked unsure and must be confirmed; NACEL notes-versus-survey comparison unverified. || sharpen: Keep this distinct from C19-32 by leading with the two illustrative note entries and the teachable question, citing the LCP review only as support.
- [   ] C19-26 total 44.5 | public | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | CPR on television and in real hospitals :: On popular TV medical dramas most people given CPR survive, while in UK hospitals fewer than 1 in 5 adults given CPR survive to leave hospital, so expectations formed by television can mislead decisions. || accuracy: TV studies may report immediate survival rather than survival to discharge, so check outcome definitions match before comparing with the audit's 18.4%; audit data are 2011 to 2013; the frailty study was small and single-centre. || sharpen: Match outcome definitions before setting the numbers side by side, and add the lower audit figure for non-shockable rhythms, which make up most in-hospital arrests.
- [   ] C19-11 total 44.0 | public | Care that works | public_explanation | risk low | The 'just in case' box :: Anticipatory ('just in case') medicines are prescribed and left in the home so that pain, breathlessness, sickness, noisy breathing or distress near the end of life can be treated quickly at night or at weekends; having them does not mean death is imminent or that they must be used. || accuracy: Bowers 2019 found the evidence that anticipatory medicines improve symptom control or prevent admissions is inadequate, so the post should present them as a way to avoid delay, not as proven to relieve symptoms faster; contents and who may give them vary by nation and service. || sharpen: Add, if a source supports it, how often anticipatory medicines are actually used, and what families should do with unused medicines after the death.
- [   ] C19-20 total 44.0 | public | Small detail, real effect | public_explanation | risk low | Mouth care in the last days :: In the last days of life a dry mouth is common and uncomfortable; frequent mouth and lip care relieves it, families who wish to can help, and a drip does not reliably relieve thirst or other symptoms. || accuracy: Bruera 2013's primary outcome was fatigue, myoclonus, sedation and hallucinations, not thirst, and the population was advanced cancer in home hospice, so 'a drip does not reliably relieve thirst' is not directly supported by that trial. || sharpen: Cite a source that addresses thirst directly and word the drip point as 'did not improve symptoms in a trial in advanced cancer'.
- [   ] C19-25 total 44.0 | both | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk medium | Antibiotics for chest infection in advanced dementia: survival and comfort :: In a US study of nursing home residents with advanced dementia, antibiotic treatment of suspected pneumonia was linked with longer survival but not with better comfort, and oral antibiotics were linked with survival similar to injections, drips or hospital admission. || accuracy: Observational with likely confounding (sicker residents more likely treated, only 8.9% untreated); 'survival similar with oral treatment' rests on overlapping adjusted hazard ratios and must not be stated as equivalence; treated residents had worse, not merely no better, comfort scores. || sharpen: State the confounding in one plain sentence and consider pairing with C19-24 as a two-part set from the same cohort.
- [   ] C19-13 total 43.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | teaching | risk medium | Turning in the last days: by comfort, not by the clock :: In the last days of life, how often a person is repositioned should be agreed with them or their family and guided by comfort, with pain relief before turns that hurt, rather than set by a fixed schedule kept for the chart. || accuracy: EPUAP/NPIAP 2019 is largely paywalled; confirm the exact palliative-care recommendation wording and the source for analgesia before repositioning. SCALE is expert consensus, not measured evidence. || sharpen: Add one line that the plan changes how often and how, not whether, the person is cared for, so it cannot read as neglect.
- [   ] C19-31 total 43.0 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Ask the carer what support they need :: The Carer Support Needs Assessment Tool asks family carers of people near the end of life which kinds of support they need; in an Australian stepped-wedge trial, its use by nurses reduced carer strain, and carers' top needs included knowing what to expect. || accuracy: Stepped-wedge trial with about 45% attrition; confirm which outcomes improved beyond caregiver strain; CSNAT use involves a licensed approach and training. || sharpen: Name the CSNAT support areas on the card from the published tool and say which outcome the trial improved.
- [   ] C19-35 total 42.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | Slow decline, and no clear moment :: People with frailty or dementia usually decline slowly over years from an already low level of function, so there may be no clear moment when anyone says they are dying, and conversations about the future are best started early. || accuracy: The trajectories are simplified averages; individual courses vary widely and many people do not follow a single pattern. || sharpen: Label the curves as simplified with wide individual variation, and give families a first sentence to open the conversation.
- [   ] C19-23 total 41.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Diabetes in the last days: fewer tests, and insulin still in type 1 :: In the last days of life most glucose-lowering tablets can be stopped and finger-prick testing reduced, but people with type 1 diabetes usually need some long-acting insulin continued to prevent ketoacidosis, which causes thirst, nausea and distress. || accuracy: End-of-life diabetes guidance allows withdrawal of insulin in type 1 diabetes in the final days to be discussed with the person and family, so 'usually need insulin continued' must be framed as the default with discussion; edition and exact monitoring advice need checking. || sharpen: Present continued once-daily insulin in type 1 as the default to discuss with the person and family, and verify the table cells against the current Diabetes UK and TREND guidance.
