- [   ] C19-30 total 41.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Home palliative care teams and dying at home :: In a Cochrane review, home palliative care services roughly doubled the odds of dying at home, about one extra home death for every five people served, and slightly reduced symptom burden, without affecting carers' grief. || accuracy: 'Roughly doubled the odds' will be read as doubled chance, against the house rule on odds ratios; the number needed to treat depends on baseline rates; the review is from 2013, mostly cancer, and found no evidence of effect on carers' grief rather than showing no effect. || sharpen: Say 'more people died at home, about one extra for every five supported' in plain words, drop the odds language, and check for a later update of the review.
- [   ] C19-33 total 41.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Advance care planning in people over 80: what one trial found :: In a Melbourne trial of inpatients aged 80 and over, facilitated advance care planning made it much more likely that end-of-life wishes were known and respected, and relatives of those who died had less stress, anxiety and depression, though later trials in other groups have been less consistent. || accuracy: Detering was single-centre, unblinded and small among those who died (29 and 27); the Morrison viewpoint and ACTION trial citations are marked unsure and need checking. || sharpen: Keep the family outcome here and remove it from C19-09, and give the ACTION result in one plain sentence.
- [   ] C19-36 total 41.5 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk low | Palliative care can start alongside treatment :: Palliative care can start early in a serious illness, alongside treatment aimed at the illness, and in a well-known trial of early palliative care in advanced lung cancer, people had better quality of life and mood. || accuracy: Temel's participants were in their 60s on average, so 'younger people' is loose; the survival difference was a secondary finding and must not lead; quality of life and mood improved in lung cancer only. || sharpen: Correct the age description and say what a palliative referral offers an older person with non-cancer illness (symptoms, support, planning).
- [   ] C19-27 total 41.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | CPR decisions are made one person at a time :: In the UK a decision not to attempt CPR must be made for the individual, based on their health and wishes, and the person, or those close to them if they lack capacity, should normally be involved; age, disability or living in a care home are not reasons in themselves. || accuracy: Tracey and Winspear are English law, so state which UK nations they bind and cite the Scottish policy separately; Tracey's exception concerns physical or psychological harm, not mere distress; quote the CQC review's own wording rather than implying blanket decisions were proven widespread. || sharpen: Add a sentence people can use to ask whether a CPR decision exists, what it rests on and whether they were meant to be involved.
- [   ] C19-07 total 40.5 | both | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Palliative, and still getting to the toilet :: People with advanced illness often want to keep the ability to stand, move and reach the toilet, and physiotherapy and occupational therapy aimed at those goals remain part of good palliative care. || accuracy: Two claims lack sources: that toileting and mobility are priorities for many people with advanced illness, and that therapy is reduced once people are labelled palliative. Salakari 2015 covers advanced cancer only. || sharpen: Anchor on a sourced statement from the Hospice UK rehabilitative palliative care report and drop the 'therapy stops at the label' claim unless a study supports it.
- [   ] C19-14 total 40.5 | practitioners | Who gets left out | teaching | risk medium | The discharge letter as a prompt :: An unplanned hospital admission in an older person with advancing illness is a recognised indicator of deteriorating health, and the GP team reading the discharge letter can use it to ask whether a conversation and plan about future care are needed. || accuracy: Harrison 2012 is a six-practice study of deaths around 2009, so the 29% and 'about 1 in 5 non-cancer' figures are old and small; SPICT indicator wording must come from the current version. || sharpen: Supplement the 2009 figures with more recent identification data in non-cancer illness and give the GP team one concrete step (for example, a read-coded review within two weeks of the letter).
- [   ] C19-15 total 40.0 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | Count the moves in the last days :: Moves between care home, emergency department and wards near death are common and can signal missed planning; in a US national study, nearly 1 in 5 nursing home residents with advanced cognitive and physical impairment had at least one burdensome transition near the end of life. || accuracy: The 'burdensome transition' definition spans the last 90 days (multiple hospitalisations, loss of home continuity), not only moves in the last days as the title implies; US Medicare data 2000 to 2007; the claim that UK indicators do not count last-days transfers is unverified. || sharpen: Reframe the title and card to cover the study's full definition and verify the UK indicator claim before making it the position.
- [   ] C19-34 total 37.5 | both | Who gets left out | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Care homes are becoming the most common place to die :: If trends from 2004 to 2014 continue, deaths in care homes in England and Wales will more than double by 2040, making the care home the most common place of death, so end-of-life care in care homes is central to how many older people will die. || accuracy: The 2040 projection predates the pandemic; since 2020 deaths at home in England and Wales have risen sharply while the care home share has not risen as projected, so the working title 'becoming the most common place to die' may already be contradicted by current ONS data, which could not be opened here. || sharpen: Check current place-of-death data and, if the trend has changed, reframe around the care home's large existing share and the support staff need.
- [   ] C19-09 total 36.5 | practitioners | What gets counted | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Ask the family how the last days went :: How someone dies is linked with how their relatives fare afterwards, so care of dying people should also be judged by asking bereaved relatives, not only by records that stop at the death. || accuracy: Wright 2008 is observational (adjusted OR 3.37, US cancer cohort) and the opening invites a causal reading that aggressive care causes carer depression; the England VOICES survey was last run in 2015 and should not be described as ongoing; the Detering family finding rests on small numbers and overlaps C19-33. || sharpen: Drop the Wright association as the opener and lead with the measurement gap: routine indicators stop at the death, and NACEL's bereaved-relative survey shows one way to ask.
- [   ] C19-32 total 36.5 | practitioners | Care that works | teaching | risk medium | What replaced the Liverpool Care Pathway, and why :: The 2013 review of the Liverpool Care Pathway found that a document meant to support good care of the dying was often applied as a checklist, and the priorities that replaced it put recognition, honest communication, involvement, support for families and an individual plan at the centre. || accuracy: 'Often used as a checklist in place of care' may overstate the Neuberger review, which also reported good care in many cases; use the review's own wording. England-specific; Scotland issued different guidance. || sharpen: Recast as a bedside test built on the five priorities, with the LCP history as one line, so it does not overlap C19-12.

## C20 Ageism, research exclusion, uncertainty and measurement (judge recommends 14; mechanical allocation 10)
Judge balance note: The top 14 by score (22, 19, 17, 02, 06, 30, 03, 14, 11, 28, 26, 10, 24, 08) lean to practitioners: 9 practitioner, 3 public (17, 30, 10), 2 both (22, 14). To reach about 5 public posts, swap 08 for 20 (stage 3 kidney disease, public, 44) and consider 27 (public counterpart of 26, 42.5) in place of 26 or 11. The set is well balanced between criticism and teaching or good care: 19, 24, 30, 06, 26, 28 and 11 explain or show good practice.
Judge allocation reason: 28 of 30 candidates reach 36 with no disqualifying flag (22 reach 40, roughly 36 rescaled because the stated formula actually has a maximum of 55, not 50), and after collapsing the overlapping clusters (trial exclusion 01/05/07/09; research participation 13/21; self-rated health 10/12/18/23; uncertainty 27/29) more than 14 distinct strong ideas remain.
- [SEL] C20-22 total 50.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Checking the 7.5 years :: The often-quoted finding that people with positive views of their own ageing lived 7.5 years longer comes from one observational cohort of 660 people; later pooled studies support a small link, but the figure describes an association, and reverse causation and confounding remain possible. || accuracy: The 7.5 years is an adjusted median survival difference in 660 Ohio residents with perceptions measured up to 23 years earlier; use 'linked with', never 'because of', and do not call the figure false; keep Westerhof's 'small' effect wording exact. || sharpen: Open with the figure as it is usually shared and deliver the three checks in the next lines, closing on the equal-dignity judgement.
- [SEL] C20-19 total 49.0 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | A clot test cut-off adjusted for age :: Because D-dimer rises with age, a fixed cut-off rules out pulmonary embolism in few older people; using age multiplied by 10 as the cut-off in people over 50 with a non-high clinical probability let far more people aged 75 and over avoid a scan, with no extra missed clots in that group. || accuracy: 'No additional false negatives' applies to the 75-and-over non-high-probability subgroup in ADJUST-PE; the rule applies only after a non-high or unlikely clinical probability score; units are assay-dependent (FEU); NICE NG158 'consider' wording must be confirmed. || sharpen: Frame it explicitly as a test threshold recalibrated for older people, the positive case of measurement fitted to the population, with the condition (over 50, after a probability score) in the first two lines.
- [SEL] C20-17 total 48.0 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | What would you advise if I were 20 years younger? :: Asking a clinician what they would advise if you were 20 years younger, and why, shows whether a recommendation rests on reasons about your health or only on your age. || accuracy: If the Equality Act is mentioned, it applies in Great Britain not Northern Ireland, to adults 18 and over, with exceptions and objective justification; do not borrow Chang 2020's causal wording about ageism and health. || sharpen: Include one legitimate age-linked reason (time to benefit) so readers expect some answers to differ, and drop the legal claim unless it is opened and worded exactly.
- [SEL] C20-02 total 47.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Several good guidelines, one person :: Following each single-condition guideline for a person with several conditions adds up to a plan that no trial tested and no guideline weighed as a whole, so the combined plan needs its own review. || accuracy: Boyd 2005 applied US guidelines to a hypothetical patient; confirm 12 medicines (and dose count) from full text and do not present it as NICE; Dumbreck's 133 are potential interactions between guideline recommendations, not observed harms; 'No trial tested this combination' is an inference and should be worded as one. || sharpen: Put the UK finding (Dumbreck or Hughes) beside Boyd so UK readers cannot dismiss it as American, and close on one concrete step: review the whole list against the person's top two priorities. Check against C06 polypharmacy picks.
- [SEL] C20-06 total 47.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Can do, did do, does do :: What a person says they can do, what they manage in a test and what they actually do on an ordinary day are three different measurements that often disagree, so any function score should say which one it records. || accuracy: Wording must match Glass 1998 ('discordance' among hypothetical, experimental and enacted function) rather than 'consistent disagreement'; the ICF capacity and performance qualifiers are defined for a standard versus current environment; the self-report versus performance agreement claim still needs a source. || sharpen: Anchor it in the discharge decision: the home visit or the carer's account of 7am is the 'does do' measurement, and the discharge letter should say which one the score records.
- [SEL] C20-30 total 47.5 | public | Care that works | good_care | risk medium | Talking therapy works in later life :: In NHS psychological therapy services in England, people aged 65 and over were more likely than working-age adults to recover and less likely to drop out, yet older people are under-represented among those treated, so an older person with depression or anxiety should be offered, or ask about, talking therapy. || accuracy: The comparison is adjusted and observational, and older adults who reach the service may be a selected group; crude recovery percentages must come from the full text; under-representation needs a primary source; the service described is England's, and Scottish access differs. || sharpen: Give crude recovery percentages by age from the full text, one line on who gets referred, and a route for readers in Scotland.
- [SEL] C20-03 total 47.0 | practitioners | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Exclusion should need a reason :: Many studies exclude people who can't consent for themselves by default, although UK law allows them to take part with safeguards, so people with conditions such as dementia and stroke end up with the weakest evidence for their care. || accuracy: Taylor 2012's 6% may be a share of all 434 articles rather than of the studies that excluded people with cognitive impairment, so the draft opening may use the wrong denominator; Adults with Incapacity (Scotland) Act s51 and the Clinical Trials Regulations legal-representative rules must be worded from the legislation itself. || sharpen: Confirm the denominator for the 6% and replace the three legal boxes with one precise sentence per jurisdiction, keeping the inclusion argument labelled as ethical judgement.
- [SEL] C20-14 total 47.0 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | Illness in later life arrives earlier in poorer areas :: In Scotland, people in the most deprived areas develop several long-term conditions 10 to 15 years earlier than people in the most affluent areas, so services and studies that use a fixed age as their entry point reach poorer people later in their illness. || accuracy: Barnett's 10 to 15 years is the population difference in onset of multimorbidity (two or more conditions) in 2007 data, not a statement about individuals; healthy life expectancy gaps need an opened source; the chart values must come from the Lancet full text. || sharpen: Name one concrete age-gated entry point (a service or a trial age criterion) as the example, and keep the need-based alternative explicitly labelled as judgement.
- [SEL] C20-11 total 46.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | When five-year risk figures run high :: In older people, standard survival analysis can overstate the chance of an event such as a fracture because it treats people who died of something else as if they were still at risk; competing-risk methods give lower, more realistic figures. || accuracy: The 37% and 75% are relative overestimates, so the absolute 10-year risks must come from the full text before use; do not imply that FRAX or other common calculators share the flaw (FRAX accounts for mortality); 'benefit overstated' should be phrased as absolute benefit estimates built on the inflated baseline risk. || sharpen: Lead with the absolute pair of 10-year risks and give one question to ask of any risk figure in a very old person: were deaths from other causes allowed for?
- [SEL] C20-26 total 46.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk low | Reading the age subgroup in a forest plot :: When a trial reports that its effect was consistent across age subgroups, the oldest subgroup may hold few people and have a wide confidence interval, and a non-significant test for interaction is weak evidence that the effect is the same in them. || accuracy: Brookes 2004's 'about four times the sample size' applies to detecting an interaction of the same size as the overall effect and needs checking; the caution should run both ways, since apparent subgroup differences are also often spurious. || sharpen: Use a real trial's oldest subgroup (numbers of people and events, interval width) instead of only an illustrative plot, and keep the two-way caution.
- [RES] C20-28 total 46.0 | practitioners | What gets counted | teaching | risk low | An outcome set built for older people :: An international consensus set of outcome measures for older people's health care lists what services should track, including taking part in decisions, autonomy, mood, loneliness, pain, daily activities, falls, time in hospital, strain on carers and place of death, and a service can compare its own measures against it. || accuracy: The exact list and count of outcomes must come from Akpan 2018 (the draft opening leaves N blank); describe 'carer burden' as strain on carers; 'most services report activity' is a general claim that needs careful wording. || sharpen: Open with the two outcomes readers least expect on a dashboard (loneliness, place of death) and make the visual a fill-in audit card.
- [RES] C20-10 total 45.5 | public | What gets counted | public_explanation | risk low | Several conditions, and health rated good :: In a large study of 85-year-olds in Newcastle, most had several diseases on record, yet about three in four rated their health as good, very good or excellent compared with others their age, so a list of diagnoses says little about how a person experiences their life. || accuracy: The self-rating was comparative ('compared with others your age'), which tends to run optimistic, so the wording must be kept on caption and visual; 'says little about how a person experiences their life' overclaims beyond what the study measured. || sharpen: Replace 'says little' with a literal contrast (5 diagnoses, 3 in 4 rate health good or better) and close on the question a family can ask; schedule apart from C20-18 and C20-23.
- [RES] C20-24 total 45.5 | practitioners | Care that works | good_care | risk low | Measure the goal the person set :: Goal attainment scaling measures progress on goals the older person chooses, each scored from much worse to much better than expected, and in a trial of geriatric assessment it detected meaningful change that standard function and quality-of-life scales missed. || accuracy: Responsiveness is not validity: goal attainment scores in the trial were set and rated by clinicians, who may not have been blinded; the idea that good care 'looked ineffective because the wrong thing was measured' is speculation. || sharpen: Add one line on the limitation (the people who set the goals also rate them) and make the visual a worked goal ladder with the review question.
- [   ] C20-08 total 44.5 | practitioners | Small detail, real effect | good_care | risk medium | An information sheet only some people can read :: People with aphasia after a stroke are often left out of stroke research because consent materials assume fluent reading and speech, yet many can decide for themselves when information is given in an accessible form. || accuracy: The 10-of-14 figure comes from trials of information provision after stroke only and should not be generalised to all stroke research without Townend 2007 or similar; 'about a third' needs a primary epidemiological source; 'many can decide for themselves with accessible information' needs its own support (Jayes and Palmer unverified). || sharpen: Keep the scope of the 10-of-14 figure explicit, show one verified accessible-format consent sentence beside the standard one, and name one tested consent support tool.
- [   ] C20-16 total 44.0 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | When the people who died drop out of the average :: In studies of older people, deaths and dropouts remove the sickest people from later measurements, so average function among those still measured can look better than the group's real course; check how deaths and missing data were handled. || accuracy: The claim that survivor-only averages explain studies that 'don't match clinical experience' is speculative; the illustrative diagram must be visibly marked as not data; the Kurland and Diehr methods references are unverified. || sharpen: Use a real published example where a survivors-only average and an analysis including deaths diverge, if one can be verified; otherwise cut the speculative explanation and keep the two checks. Schedule apart from C20-11.
- [   ] C20-20 total 44.0 | public | Ask this | public_explanation | risk medium | Told you have stage 3 kidney disease at 85? :: Many older people are told they have stage 3 chronic kidney disease after a routine blood test; in a large US study of people with this level of kidney function, those aged 85 and over were more likely to die of other causes than to need dialysis at every level studied, so asking whether the result is stable, what the urine test shows and which medicines need a different dose is more useful than fearing dialysis. || accuracy: O'Hare compared all-cause death with end-stage kidney disease, so 'more likely to die of other causes' misstates it; the cohort was US veterans, mostly men, 2007; the study covered stages 3 to 5, so the stage 3 statement needs the stage-specific figures. || sharpen: Phrase the finding as 'more likely to die than to reach kidney failure needing dialysis', date and place the study, and put the three questions before the statistic.
- [   ] C20-27 total 42.5 | public | Weighing it up | decision_support | risk low | When the evidence stops before your age :: When a treatment was studied only in younger people, the lack of evidence at older ages is a gap, which is different from evidence that it fails; the decision then rests on how the treatment works, its known risks at older ages, how long any benefit takes and what the person wants. || accuracy: Needs one specific, sourced example of a risk that rises with age; Path B must not read as encouragement to try untested treatments. || sharpen: Give one real treatment where the evidence stops at a stated age, and keep it as the public counterpart of C20-26 rather than running C20-29 as well.
- [   ] C20-01 total 42.0 | both | Reading the evidence | evidence_interpretation | risk low | The age of the trial and the age of the patients :: People in treatment trials have often been much younger than the people who have the condition, so before applying a trial result to an older person, look at the ages of the people who were studied. || accuracy: Hutchins data are SWOG trials from 1993 to 1996 and must be dated in the post; the claim that the gap persists rests on Ludmir 2019, which is unverified; the 63% comparator is the share of US cancer patients aged 65 or over, not people with cancer worldwide. || sharpen: Pair the 1990s figure with a verified recent analysis so the post shows whether the gap persists, and end on the one-line baseline-table check; choose this or C20-05, not both plus C20-07.
- [   ] C20-05 total 41.5 | practitioners | Who gets left out | evidence_interpretation | risk low | Excluded without an age limit :: Trials often shut out older people without an upper age limit, through criteria such as other illnesses, several medicines, reduced kidney function, cognitive impairment, living in a care home or being unable to attend visits, so reading the whole exclusion list shows who a result can apply to. || accuracy: Cherubini's two categories overlap (a trial can have both an age limit and poorly justified criteria), so 'more common than upper age limits' should be two separate percentages; the checklist items 'living in a care home' and 'unable to attend visits' must appear in the cited reviews or be dropped; the data are 2008 registrations. || sharpen: Anchor it in one named trial's exclusion list with the Cherubini percentages as context, and run it instead of, not alongside, C20-01 and C20-07.
- [   ] C20-29 total 41.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Three kinds of 'we don't know' :: Clinical uncertainty comes in different kinds, such as known odds of several outcomes, missing evidence for people like this patient, and several conditions interacting unpredictably, and naming which one applies helps an older person and their family understand what, if anything, could reduce it. || accuracy: Han 2011 names probability, ambiguity and complexity as sources of uncertainty; that each 'calls for a different response' is judgement, and evidence on how best to communicate uncertainty is limited. || sharpen: Give one sentence a clinician could say for each kind, and keep it for practitioners so it does not overlap C20-27.
- [   ] C20-12 total 40.5 | public | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk low | Age tells you less the older people get :: Differences in health between people of the same age tend to widen in later life, so a person's age is a weaker guide to their health, and to what a treatment will do for them, at 85 than it is in middle age. || accuracy: Nelson and Dannefer's 65% refers to dispersion across many kinds of variables, not health alone; at the oldest ages selective survival can narrow variation, so the flat 'people differ more in health at 85 than at 50' needs hedging; the step to 'what a treatment will do' is an inference, not a finding. || sharpen: Build on Lowsky's measured spread within the 85-and-over group, drop the older heterogeneity tally, and keep clear of C20-10's self-rated-health point.
- [   ] C20-23 total 40.5 | practitioners | Teaching round | evidence_interpretation | risk medium | Others rate older patients' lives lower than the patients do :: In studies of older outpatients and of hospital patients aged 80 and over, doctors and family surrogates judged the patients' quality of life or health values lower than the patients themselves did, so a treatment decision that brings in quality of life should start by asking the person. || accuracy: Both studies are US and from the 1990s; Tsevat measured time trade-off health values, not quality-of-life ratings, so 'rated lives lower' is loose for that study; generalising that proxy estimates 'tend to run low' needs a more recent source. || sharpen: Find a recent replication or review of proxy versus self ratings in older people, describe the time trade-off result literally, and close on how to ask the person; keep clear of C20-10.
- [   ] C20-18 total 39.5 | public | What gets counted | public_explanation | risk low | Healthy ageing can include illness :: The World Health Organization defines healthy ageing by what a person is able to do and be, which depends on their surroundings as well as their body, so a person with long-term conditions can still be ageing healthily, and changes to their surroundings can add to what they are able to do. || accuracy: Strawbridge's 50.3% is self-rated successful ageing, not self-rated health, and should not be conflated with C20-10; the WHO definition of healthy ageing must be quoted precisely from WHO 2015 or Beard 2016. || sharpen: Make it practical: turn the environment half of the WHO definition into three changes a family or council can make, and schedule well apart from C20-10.
- [   ] C20-15 total 39.0 | both | What gets counted | criticism | risk medium | What nobody counts in care homes :: England has no routine national minimum data set on care home residents, so their health, function and quality of life are largely missing from routine statistics; a national study has now built and piloted a prototype. || accuracy: England now collects some client-level adult social care data (from 2023) and Capacity Tracker data, so 'largely missing from routine statistics' and 'as they are for other groups of NHS patients' need careful wording; every item on the counted/not counted card needs individual verification; the DACHA pilot covered 727 residents. || sharpen: Lead with one verified concrete gap from DACHA (for example no routine national record of residents' function or quality of life) rather than a broad list.
- [   ] C20-07 total 38.5 | practitioners | Reading the evidence | teaching | risk low | Read the first box of the flow diagram :: A trial's flow diagram reports how many people were assessed for eligibility and why they were not included, which tells a reader how selected the participants were, often the main limit on applying a result to older people. || accuracy: Gross 2002 describes trials from 1999 to 2000, before the CONSORT flow diagram was routine, so the 52% figure should not be presented as current practice; the 'assessed for eligibility' number often counts only pre-screened people, so the box can understate selection; the 68-per-enrolled figure needs checking. || sharpen: Use one recent trial in older people with a verified screened-to-randomised ratio and ask one question of it, instead of old reporting statistics.
- [   ] C20-21 total 38.5 | public | Who gets left out | public_explanation | risk medium | You can volunteer for health research :: Older people are under-represented in research that concerns them, and in the UK anyone can register to be contacted about studies, through SHARE in Scotland, Join Dementia Research across the UK and the NIHR's Be Part of Research service. || accuracy: Whether Be Part of Research registration covers Scotland, and SHARE's current eligibility, need confirming from opened sources; registering does not change trial exclusion criteria, so the post should not imply it fixes under-representation. || sharpen: Merge with C20-13, be exact about which register covers which nation, and keep the claim to 'being asked', not 'being included'.
- [   ] C20-09 total 36.5 | practitioners | Who gets left out | teaching | risk low | Who pays for taking part :: Taking part in a study costs participants travel, time and often a companion's time, and designs that reduce these costs, such as home or remote visits, arranged transport and flexible appointments, help older people join and stay. || accuracy: The premise that poor recruitment is 'often put down to reluctance' is asserted without a source; Forsat's percentages are shares of studies reporting a barrier, not shares of people; several checklist items (companion costs, large print, hearing support) are not in the review. || sharpen: Say that percentages refer to studies, drop unsupported checklist items, and consider folding the transport and flexible-team finding into C20-05 since both rest on Forsat 2020.
- [   ] C20-13 total 36.5 | public | Ask this | decision_support | risk medium | Ask how you will hear what the study found :: If you are asked to take part in a research study, you can ask at the start how and when you will be told what it found, and UK research regulators expect researchers to tell participants the findings. || accuracy: The HRA 'Make it Public' expectation must be quoted from an opened document (official pages may not open here); 'many trials don't share results with participants' needs a verified source. || sharpen: Merge into C20-21 as one public post on taking part in research, with 'How will I hear what it found?' as the closing question.
- [   ] C20-04 total 35.0 | practitioners | Teaching round | teaching | risk medium | Is the change bigger than the test's variation? :: Repeated measurements such as memory test scores and eGFR vary from test to test when nothing has changed, so a small change should be compared with the test's known variation and read with the history and daily function before it changes a diagnosis or a plan. || accuracy: Hensel's 2 to 4 point reliable-change range applies to cognitively normal people over 75 retested at 18-month intervals at 90% confidence and does not transfer to people with dementia or other intervals; KDIGO 2024 may not keep the 2012 'certain drop' wording; the MMSE and family-account point partly repeats E015. || sharpen: Pick one measurement, probably eGFR in older people, give one verified threshold with its conditions, and drop the second half-explained rule.
- [   ] C20-25 total 33.5 | both | Who gets left out | criticism | risk medium | The words used about an ageing population :: In a US experiment, short messages that presented ageing in terms of older people's contributions, accumulated experience, or how age prejudice works reduced measured age bias in readers, while a plain message about ageing did not, so the words chosen to describe an ageing population can change attitudes. || accuracy: A single-session change on an implicit association test does not show changed attitudes or behaviour; the claim that catastrophe framing is common in media needs a source or must go; banned phrases cannot be shown, which weakens the visual. || sharpen: Recast around one concrete rewrite of a sentence type used in service reports or bids, with the experiment as modest support, or drop.
